What the Morning Gives
Created on November 26th, 2026
I went out early,
before the kettle, before the news,
while the fog still lay in the low field
like something the night forgot to take with it.

Between two fence posts
the spider had been working in the dark.
Every thread held its row of dew,
and when the sun came over the hill
it went into each small drop
and came out again as light.

I watched one bead grow heavy,
tremble, and let go.
It fell onto a maple leaf below,
a red one, the red of plums,
already loosened from its branch,
and the wind, which had been waiting for exactly this,
lifted the leaf and the drop together
and carried them down the slope toward the creek.

So I followed.
What else was I going to do
with a morning like that?

The creek was talking to itself
the way it always does,
low and patient, over the stones.
The maples along its banks were burning,
scarlet and amber and gold,
letting go of their leaves one at a time
as if each one were a small gift
they had been saving all year.
My red leaf rode among them,
turning slowly,
the dewdrop still shining at its center.

A heron stood in the shallows,
gray as the fog that had lifted,
so still I almost walked past.
Then, overhead, the geese,
a long unsteady V of them,
calling to one another,
heading south, heading home,
and the heron did not even look up.
It already knew where it belonged.

Where the creek bends near the road,
a child was crouched at the water's edge,
her mother waiting a few steps back.
She was watching the leaves go by
the way you'd watch a parade,
and when my red one came floating past
she reached for it and missed
and laughed anyway.

That was when the butterfly came,
a monarch, late and a little tattered,
going the same way the geese had gone,
south, toward some warm country
it had never seen
and somehow already knew.
It dropped down to rest
on a stalk of purple aster
right beside her hand.

She went completely still.
Her whole face opened like a door.
She did not know its name.
She did not need to.
She only breathed, Oh,
which is, I think,
the oldest prayer there is.

Then it lifted, and was gone,
and she turned and looked at me,
a stranger on the bank,
to make sure I had seen it too.
I had.
I nodded.
That was all,
and it was everything.

By evening I came back the way I had come.
The fog was rising again from the low field.
The geese were somewhere far down the sky.
And the web was still there between the fence posts,
dry now, nearly invisible,
until the last light caught it
and every thread turned gold.

So this is my thanks,
not for anything I earned,
but for the web that let the drop go,
and the leaf that carried it,
and the creek that kept it moving,
and the heron, and the geese,
and the child, and the wings,
everything traveling, everything held,
everything handing the day to the next thing,
the way it was handed to me.

And what else is gratitude, after all,
but this:
to follow where the morning leads,
to stand still in the wet grass,
to say yes, and yes again,
and thank you.

The X in Christ's Mass
Created on November 25th, 2026
Beneath the green boughs of the tree shining bright,
I pondered a puzzle one still Advent night:
Why some folks write "X-mas" on banners and signs,
As though they'd cross Christ out of Christmas's lines.

And then I recalled what the old scholars knew:
That X is the Greek letter Chi—and it's true!
It starts Christ's own name in the Gospels' first Greek,
So X still means Christ, if it's Christ that you seek.

And "mas" is the Mass, where the faithful still kneel,
Where candles are glowing and hearts start to heal;
So X-mas, read rightly, as you now can see,
Spells Christ at the Mass, just as Christmas should be.

But let me be honest and say what I think:
Most X-mas today is just saving some ink.
It's not scholars of Greek with a reverent pen,
Just hurrying shoppers, cut-corners again.

They'll spend hours wrapping and stringing the lights,
And baking and shopping through long, busy nights,
Yet can't spare five letters, a second or so,
To honor the Name of the Babe long ago.

Now there is a fellow of rockets and cars,
Who's building great ships to go sailing to Mars;
He took a blue bird and renamed it with X,
And ponders what marvel he'll launch to it next.

So here's a suggestion, all friendly and kind,
For one with such rockets and stars on his mind:
Your X is half there, so just add on the Mass.
Come kneel where the hymns ring from organ and brass!

For no rocket climbs to the height of the Star
That guided the Wise Men who traveled so far,
And no feed or app, though it circles the earth,
Can share news as good as our dear Savior's birth.

So write it in full; we've no need of the X,
Except for that fellow, whose Mass may come next!
The Christ in the Christmas, the Child in the hay,
Is the reason we gather this glad holy day.
Spin
Created on November 23rd, 2026
The spider spins her silken thread,
The weaver spins the wool to twine,
The child's red top spins on its head
Until it wobbles out of line.

The DJ spins a vinyl track,
The bowler spins the ball to bite,
The washer spins my laundry stack,
The planet spins from day to night.

My grandpa spins a fishing tale
That grows a foot each time it's told;
The pilot fights a spin, gone pale,
As engines sputter, stall, and fold.

The press aide spins the morning news
Until the scandal sounds like praise;
At spin class, sweating in my shoes,
I pedal nowhere in a daze.

Dad takes the Chevy for a spin,
An electron spins, though not around;
A hit show spawns a spin-off twin,
A coin spins ringing on the ground.

So when you say "spin," which one is meant:
The thread, the lie, the plane's last fall?
One word, a hundred ways it's bent,
And now my head spins most of all.

The Famous Child
Created on November 18th, 2026
At every party they ask for her by name:
e to the i pi, plus one, equals zero,
five old strangers holding hands in a single line.
Pi came in from the circles, still dizzy.
E arrived from the banks and the bacteria, always growing.
I, the one they called imaginary, stood at the door
waiting for someone to admit she was real.
One brought the counting. Zero brought the quiet.
Together they balance, and the guests applaud,
and someone always whispers the word “beautiful.”
But nobody invites her father, Euler’s formula,
e to the i theta, who never sits still,
who spins sines and cosines the way a potter spins clay.
He is down in the wall sockets right now,
humming sixty times a second.
He is inside your earbuds, sorting the drums from the voice,
carrying this poem through the air to your phone.
He doesn’t mind the spotlight going to his daughter.
He only stopped once, at the angle of pi,
long enough to make her, and then kept turning.

Mathematical Equations
Please Read Rules Before Submitting. Any poems that are not complicit will not be considered for judging. The prompt here is simple: Write a poem about your favorite mathematical equation, and personify it! Tell the story of its variables, its context, etc.

Rules:
-20 line maximum
-No prewrites/AI
-No rhyming
-NO E=MC^2 or Pythagorean Theorem (be more creative!)

Cousin Schnoz
Created on November 14th, 2026
The Fonz had a cousin they all called the Schnoz,
a nose with a fella attached, just because.
It entered each doorway a full step ahead,
like the prow of a tugboat, the regulars said.

At Arnold’s one evening, the jukebox went dead;
the Fonz gave it whacks on the side of its head.
He snapped both his fingers and murmured, “Aaaay!”
But not even Fonzie could make that thing play.

The Schnoz, who’d been dozing on top of the keys,
woke up with a snort and a thunderous sneeze,
and plain as the nose on his face, you could see
it knocked the old needle right onto B-3.

“Ha-cha-cha!” he rasped as the gang crowded near
to honk and to sniffle and snort in his ear.
He shooed off the bunch with a flap of his hat:
“Everybody wants to get into the act!”

Then Fonzie leaned cool on the jukebox and grinned,
gave a thumbs-up to genius that ran on the wind.
The Schnoz tipped his hat, stepped out under a star:
“Goodnight, Mrs. Calabash, wherever you are.”

Moon Jelly at Ebb Tide
Created on November 13th, 2026
The ebb had drawn the bay out of itself.
Boats leaned in the mud, their ropes slack,
and under the pier the last of the day
lay in thin brass on the pools.

There I found it: a moon jelly,
no bigger than a dinner plate,
its four dim rings showing through
as through a window breathed on.

I slid a split plank underneath.
Its bell tightened once—not a hand,
not a mouth, but the whole soft body
trying the element it had lost.

The sun dropped behind the fish sheds.
Night came up the beach without a sound
and took the colour from the barnacles,
the orange weed, my red sleeve.

I carried it down the shining channel.
Cold entered my boots. The plank
grew weightless as the water deepened,
and the jelly slid from it.

For one pulse it hung beneath me,
then another—so faint
I might have given it that second
out of my own wanting.

Behind me, the flow began.
Not day returning, not a pardon:
the sea put its cold mouth
to every footprint I had left.
The Rooms We Leave
Created on November 13th, 2026
The boxes came on Saturday
We lined them by the wall
Dad took the pictures down at last
And left the shadows tall
Her Sunday dress was in the chest
Her scarf still smelled of rain
My sister pressed it to her face
Then folded it again

The rooms we leave still hold her voice
The floors still know her tread
We pack the plates, we wrap the frames
We fold the quilt upon her bed
No ribbon tied around a box
Can hold the years we've known
We take the love she left behind
And leave the house alone

I found her gloves beneath the sink
Still stained with garden clay
The roses she had tied in spring
Kept climbing day by day
My brother read her recipe
With flour on his sleeve
We laughed until the laughter broke
And nobody could leave

The rooms we leave still hold her voice
The floors still know her tread
We pack the plates, we wrap the frames
We fold the quilt upon her bed
No ribbon tied around a box
Can hold the years we've known
We take the love she left behind
And leave the house alone

At dusk we sat around her chair
And held her wedding band
No one could bear to put it down
We passed it hand to hand
Then quietly Dad sang her song
His voice began to shake
And through the window roses swayed
Like she was still awake

The rooms we leave still hold her voice
The floors still know her tread
We pack the plates, we wrap the frames
We fold the quilt upon her bed
No ribbon tied around a box
Can hold the years we've known
We take the love she left behind
And leave the house alone

I turned the key, we stood outside
No words could make it right
Her scarf was warm around my neck
Like her, it held me tight
Author's Notes: Most of the time my poetry comes from the mood I am in that day. Today was Nostalgia day. The image is my creation.

The Matchstick and the Snowflake (An Exercise in Thermal Incompatibility)
Created on November 11th, 2026
A Matchstick sat upon a shelf, a fellow rather fond of friction,
Who considered spontaneous combustion not a danger but a personal conviction.

A Snowflake floated through the room, all delicate, white and airy,
And the Matchstick thought her quite divine, though chemically somewhat contrary.

"Good evening," said the Matchstick, "I am pleased to make your acquaintance,
Though I fear my natural disposition may require a certain degree of maintenance."

The Snowflake blushed—or would have blushed, had blushing been within her constitution,
And replied, "Your warmth is most attractive, but presents a problem of dissolution."

"I'd gladly give my heart to you," he cried, "and set the world aglow!"
"That's very kind," the Snowflake said, "but I'd prefer you didn't, though."

They spoke of love and destiny, of thermodynamics and fate,
And whether mutual admiration could survive a change of state.

And so they parted, sweet and sad, their passion left unmet,
For love may conquer many things,

But someone always ends up wet.
Author's Notes: Every now and then I write what I call a "mood" poem. Guess what mood I am in.
No Words of Mine
Created on November 9th, 2026
You stayed tonight to ease the pain
But I can feel what still remains
Don't tell me things you don't believe
Just hold me close until you leave
And if I ask you why don't explain

No words of mine will ever make you stay
I can't bring back the love that slipped away
I know this borrowed night is all you'll give
I'll take it, though I don't know how I'll live
You're here with me, but miles away
No words of mine will ever make you stay

I'll close my eyes and just pretend
That holding you won't have to end
When morning comes, I'll let you go
There's nothing left to say, we both know
But part of me won't let go

No words of mine will ever make you stay
I can't bring back the love that slipped away
I know this borrowed night is all you'll give
I'll take it, though I don't know how I'll live
You're here with me, but miles away
No words of mine will ever make you stay

Maybe love doesn't always need a reason
Maybe this was all the time we were given
And morning is coming in to take its due
I'll let you go the way that mornings do
No words of mine will ever make you stay

I can't bring back the love that slipped away
I know that this borrowed night is all you'll give
I'll take it though I don't know how I'll live
You're here with me but miles away
No words of mine will ever make you stay

Our borrowed night has turned to day
I watch you turn and walk away
There's nothing left for me to say
No words of mine will ever make you stay
Author's Notes: Contest - Write a song about unrequited love with strong and highly emotional lyrics

Gravity Is Generous
Created on November 8th, 2026
First, nothing in the morning blue,
then, soft as breath, a drifting white,
a weightless someone, small and new,
with falling hidden in its height.

We rise as saplings in a gale,
all knees and nerve and stubborn green,
and call each gust a foe turned frail
while roots drink what once fell unseen.

Then thaw: the river, brown and loud,
carves restless names in banks of clay,
its swollen current, fierce and proud,
still blind to what it bears away.

Then summer: oak in heavy green,
sheltering shoots that seek the glow,
storing each year we’ve ever been
in rings of flood and drought below.

Then autumn takes the borrowed green
and lets the glow fall on each shoot;
the boughs that summer kept unseen
stand bare above the buried root.

We settle, leaf by leaf, below.
Our fallen leaves feed roots anew;
the shoots exhale; the breezes blow
a thread of white through morning blue.
Author's Notes: Image is my creation using photoshop
Gary Pruitt, Executive Chef
Created on November 5th, 2026
ACT ONE

Gary Pruitt, fifty-two, was sweet but not too bright,
And fell for sweet Denise on their first date, that very night.
She asked him what he did for work. He choked on his chardonnay
And blurted, “I’m a chef!” in the strangled, desperate way
Of a man whose finest kitchen feat, in half a century of trying,
Was microwaving leftovers and calling it “fine dining.”
Six months went by. Denise announced, “My folks are driving through.
My mother’s a retired chef. She trained at Cordon Bleu.”
Poor Gary smiled and nodded, and his left eye gave a twitch.
He said, “I’ll cook the dinner, dear,” and felt his stomach pitch.
He bought a toque, a copper pan, a book on carving knives,
And an apron, “Kiss the Chef,” to complete his web of lies.

ACT TWO

Sunday came. The in-laws sat. Stan cracked his knuckles, stern.
Dolores raised an eyebrow. “Tell me, Gary, where’d you learn?”
“Paris!” Gary lied, then added, “Also Lyon. And Spain.”
(He’d never left Ohio, but had watched Ratatouille on a plane.)
The soufflé rose in glory, then collapsed with a sigh,
Like Gary’s hope, and Gary’s pride, and Gary’s alibi.
He lit the shrimp flambé; the flames climbed up the drapes.
Stan doused them with the merlot. (Gary mourned the grapes.)
Dolores tasted the béarnaise. She set her spoon down slow.
“Young man, this tastes like a wet sock that’s been somewhere it shouldn’t go.”
Stan said, “Let’s see that knife work, son.” Gary seized a knife,
Sliced off a thumb-tip, shrieked, and pleaded, “Spare my life!”
The dog then stole the roast whole, and fled with it down the hall,
And Gary, bleeding, half in wine, sat down and began to bawl.
“I’m no chef!” he sobbed. “I’m a claims adjuster, God help me, it’s true!
I lied on our first date, and every date, and now I’m through!”

ACT THREE

The silence in the room grew thick as Gary’s gravy,
Till Stan said, “Son, I lasted nine days as a Marine, then a hernia saved me.”
Dolores snorted merlot out her nose and wiped her chin.
“My Cordon Bleu was one weekend class at a Holiday Inn.”
Denise then took his bandaged hand and said, “You silly man,
I knew by date two. Your fridge held mustard, beer, and one sad can.
I stayed because you’re kind, and funny, and that apron’s rather hot.
It says ‘Kiss the Chef.’ So come here, you fraud, and give it a shot.”
They ordered Chinese takeout. They were wed by early spring,
And Gary hasn’t cooked since then, not one single thing.
The moral? Lie about your height, your hair, your pay, your car,
But never claim you’re a chef, my friend. That’s a bridge too far.
Author's Notes: Image is my original made with Photoshop
Never Built for Clouds
Created on October 30th, 2026
They cut the strings the year the soldiers came,
Hung the hollow frame above the hall.
Said a people with no music left to play
Would soon forget they were people at all.

But a boy kept the tuning in his fingertips,
Played the air above the empty wood,
And every soul who’d lost the name that year
Heard it whole, and understood

It was never built for clouds.
It was built for carrying all the weight
A voice can’t hold alone,
And the names it’s scared to sing.

So let it ring, let it ring, not for the angels.
Let it ring for the ones who stay,
For the broken, for the grieving, for the ones still standing
At the threshold, unafraid.

It’s a door with strings across it,
Every hand that touches it walks through.
Let it ring, let it ring.
It was never heaven’s, it was always for me and you.

Across the sea, a griot tunes his kora
Under a baobab by the edge of town,
Sings a thousand years of father’s father’s names
So not a one of them can ever be lost or drowned.

The thumb keeps the rhythm, fingers tell the story:
Weddings, wars, and who still owes a cow.
His heart talks back, it teases, it remembers,
Every praise song is a promise: I’m here now.

It was never built for clouds.
It was built for carrying all the weight
A voice can’t hold alone,
And the names it’s scared to sing.

So let it ring, let it ring, not for the angels.
Let it ring for the ones who stay,
For the storyteller, the mourner, the stubborn ones
Still singing at the threshold, unafraid.

It’s a door with strings across it,
Every hand that touches it walks through.
Let it ring, let it ring.
It was never heaven’s, it was always for me and you.

Now a woman wheels her harp down a hospice hallway,
Room 12, and an old man’s breath like paper-thin.
Once a soldier, once a father, and now afraid,
Restless as a king with the dark closing in.

She doesn’t play a hymn, she doesn’t play a lullaby,
Just a slow, uneven river of a tune,
And his hands stop shaking on the blanket
Like David never left the room.

But a string will snap sometimes, mid-phrase, mid-breath,
A crack like a sentence left undone,
And the silence that follows is part of the song,
Not a failure, not a fault,
Not the end of the one.

Because a harp that only ever soothes
Would be a liar at the door.
Every note you love is already leaving.
That’s what the strings are for:
To make the leaving beautiful,
To teach the leaving how to sing, to soar.

So let it ring, let it ring, not for the angels.
Let it ring for the ones who stay,
For the boy with the empty frame,
The griot, the dying, and the ones who sit and wait.

It’s a door with strings across it,
Every hand that touches it walks through.
Let it ring, let it ring.
It was never heaven’s, it was always for me and you.

The soldier’s boy, the griot’s thumb,
The hand beside the bed,
One harp, a thousand doors,
And every one of them said:
Stay a little longer.
Author's Notes: The Harp has more history than you might think. I wrote and produced this initially as a song. No AI was used during the writing process, The words are mine, but the vocals are AI generated, I can't sing. This was produced in Fender Studio Pro. The image used is my creation with the help of Photoshop and other image editing software.

After You, No, After You
Created on October 30th, 2026
Four small trick-or-treaters stood
At the edge of a crooked wood,
Where a gravel drive climbed up the hill
To a house lit up and eerily still.

The windows glowed like candy corn,
The sky above looked cold and torn,
And everyone was thinking, “Gee,
That’s quite a walk for little me.”

“You go first,” the witch declared.
“Not me,” a pumpkin said, scared.
“Well, I’m not going!” “Nor am I!”
“You go on up.” “No, you! Not I!”

Now look at the pumpkins, if you please:
Their grins are turned back toward the trees,
Their costumes spun the wrong way round,
All pointing back to safer ground.

So there they stand as the daylight fades,
Four small ghouls in their masquerades,
Still insisting, “After you!”
While the old house sighs, “Well, someone do.”

Trick or Tree-eat
Created on October 30th, 2026
The fog rolls in on silent feet,
And whispers, “Trick or Tree-eat!”
The old tree bends with a crooked grin,
A hollow mouth where the dark pours in.

Its mossy lips are stretched out wide,
With shadows waiting deep inside.
Its gnarled arm reaches, bent and low,
Like a hand that begs, “Come close. Hello.”

Behind it, ghostly giants stand,
Half-lost in mist, a silent band.
They lean together, hushed and still,
As if they’ve gathered for the kill.

The broken fence posts, thin and gray,
Look like old bones where wanderers lay.
No candy here, no porch light’s gleam,
Just roots that twist like a waking dream.

So if you pass this haunted place
And glimpse that wide and hungry face,
Don’t linger long, don’t stop to greet:
Or you’ll be the treat in Trick or Tree-eat!
I Beg Your Pardon
Created on October 29th, 2026
I beg your pardon, Mr. President,
Did I hear my name today?
Yes, they call me Drumstick, sir,
And I’m saved, I’m glad to say!

I beg your pardon, brother birds,
For being the lucky one,
While you’re headed for the oven
To be roasted, stuffed and done.

So thank you for the pardon, sir,
And pardon me, I pray,
If I’m not on your table
On this Thanksgiving Day!
Pom-Era-Rain-ians
Created on October 29th, 2026
They say it’s raining cats and dogs,
But look what’s on the couch:
Seven fluffy raindrops fell
And landed in a slouch!

Two black clouds, so dark and sleek,
Roll in with stormy flair,
While three white flurries, soft as snow,
Drift down without a care.

A white-and-tan one, sunset-bright,
Peeks out between the showers,
And one mottled, misty pup
Adds mystery to the hours.

All seven stare right at the lens,
Each face a little storm,
But one white puff tilts up her head
And asks, “Is this the norm?”

Pom-era-rain-ians, every one,
A forecast I adore:
Seven puffs of pouring joy,
And I would take some more!
All Aboard the GHOUL BUS
Created on October 22nd, 2026
Green-skinned rider in a witch’s hat waves hello,
Hairy wolf and caped vampire ride along for the show.
Orange wheels on the bus go round and round,
Under a ghost on the roof, who haunts without a sound.
Leaves swirl past the porch like ghosts from a tomb,

Boarding is open, there’s plenty of room!
Up the steps and climb aboard,
Shrieking with laughter, the whole haunted horde!

Hollow Road, October 31st
Created on October 21st, 2026
The post leans where the gravel gives out,
gray as a tooth, one crow on top of it,
and I stand a few feet off, hands loose,
thinking of my grandmother’s kitchen,
her bird on a fence means somebody’s missing you,
all that sweet old-country talk
I used to smile at over cornbread.

Woodsmoke and wet leaves. No lamp for a mile.
The moon does what it wants with the sign.
In daylight, HALLOWEEN is paint,
a joke somebody’s cousin made.
Tonight the letters have gone thin and wrong,
the L’s stretched too tall,
the whole word breathing a faint green
like something held under creek water.

The arrow points north.
Past the ridge, past the last porch light,
where the road turns to a rumor,
and the crow has turned its head that way too,
as if it’s been listening to something up there
since before I came.

Kaaah.
The hollow takes it and hands it back thinner.
Kaaah.
My breath hangs white in front of me.
I reach in my coat for a lighter, a key, anything,
and find only the cold lining.
Kaaah.

Now the left. Any bird would go left now.
That’s how it was told. That’s the whole of the telling.

The crow stays. It folds itself smaller on the post,
one black eye holding the moon,
and I hear myself say it anyway,
low, the way you’d speak to a dog you don’t know:
carry it kind and far.

Nothing moves. Even the leaves wait.
I step back once, then again,
and the arrow stays pointed at the dark,
and the bird stays pointed at me.
Author's Notes: Prompt #3 Crow on a fence post - WC 272 - Image created by me.
Supper for One
Created on October 20th, 2026
The tap runs brackish for a minute,
the color of weak tea, then clears.
I boil a turnip, since it was there,
the one thing left in the drawer
that wasn’t already soft.

The milk has begun to curdle
in a glass I forgot by the sink.
Out back the garden has gone feral,
beans over the fence, a squash
the size of a sleeping dog.

I eat the turnip standing up.
It tastes like a coin held in the mouth.
Rain has come in through the window
I have meant to fix since spring
and stands in a line along the sill.

But then I remember the cabinet door,
the hinge that has hung loose for years,
and how tonight I tightened it.

I open it and close it twice.
Inside, a thimble, and in the thimble
your molar, which the dentist said was mine to keep.
Glossary of Longing
Created on September 25th, 2026
They handed me a word and said: describe it.
Ineffable — impossible to say —
so I built a tongue that isn't language,
a grammar made of static and delay.

I called it hushglow, called it velverune,
the shiver-hymn that lives beneath your name.
I coined thrumlight for the way you enter
a room and every surface learns to flame.

You are not metaphor. You resist translation.
I have tried simile — you slip the noose.
So I invented susurral, a word
for breath against a collarbone, unloosed.

Ineffable: the paradox of trying —
to pin the wordless wriggling thing with words,
to net a comet, bottle up a season,
teach silence how to sound like it's been heard.

I made lumenous for your particular dark,
the kind that isn't absence but a keeping.
I made quiverspan for the exact
duration of a held breath, almost weeping.

Every language failed you first. I'm just
the latest fool to reach and come back empty —
so I forge new coin from old desire,
mint achelume for wanting, soft, in plenty.

Call this cheating. Call it makeshift scripture.
I call it honest: if the word's not there,
I'll build one from the wreckage of the trying —
unfathomere, for how you fill a room like air.

Here's my ledger of impossible words,
my glossary of longing, my forged decree —
hushglow, velverune, thrumlight, susurral,
lumenous, quiverspan, achelume, unfathomere —

none of them mean anything but you,
and that, at least, is perfectly clear.
The dictionary has no page for this.
So I wrote my own, in disappearing ink —

a language built to fail, and fail, and fail
more beautifully with every new exhale,
better than finding words already made,
already spent, already going stale.

Take this glossary. It's incomplete
on purpose — every ineffable thing
leaves room for one more made-up word,
one more impossible attempt to sing.

My Friend in Rhyme
Created on September 25th, 2026
October brought him, tapping at my door,
A stack of books, a wooden raven he bore,
He set it on my shelf, and there it's perched since then,
"For luck," he said, "or maybe just for when."

He reads my drafts with weary, sunken eyes,
He circles every rhyme too worn, too wise,
"Your meter falters here — it's flat, it's sore,"
Then sighs and shakes his head, and frowns once more.

He caught me Googling once, some app to compose,
He blanched a shade whiter — something in him froze,
"A machine writes your heart? That's the coldest of jokes,
I've outdueled worse rivals than silicon folks."

I asked for his birth year — he coughed and turned pale,
Said "eighteen-oh-nine," then went thin as a veil,
I reached out to touch him — my hand passed right through,
"Ah," he said, "right. I forgot to tell you."

Sometimes he speaks of someone, a girl named Lenore,
Lost long ago to fever, back in Baltimore,
His voice drops to a hush like a bell tolling slow,
And something in the floorboards seems to beat, soft and low.

So he's been dead two centuries, give or take a few,
Still critiquing my meter as dead poets do,
A raven, a Lenore, a heartbeat, a door —
He'll haunt my bad couplets forever and more.

The Shoe That Grew a House
Created on September 23rd, 2026
An old leather shoe went wandering far,
Over hilltops and meadows and streams,
With no one to wear it and nowhere to be,
Just following the sun and its dreams.

It splashed through the marsh and it clomped down the lane,
It tiptoed past owls in the trees,
It hopped over puddles and dodged through the rain,
And it shook off the burrs and the bees.

Then one golden evening, as daylight grew thin,
It came to a moss-covered glade,
Where the forget-me-nots nodded, and buttercups grinned,
And it sat down in the softest of shade.

"This is it," sighed the shoe, "I have wandered enough,
And my toes have stopped wanting to roam.
So I'll stay in this spot, where the moss is so soft,
And I'll make it my very own home."

Then the rain gently fell and the sun shone through,
And the moss stitched a roof, soft and green,
A door and a window came peeking out too,
The cosiest cottage you've seen.

Last, a chimney of brick rose up to the sky,
And it breathed out a curl of warm air,
A sign for all wanderers passing by:
"There's a home, and there's room, and there's care."

Then along came a mouse in a jacket of gold,
Who knocked at the small arched door,
And the house said, "Come in, it is warm, and I'm told
That I've room for one traveler more."

So if ever you wander the woods and you see
A shoe with a chimney on top,
Remember it wandered as far as could be
Till it found the right place to plop.
Between Two Doors
Created on September 18th, 2026
I. Birth

They say the first sound is a scream —
mine was, wet and furious,
torn out of a woman
who was screaming too,
both of us doing the same violent work
from opposite sides of the same door.
Somebody laughed. Somebody wept.
I did not choose the room,
the light like a struck match,
the hands that weighed me like fruit
and called it love.
I arrived the way everyone arrives:
uninvited, undeniable —
a mouth that hadn't chosen its first word,
no history yet,
no name that meant anything
until they gave me one.

II. Choices

But then the days accumulate like teeth.
I learned to want things —
that was the first betrayal of sameness.
I said no when I meant yes and yes
when the word cost me something.
I loved the wrong mouths on purpose.
I stayed in rooms with the windows painted shut
because leaving felt like proof I didn't belong there.
I built a self out of small refusals:
the job not taken, the woman not married,
the country left, the god put down
like a coat that no longer fit the weather.
Every choice was a little death of who I might have been —
the other selves pile up behind me
like coats on a hook, unworn, unmourned,
and I am only ever the one
still standing in this skin,
still choosing, which is the same as still becoming.
No one else has my wreckage.
No one else has my particular hunger,
shaped exactly to the shape of what starved me.
This is the cruelty and the gift of the middle:
that nothing here was given —
I built it with my own two furious hands
out of what the world put in front of me
and what I refused to let it keep.

III. Death

And at the end, they will gather again,
the same weeping in a different key —
grief this time instead of joy, or maybe not
though the body doesn't know the difference,
salt is salt.
Someone will hold my hand
the way they held it when it was new,
small and curling,
except now it will not curl back.
I will leave the way I came:
without my consent, without my say,
the room narrowing to a single point of light
then closing like an eye that has seen enough.
They will remember the specific things —
not that I was born, not that I died,
everyone does that, it means nothing alone —
but the way I said their name,
the choices that made me unmistakably myself
and not some other man
who also screamed his way into the world
and also, someday, will scream his way out.

Between two doors that open the same way for everyone,
I did something only I could have done.
That is the whole of it.
That is enough.
Something Larger Than Your Name
Created on September 17th, 2026
I kneel at the tree line where light comes apart
in the maple leaves, each one holding
its own coin of gold before it lets go,
and press my palm against the ridged bark,
rough as a knuckle, warm where the sun
has been lying against it all morning.

Around me the whole canopy is spending itself —
red-rimmed leaves loosening from their stems
one at a time, unhurried, as if falling
were something practiced for years
before it was allowed to look this easy.
They drop through no wind I can name,
turning once, twice, a slow coin-flip
of red and gold, and land without a sound
in the grass already thick with yesterday's,
building a floor the color of embers
that have decided not to go out.

Then the ground answers before my ears do —
a weight moving through the undergrowth,
patient, deliberate, each step
pressed into the leaf-mold and held there
a moment before the next.
Antlers come first, parting the low light
into pieces the way a hand parts water,
and behind them a body the color of the bark
I've just been touching, steam lifting
off its back into the cold air, unraveling
the same way the leaves are unraveling,
as if the whole clearing were breathing
through a hundred small openings at once.

More come behind the first — necks bowed,
hooves finding the same worn path,
breath rising white from wide black nostrils
and hanging there before the branches take it.
One bull stops at the stream and drinks,
throat working, water darkening his chin,
the surface breaking into rings
that carry all the way to the bank
where I am kneeling, not breathing,
watching the rings reach the grass
and disappear into it, the way the leaves
keep disappearing into the ground,
the way the light keeps disappearing
into the animals' backs and coming out changed,
softer, the color of something remembered
rather than seen.

He lifts his head. Water drips
from his jaw in a string of small bright falls.
He crosses without hurrying,
without checking behind him,
as if the crossing cost him nothing,
as if the whole meadow had already
agreed to let him through.

Kneel where something larger than your name walks past.
Let the leaves land on your shoulders and stay there.
Do not stand until the herd is gone
and the light, too, has moved on without you.
The Congregation of the Bar
Created on September 17th, 2026
A rabbi, a priest, and a pastor one night
Walked into a bar under dim amber light,
And perched on the stools, already at ease,
Sat a rat, a chimp, a dolphin, and a parrot — swapping keys
To jokes so old the bartender just groaned,
But the animals roared like the punchline was owned.

The rabbi sat down and said, "Gentlemen, please —
Explain to me why beasts are laughing with such ease."
The rat merely grinned and adjusted his tie:
"We've heard every sermon you've ever preached — twice — and still don't know why."

The priest, mildly wounded, turned up his collar,
"We speak of forgiveness," he said, "not for scholars—"
The chimp interrupted with a bark and a hoot,
"Forgiveness is easy, when you're throwing no fruit!"

The pastor leaned in with his warmest church-smile,
"Faith isn't a laughing matter — not for a while."
But the dolphin just clicked, spinning circles with glee,
"Everything's funnier under the sea!"

Then the parrot, who'd silently soaked in each word,
Repeated the punchlines — reversed and absurd:
"A bar walks into a rabbi! A priest meets a joke!"
And the whole room dissolved into laughter and smoke.

The three holy men blinked, then started to chuckle,
Their solemn faces starting to buckle,
For somewhere between scripture and animal noise,
They remembered that laughter's the oldest of joys.

So if ever you wonder what heaven finds funny,
Ask a rat, a chimp, a dolphin — spare no money —
Or a parrot who's mastered the art of the tale:
The punchline's not sacred. It's just the wag of a tail.
A Wild Little Mix-Up
Created on September 16th, 2026
A wild little wasp with a watermelon hat
Attempted to waltz with a very wry cat.
“I wish you would whistle,” the wasp told her friend,
“And promise to misbehave right to the end.”

The cat gave a wink—then the W turned to M;
A mink took its place. What a fix for them!
It stole the last waffle and climbed up a chair,
Leaving a whisper of crumbs in the air.

“How weird!” cried the wasp. “There’s a wrinkle in things!
That mink has my waffle; the cat has my wings!”
Then wild became mild with one switch of a letter,
And everyone’s manners grew suddenly better.

They shared what was left, and the cat poured the tea.
“One letter,” she said, “makes a new sort of me.
But bring back the wild when we’ve finished this spread—
I’d rather go dancing than toddle  to bed!”

The Unsinkable Pugs
Created on September 6th, 2026
Upon the prow where once a girl
Spread arms against the salt-cold air,
And a boy behind her, steady, held
The whole wide ocean in his care—

Two pugs now stand, and she throws wide
Her stubby paws to greet the gale,
While he, pressed close against her side,
Keeping her safe against the rail.

"I'm flying!" barks the girl, no doubt,
Though no one hears above the foam—
The boy grunts back, a wheezy shout,
"I've got you, Rose. I'll bring you home."

But hush—remember what this steel
Once carried through the April dark:
The chandeliers, the waltz, the meal,
The band that played upon the ark.

Remember fifteen hundred names
The freezing water would not keep,
The lifeboats launched with half their frames,
The unsinkable, gone down to sleep.

The sea that night was black as coal,
The stars indifferent and high,
And every soul that lost its soul
Beneath a North Atlantic sky.

And yet—and yet—upon that rail,
Two pugs, oblivious and round,
Rehearse a love that cannot fail,
The only flying to be found.

She spreads her paws, a tiny queen
Of all the wind and all the spray;
He clings to her, a loyal thing,
To keep his darling from the gray.

No iceberg waits, no hull will tear,
No orchestra need play them down—
Just two small dogs with flattened hair,
The happiest fools in all the town.

So let the serious sea recall
Its grief, its grandeur, and its cost,
And let the pugs, above it all,
Reenact the love we almost lost.

For history keeps its heavy score,
But love, too, must have its turn:
Two pugs, one bow, one myth, one roar—
"I'm flying, Jack!" — and still we learn

That even on the edge of doom,
Where water waits and steel gives way,
A little love can fill the room,
And two small dogs can save the day.
Author's Notes: Image is my creation in support of the poem. This song is just something fun I added.

Sedge Pond, Early June
Created on September 6th, 2026
I walk down through grass silvered with dew,
before the sun has climbed high enough to dry it,
my ankles numb inside their own chill,
cattails leaning their brown heads
over water gone thick with sky.

Marsh wrens cut the air with small cries.
A bullfrog drops from a low root
into water that closes over him
without a sound.

I hold my hand out over the shallows,
one finger lifted, still as the sedge,
and wait while the sun climbs
and my shoulder aches
and the morning goes on without me,
minute by minute, not caring if I stay.

The dragonfly comes from nowhere,
blue as chicory, quick as a thought turning,
its double wings ticking like dry grass
in a wind only it feels.
Then it comes down.
Six legs, thin as sedge stems,
fold onto my finger.
Its body, a blue stalk between two wings,
its two dark seeds of eyes
turn to take in
the whole trembling pond, the whole sky,
and me, and do not lift away.

I do not breathe.
My arm turns into a dead branch, holding still,
not a boy's arm at all,
and the dragonfly rides it,
its wings ticking down to nothing,
staying.

You want the world to notice you—
so stop moving. Stop asking.
Empty your hand of everything
you meant to do with it.

The dragonfly lifts once, testing the air,
then settles again on the same finger,
its wings printing small circles of light
onto the skin of the pond.
Author's Notes: 67 years later and I still remember this as if it just happened minutes ago. There I stand at the edge of the pond I visited every day, standing at waters edge finger out waiting for that dragonfly to land on my finger. I did this for 5 days, then on the 6th day it happened.

You're tuned to the grooviest station in town
Turn up the hi-fi, kick off your go-go boots

I'm the swingingest feline on the street

I'm the queen of the scene, a cool cat on the mic

I'm crooning like a cat on the moon

The Emu's Bad Day
Created on August 24th, 2026
Out in the garden where the wattle burns gold,
An emu came strutting, cheeky and bold.
He sniffed at the boronia, sweet on the breeze,
Then flattened the mulla-mulla down to his knees.

"Bloody oath!" hollered Shirl, "would ya look at that bird,
He's mad as a cut snake" — and gee, she'd not erred.
He spun through the flowerbeds, wild and unchecked,
Not one sprig of wattle escaped with respect.

"That bird's got a roo loose in his top paddock, love,
Chasing his tail like there's no God above."

A Minnow-t to Spare
Created on August 21st, 2026
A bait shop sign hung crooked and bright:
"OUT TO LAUNCH," in paint gone thin and pale,
And just below it, dozing in the light,
A fisherman sat lost in some quiet tale.

I thought of every sleeper who's been shown
A mustache, penciled while they lay unknowing,
And felt that same old mischief, fully grown,
A little itch of trouble, softly growing.

Slumped in his chair, his hat pulled low and deep,
His breath gone slow beneath that weathered brow;
I drew a mustache while he lay asleep,
Then taped my little sign beneath him now.

"Be Back in a Minnow-t," it proudly read,
Below the dreamer, still as any stone;
While "Out to Launch" hung crooked overhead,
Two silly signs made mischief of their own.
Author's Notes: Image was created by my me specifically for this poem.
What I Called Him
Created on August 15th, 2026
Me and Tommy had a fight
about whose turn it was to write
the answer on the chalkboard first,
so I let him have my very worst.

"You're an ALAPAZEL, through and through!"
(I made that word up, but it's true.)
His face went red, he stomped his shoe,
and hollered back, "Well, YOU'RE one too!"

"BOONEYDONDLE!" I said next,
the ugliest word I know, in text,
he gasped so hard he dropped his chalk
and couldn't even find his walk.

"CHUCKLECHAZEL butt" I roared,
the meanest thing I could afford,
he called me something worse, I'm sure,
but I forgot it — that's for sure.

"DOODERBEEDERBOB!" I hollered last,
the biggest word from all my past,
he blinked, he paused, he had to grin—
"That one's too good. You win."
Author's Notes: Submitted for the contest - An UN-WORD Contest for your Writing Pleasure hosted by Mel is Carmela - Imige is my creation specifically for this poem.
The Second Cup
Created on August 15th, 2026
She sets the coffee mug down
with the handle facing my hand,
a gesture she has made
for so many mornings
her fingers know the angle
without consulting her eyes.

The table has recorded
our positions over the years.
The wood has darkened
where my elbow rests,
and the chair legs have scraped
shallow grooves in the floor
at exactly the distance
between her chair and mine.

This morning I reached
for the pepper
and found her fingers
already there,
our hands meeting
over the same wooden mill
for the thousandth time.

We never discussed
where the sugar bowl would live
or how the salt shaker
would migrate to the center,
the way our chairs
have drifted closer
over the years.

Two mugs on the table,
their handles
facing each other.
Author's Notes: Image was created by me for this poem
The Troglodyte's Cave
Created on August 3rd, 2026
Deep within a mossy hole
there dwelt a shaggy troglodyte,
a hobbledehoy in a caveman's role,
all elbows, knees, and appetite.

He'd trip on rocks he'd tripped on twice,
and bonk his head on the ceiling low,
his cave-hair matted, never nice,
a bumbling sort, as cavemen go.

His home was crammed with odd gubbins —
a broken wheel, a chipped stone axe,
a pile of bones from ancient dubbins,
and forty rocks he called "the facts."

He'd organize his precious hoard
by size, by smell, by how they gleamed,
then trip again, and — oh, how he roared! —
watch it scatter, as he screamed.

At supper time he'd feel the dread,
the rumbling deep down in his gut,
for mammoth meat, three days now dead,
gave him collywobbles something fierce, tut-tut.

He'd clutch his belly, moan and groan,
and pace his cave in wobbly rings,
this awkward beast, alone, alone,
undone by half-cooked, spoiled things.

Yet still he lived, this clumsy chap,
this troglodyte of gadgets, gas, and grace,
a hobbledehoy who'd stumbled, gone to nap,
and woken with a smile upon his face.

For gubbins broken, guts a-churning,
knees that knocked and thoughts that strayed —
were simply proof, without much yearning,
that even cavemen's flaws can be okay.
Author's Notes: written for the contest "Silly Words in the English Language" by Mel is Carmela - Image is my creation for this poem.
The Dandelion
Created on July 29th, 2026
I am watching a dandelion cross the yard,
its seed head loose and pale as a held breath,
riding a current I cannot see or name,
though the grass bends slightly to mark its going.

It passes the chair I dragged outside in April
and never brought back in,
the one with a cushion the rain has been slowly
rewriting all summer.

For a moment it hangs above the fence post,
turning the way a thought turns
when you almost remember someone's name,
then lets the wind decide for it.

I think of my father's hand closing
around a stem exactly like this one,
years before I was a fact in the world,
his breath doing what the wind is doing now.

By the time I stand to follow its path
it has already crossed into the neighbor's yard,
settling somewhere among the low weeds,
none of us any wiser for watching.

The chair sits empty behind me,
its cushion darkening at the corners,
holding the shape of no one
in the shape it always holds.
Author's Notes: I created this image for this poem
The Misadventures of Bud, Cherry, Grant and Clara
Created on July 17th, 2026
Young Bud was Weiser than the rest,
he kept a fizzy brew in his vest.
He hissed and foamed when the sun came near —
young Bud was nothing but a beer.

He went to visit Cherry one Sunday,
and found she'd turned into a Sundae.
Whipped cream hair and a cherry crown,
with gooey syrup dripping down.

They stumbled next upon Grant Ted,
who had moss growing on his head.
He gave them everything they wanted,
and so they took Grant Ted for granted.

Then Clara Bell got smeared about,
red and white, inside and out.
With clownish cheeks and painted flair,
she left her mark on every chair.

So Bud and Cherry, Ted and Bell,
went toddling off and all was well.
Ted Grant-ed smiles and Bud Weiser brought brew,
while Cherry Sundae dripped a drop or two.

They left behind no note or clue,
just sticky footprints, paint, and brew.
And if you find a striped armchair,
don't sit too fast upon it there —

for Clara's colours travel far,
from stool to sofa, seat to bar;
and should her paint get smeared somewhere,
you'll wear Bell's mark on your derriere!
The House That Didn’t Ask
Created on July 2nd, 2026
I used to be a plain red building,
brick and mortar, nothing more,
until a hand came, slow, unyielding,
and painted every wall and door.

I did not ask to wear this pattern,
did not choose to be a joke,
but now the tourists stop and flatten
their faces to the glass, yet no one spoke

to warn me I would become a story—
a house too loud for its own street,
too bright to blend, too full of glory,
too strange for anyone discreet.

The yellow house beside me whispers.
The other homes turn heads away.
I stand between two shy companions,
both safer in their quieter way.

My chimneys hold their smoke like secrets,
my windows blink behind the vine.
The flowers at my feet don’t ask it—
they bloom regardless, wild and fine.

Maybe that’s the trick of living:
loud enough that no one’s sure
if it’s the paint or you that’s giving
the street its courage to endure.
Author's Notes: contest Who Lives in this Gorgeous House - PinkFaerie5 - WC 163 - Credit goes to Maria Rojas for the art work
The Senator in the Kitchen
Created on June 5th, 2026
Flour dusts the wooden board
like snow that never melts.
I press my palms into the pale mound
as it pushes back, soft and silent under my tongue.

I watch the pale surface crack under my thumbs,
the word yielding like the paste itself,
a soft coin, blank page we leave empty.
The lamp hums and the dog sleeps.

But then I remember dough names the senator
who bent toward the South from his desk in the North,
the burnt bread, the thin wallet.

The dough forgets its dark syllable.
I fold it again, noticing the crease
catch the light.

Circuit
Created on June 4th, 2026
Water pushes against my tail
and I push back, stirring
the only current in this sphere,
circling the same three inches
I have always circled.
My tail beats and the water moves —
the plastic plant sways,
the gravel shifts,
but the clear wall
never yields an inch.

I know each fraction of this loop:
the shadow where the filter hums,
the green tuft that never grows,
the bend where light warps
my whole world outward.

Then your face arrives,
filling the curved window,
one finger pressing flat
against the outside,
tracing my path as I circle.

You tap and tap, trying
to scratch through glass
that does not give,
your finger sliding
over the shape of my orbit.

I turn toward your hand
every single time.

Three inches of water.
Your palm, warm on the glass.

Sock Monkey
Created on June 3rd, 2026
From the red heel of the work sock, inverted,
the grin emerges—two strands of black yarn
drawn through looped cotton in a running stitch,
mouth set where the manufacturer's crimson
once cushioned an instep. Glass buttons admit
the hall's thin wattage, keep it, shallow and round,
each eye a lens that takes and stores
whatever arrives from the lit rectangle of door.

Arranged at the pillow's selvage each evening,
the stuffed arms rest on chenille, palms up,
fingers curved in their knit skin, ready to receive
whatever weight arrives from darkness—
the elbow, the cheek, the whole small pressing body
that folds against the sewn torso
night after night, the same arrangement,
the same crease deepening where a chin once tucked.

The tail's braid loosens at its tip. The stitched expression
holds toward the doorway while the house
contracts its joists against frost, while the furnace
counts through its ducts. Then one night
the covers did not fold back. The buttons held
only ceiling plaster. The crimson mouth kept
its sewn configuration through the second night,
the third, through weeks of unrumpled chenille,
a pillow unpressed, a spread drawn smooth.

Fresh linen stretched across the ticking.
The doorway held its rectangle of hall.
The monkey sat against the headboard—
yarn fingers open, red mouth sewn shut,
button gaze trained on the threshold's edge.
Glass Brought Through
Created on June 3rd, 2026
She carries it between two rooms of dusk,
The glass inclined, the wine collecting west
In crimson, rounded to the glass's rim
But not beyond. Her thumb secures the base,
Her finger riding on the slender stem
To hold the liquid level as she steps
Past the table's corner, across the rug's
Worn field, toward the wingback chair and him.

He let the pencil drop, the ledger close
On columns that once tracked the shadow's arc
Across the windowsill. He used to mark
Each increment of afternoon, the way
The light contracted, hour by measured hour,
From mullion to the baseboard's molding, down
The wall's plane to the floor. But now the sun
Has slipped below the frame and he has stopped.

The counting stopped, not the decline of light,
Which yet proceeds along the wainscot's edge
In increments no ledger now records,
The shadow's transit that no pen can hold.
What crosses through the darkening room to him
Brings neither answer nor reprieve, but this:
The glass brought whole through dusk, the wine that takes
Within its curve the last of afternoon.

She sets it on the chair's arm and withdraws.
The wine yet trembles, ripples from the rim
Traveling inward while the surface clears
To one dark oval gathering the room:
The ceiling's plaster, window's last red bar,
The rug's worn figures, and the empty wall
Where shadow meets the baseboard's ogee curve—
Crimson on crimson, light on darkening wine.

Two Animals
Created on June 2nd, 2026
The puppy pants against my shin,
her spotted coat, her tucked thin skin.
I press my palm against her ribs
and feel how her small breathing grips.

She dreams, her paws claw at the air—
a leopard watching somewhere there
inside her blood, that ancient eye,
still guarding, still standing by.

She wakes and presses to my side,
her wet nose cold, her mouth open wide.
She pants, she watches, keeps me near,
the leopard pacing, still, still here.

My chest aches, dull, the IV mark
still greens my arm. The yard goes dark.
She leans against me, warm, her weight,
her flank that rises, falls, and waits.

Two animals, both breathing, pressed
together, her fur against my chest.
Her paw against my open hand,
her breath, her pulse, where we still stand.

My Grandmother Knots the Apron Twice
Created on June 2nd, 2026
My grandmother knots the apron strings behind her back. The cotton snags on the dry skin of her thumbs. I press my palm into the dough, and the mound springs back, filling the hollow where my hand pressed. The yeast smells like the inside of a coat closet in March, warm and sharp. Flour drifts onto the floorboards, dusting my feet white. My brother snores through the sliver of the bedroom door, or maybe he listens, his ear tilted toward the stairwell. Grandmother folds the dough, pushes it, folds it again, and the apron pockets sway with the weight of a wooden spoon and a peppermint wrapper from last Tuesday. Last winter she wrung out a washcloth here while my mother wept behind the door she clicked shut. Now the door upstairs leans open an inch, and hallway light spills down the stairs in a thin bar across the kitchen table. The dough rises, the oven ticks, and Grandmother wipes her forehead, leaving flour across her eyebrow as she winks, her hand still buried in the soft center of the bread we will eat at dinner. I find the drawer pull with my shoulder blade, and the door exhales, the apron strings tighten, the dough keeps the print of every hand that shaped it. The yeast bubbles in the bowl. Flour drifts through the hallway sliver and settles on the stairs.

First Sip
Created on June 2nd, 2026
The hummingbird comes while fog holds the marsh
and its throat catches the first gold
through honeysuckle
and the vine has opened every pale trumpet
and waits.

I watch the needle beak enter the deepest fold
and the tongue flick once, twice
and the wings hum and blur
and the small body drink what the dark left--
dew and nectar and the last cold breath
off the black pond.

Then the bird lifts
and the blossom nods, emptied
and the light spreads across the wet grass
and touches the cracked shell at the water's edge
and touches my knee
and touches the pine boughs
and everything it reaches
gleams this one morning.

Whoever you are, no matter how long you have slept,
the dawn does not wait.
The honeysuckle opens. The bird drinks.
The light crosses the field
and does not ask permission.

Will you meet it where it falls?
The Barred Owl
Created on June 2nd, 2026
The owl dropped
from the pines
to the clothesline post,
the wood still warm
from the afternoon sun,
and then the head swiveled
and the black eyes
met mine
across the cut lawn
and the hydrangea
going pale
at dusk.

Its talons gripped
the post's split top
where the sheets had dried
that morning,
and the breast feathers
shivered
as though breath
had touched them,
though nothing
stirred.

I stopped
on the back step,
my palm flat
against the door's
peeling paint,
and the owl
watched
while the yard
darkened
and the first cricket
scratched
from the weed bed.

Tell me,
when did you last
stay still enough
for something with talons
to look at you?
The Book of Rust and Violet
Created on June 1st, 2026
She finds it where the lathe
forgets its name,
wedged between studs,
leather swollen dark as a horse’s flank
after hard rain.
Mold blooms in gray constellations.
The brass clasp holds its one tooth
bitten down on forty years of dust.

She opens it not with eyes
but with the blade of her thumbnail,
prizing the first page
where the ink has eaten clean through,
river-water through limestone.

His father’s father—
the one they erased from the Bible—
had already walked west
with a sack of stolen seed,
the unexpected journey
like a fever in the blood.
So this one, her great-grandfather,
only followed the path
that had no markers.
He walked out of the war,
boots heavy with someone else’s blood,
and kept walking
until the rifle grew roots,
until the bayonet curved
into a scythe for cutting hay.

A pressed violet falls—
still purple, impossibly—
from the entry dated August, 1918.
He stole it from a garden
behind the lines,
carried it not for a lover
but because mercy,
he writes,
is the only ammunition
that multiplies when spent.

She reads how he ferried
the wounded boy in gray
across a wheat field at dusk,
the stalks closing behind them
like a seam in gold satin.
How he learned to listen
not to the god of his fathers
but to the foxfire burning
on the rotting logs—
a light that demands nothing,
only shows the next three feet
of dark forest.

In the margin,
a sketch of her own jaw,
the notch beneath the ear,
drawn decades before she was
even a pinch of salt
in anyone’s palm.
The secret is not scandal.
It is wildness.
The book does not say he was good.
It says he was free.

Her kitchen seems small now,
the refrigerator humming
its one gray note.
She closes the cover,
but the foxfire stays
in the hollow of her collarbone,
a blue-green lamp
for the journey
she has not yet told anyone
she is taking.

Teaspoon
Created on June 1st, 2026
She swept the breadcrumbs
from the cutting board
into the cup of her palm,
the dough she'd punched down
still rising in the bowl
under the damp towel.

He gripped the doorframe,
knuckles bloodless on the jamb,
jaw set tight,
the phone receiver
swinging on its cord.

She wiped her hands on her apron,
crossed the kitchen in sock feet,
the gray ones with the patched heel,
stepped close enough
that he could hear her breathe,
close enough that the warmth
off the stove reached him.

She didn't speak.
Just let her mouth soften,
the corners lifting—
a smile so slight
it could fit in a teaspoon.

The cat on the third stair
blinked once, whiskers catching
the late afternoon light.

Steam rising
between his fingers—
and the dough
kept rising
The Meadow at Dusk
Created on June 1st, 2026
A bent stalk of timothy grass
holds one bead of light
near its seed head,
and the bead trembles
whenever the wind passes.

Goldenrod leans toward shadow.
Queen Anne's lace gathers grayness.
Milkweed silk drifts among the stems.
The meadow loosens itself
from the afternoon.

At the far edge,
a hummingbird hovers over bee balm,
touches one red flower,
then another,
then vanishes into the grass-light.

Now the field carries the evening.
The grasses lift and settle,
lift and settle,
a long green body breathing
under the darkening sky.

Tell me,
when did you last give an hour
to such a place?

A field mouse slips between the stems.
The meadow closes around it
without a sound.

Shut Up
Created on June 1st, 2026
I had been at it for weeks,
holding a cracker near the cage
and repeating simple words—
"hello, pretty bird, hello"—

the way you might teach a child
except the child doesn't stare back
with that flat, prehistoric eye
that sees you and sees through you
at the same time.

I was trying "water" that Tuesday morning,
the coffee going cold on the desk,
when he said it—
cleanly, without hesitation,
the way a judge pronounces a verdict—

"shut up."

I put the cracker down.
Outside, a car moved slowly past the window.
A pigeon landed on the wire across the street,
looked around once,
and flew off without explanation.

The parrot turned on his perch
and faced the wall,
done with me for the day,
done with the whole project of language
and what I had imagined it was for.

The cracker sat on the desk beside the cold coffee.
Somewhere in the house, a door
opened by itself in a small draft,
then slowly closed.
The Silence Inside
Created on May 31st, 2026
I held the phone to my ear this morning,
that smooth black rectangle I've been feeding
with passwords, with photographs, with the shape
of my thumb pressing down on glass
like a prayer I didn't know I was making—
and the screen went dark.

Not dramatically. No flash, no warning buzz.
Just mid-scroll through someone's careful life,
their vacation photos, their new haircut,
their coffee arranged beside an open book,
and the light simply folded itself away.

I pressed the button. Nothing.
Pressed it again, harder, as if insistence
could wake what had decided to sleep.
The same nothing, smooth and absolute.

There's something almost honest about it,
the way it doesn't pretend to struggle,
doesn't flicker hopefully when I shake it,
doesn't drain itself in slow percentages
like the old phones did, clinging to one bar,
one percent, one last gasped message
before the final dark.

This one just stopped. A small abdication.
And now I'm sitting in the sudden quiet,
the way you might sit in a room
after the music ends, hearing the shape
of what's no longer there—no hum, no glow,
no need to answer, scroll, perform, become
whatever the screen asked me to be.

Just my hands, strangely light now,
holding this warm weight of nothing,
this small brick of silence I can't throw away,
this thin door that won't open again,

and behind it, somewhere, the strange relief
of being unreachable, the world still turning
without my thumbs moving across it,
my face no longer lit from below,
just breathing here in the sudden darkness,
perfectly still, waiting to be seen.
At the Casement
Created on May 30th, 2026
The casement holds a last intensity 
Of western light that trembles on the glass, 
As though the pane itself were loath to see 
The source of day relinquish dominance. 

A wren alights upon the weathered sill 
And tilts its head toward fields already dim, 
Where one broad furnace cools against its will 
And something pale ascends to balance him. 

They pass in silence measurable as breath, 
The old provider and the quiet thief 
Of color from the world; then sudden red 
Suffuses all the margin of the west, 

As if a murmured judgment, cool and keen, 
Had touched the sun’s broad pride and made it flush. 
What word could make the maker of the scene 
Betray such warmth before his daily death? 

Perhaps the moon remarked how even fire 
Must learn the temper of a softer blade, 
How day’s fierce forging leaves the world entire 
But night’s reflection shows what day has made. 

The casement darkens; on the sill the wren 
Lifts from its perch and vanishes in flight 
Against the bronze that deepens into slate, 
Where one last ember settles out of sight.

Lie Down
Created on May 29th, 2026
I am walking through the wet field
before the sun has opened
its whole hand on the horizon.
The timothy bends and holds
the last of the fog in its seedheads.
My boots sink slightly.
The spider has tied her thread
between two yarrow stalks,
and she herself is smaller
than a grain of wild rice,
hanging in her own silver.

Something moves at the edge of the pines.
Not the wind.
The brown flank of a doe steps from the shadows
and does not lift her listening into the long neck
the way they usually do,
standing still as a stone
while the gnats rise and fall.
She turns, and behind her,
smaller than a thrown shadow, the fawn follows,
and its legs are still learning the earth,
and its coat is spotted
like light falling through leaves.

They cross the open ground
with the knowing that lives in the blood,
the old agreement between hoof and grass,
ear and air.
She does not look back,
but the fawn, once,
turns its dark eye to the trees they are leaving.
And the grass is taller there,
mixed with clover and vetch,
and the stems are heavy and ripe.
Into that green abundance they go,
and the stalks close over the spotted back,
the little ears,
until even the white flag has vanished.

Tell me, what does she know
about the open meadow that I have forgotten?
How to step into the clearing
and trust the distance?
How to lie down in the tall weight of summer
and become nothing
but the small lungs breathing?
How to give the body of what you love
to the wide daylight?

She knows the fox travels the edge of the wood.
She knows danger wears the patience of stone.
She knows the field is both door and wall,
the grass both breath and held breath.

Lie down.
Let the grass close over you.
The hawk circles.
The clover opens.
The Glovebox
Created on May 29th, 2026
The glovebox needs a good shake before it opens,
and when it does, the dog in the back seat
lifts his chin from his paws to study the situation,
as if any latching compartment in a parked car
might contain something necessary to the canine future.

Inside is the archaeology of my mother’s afternoons:
napkins from restaurants that have since closed,
a tire pressure gauge, a map folded the wrong way,
and a blue envelope with my name written in the kind of cursive
they stopped teaching two decades ago,
the letters already relaxing into the paper
like people settling onto a couch they recognize.

I was only looking for proof of insurance,
something official to wave at the authority of traffic,
but here is a letter postmarked twenty-five years ago,
its single sentence breathing with the patience
of a thing that knows everything about waiting.

This is how they taught us to approach a poem—
with clean hands and a scalpel ready,
pinning the butterfly of meaning to a corkboard
and labeling its parts until it forgot how to fly.

But then this piece of paper refuses to be tortured.
It is only a woman I used to live inside of,
sitting at a kitchen table in another century,
pressing a hospital gift-shop ballpoint into cheap stationery
to write welcome to this world son
before she knew what kind of world this was,
or whether I would turn out to be worth the welcome.

And yet the sentence asks nothing of me.
It simply waited in the dark behind the map
while the straws collected their thin white shadows,
as patient as anything that knows
it has all the time in the world.

I am sliding it back behind the napkins,
the gauge, the small history of a woman
who drove and stopped and drove again,
when the dog, who has been watching all along,
lets out a sigh that steams the cold window,
and the afternoon sunlight fills the empty passenger seat.
The Unseen Wall
Created on May 28th, 2026
I stand in the corner where the light
does not reach. The dust settles
on my shoulders like a second skin.
No one turns when I enter.

The room continues its business—
chairs scrape, voices rise and fall,
coffee cups find their saucers.
I could be air. I could be stone.

But watch—the window holds a sparrow
pressed against the glass, searching
for the opening that isn't there.
Its throat pulses. Its claws grip
the narrow ledge. It does not leave.

I know something about waiting
against transparent barriers.
I know the weight of being unseen
while the world moves through you
like water through a net.

The bird tilts its head. Studies
the room it cannot enter.
I study the bird I cannot reach.
Between us, the glass holds
both reflection and distance.

Then—it finds a seam. A gap
where the frame meets the wall.
One careful shift of weight,
one breath held and released,
and it slips into open sky.

I breathe with it. The room
notices nothing. But something
has changed. The dust on my sleeve
catches light I didn't see before.

Invisibility is not absence.
It is a kind of gathering.
Like roots under snow.
Like stone remembering water.
Like the archer's bow holding
tension before the release.

I step from the corner.
The floor accepts my weight.
The air parts for me.
I am here. I am here.

Residency
Created on May 28th, 2026
There is this tiny little spider
that has taken residency in the corner
of my bathroom window.
He hangs there like a comma
at the end of a long sentence of glass.

I brush my teeth and watch him watch the yard.
He is waiting for a fly to wander in,
or perhaps he is just listening
to the hum of the house settling,
the pipes singing behind the wall.

We have come to an agreement, he and I.
I will not spray him with anything,
and he will not drop down on a thread
to land in the sink while I am shaving.
It is a fragile treaty based on silence.

He knows the texture of the glass better than I do.
He knows the vibration of the sash.
When I tap the pane, he does not move.
He trusts the structure of his chosen angle.

But then the morning light shifts slightly,
and he becomes a speck of dust
that has decided to remain suspended.
And I realize we are both just holding on
to whatever corner we have found,
waiting for the day to bring us something
we can catch, or simply let go.
Monday Morning Staring At The Glass
Created on May 26th, 2026
I have been looking at the corner of the window pane,
where a tiny spider, no larger than a match head,
has decided to construct a modest web
between the wood and the glass,
unaware that this is a pane of glass and not the open air,
unaware that I am sitting here in my favorite armchair
drinking coffee that has gone slightly cold.

It is a remarkably poor location for business.
No flies ever come to this side of the house,
except for the occasional disoriented gnat
that usually ends up drowning in my mug anyway,
leaving us both a little disappointed.
And yet, he works with the quiet intensity
of a medieval monk illuminating a manuscript,
stretching his invisible silk from anchor point to anchor point,
as if the future of the world depended on this three-inch kingdom.

Watching him, I am reminded of how we spend our days
building these neat, intricate frameworks in the corners of our lives,
fully expecting the universe to deliver exactly what we asked for.
We check our watches, we adjust our glasses,
we turn the page of a book we have already read twice,
as if the mere act of paying attention
could persuade the world to behave.

But then, I remember a summer forty years ago,
standing on a dock in British Columbia,
watching the salmon boat crews patch their nylon seines by hand.
They used long plastic needles, moving with a rhythm so fluid
it looked less like work and more like a slow, heavy dance,
mending holes big enough to let a whole school of silver slip away.
They didn't look at the sky, or the water, or the looming fog;
they just looked at the knot right in front of them.

And I suppose that is what we are all doing here.
The spider has finished his architecture now
and is sitting motionless at the very center of his grid,
waiting for a breeze or an inheritance.
I lift my cup to take another sip of the lukewarm brew,
careful not to lean too close,
not wanting my breath to cause a hurricane
in a world that is already so fragile,
and so perfectly, uselessly square.
The Autumn Alchemist
Created on May 25th, 2026
Beneath a blue-green cap of woven mist,
He stirs the pot with gnarled, weathered wrist.
A woodland keeper in his hidden nook,
Where recipes are learned and not by book.

Through paned and rustic glass, the gold light leaks,
On silver beard and lines upon his cheeks.
The vines creep in, a tender, leafy lace,
As ancient magic warms this quiet place.

With herbs from shadowed roots and sun-kissed soil,
He transforms every daily harvest toil.
A pinch of moss, a mushroom's earthy scent,
The forest’s truest, deepest soul is blent.

The scent of paprika and pumpkin pie
Will draw the hedgehogs as they waddle by.
This isn’t just a stew he blends and brews;
It’s hearth and home and autumn’s varied hues.

So, in the heart of this cozy, wild domain,
The gnome alchemist, unbothered by the rain,
Tends to his kettle with a patient eye,
And fills the woods with spice as time drifts by.

What The Hand Remembers
Created on May 25th, 2026
The earth here is the color of old ceremony,
red-brown and iron-warm beneath the fingers,
holding the memory of a thousand plantings
in its layered dark.

She kneels at the field's edge
where the mesa catches the first gold
and presses each seed into its small hollow—
three together, the way it has always been done,
the way her grandmother's hands knew without thinking.

The corn is not yet corn.
It is still a kind of faith,
pale and closed as a held breath,
resting in the rust-colored quiet.

Yet the sky above the canyon
opens blue as water, blue as forgetting—
and the light coming over the ridge
moves across the field the way a hand
moves across something it loves,
slowly, touching everything.

This is what the hands remember
when the mind has grown quiet:
the press and release,
the covering over,
the letting go into the dark
that is not darkness but waiting.

A hawk turns on a thermal far above,
reading the warm air like old script,
and the mesa holds its ancient color—
ochre, sienna, the red that has no other name—
while below, three seeds lie curved together
like a word just spoken,
already becoming something
the sky will have to lean down to see.
