A Pocket of Stillness
Created on May 25th, 2026
The granite shoulder wakes first,
a single vein of quartz catching the early flame,
gleaming like hidden bone remembering the sun.

I stand in the hush of the valley floor
where pine needles stitch the earth in copper thread,
and the air itself feels newly born—
cool, sharp, tasting of snowmelt and stone.

The mountain does not hurry.
It leans into the light the way an old friend
leans into silence, letting it settle
across its ridged brow.

Yet as the sun climbs,
color loosens from the rock—
rose into lavender into a blue with no name—
and the ridges begin their slow conversation
with the sky.

I am small here,
smaller than the lichen's quiet patience,
yet the mountain makes room for me—
a pocket of stillness shaped exactly
to the curve of one wondering heart.

But then the wind arrives,
gentle as forgiveness,
and the aspens answer with their thousand silver tongues.
Light pours through them in sudden shafts,
holy and unasked for,
turning ordinary air into something
it has no name for either.

And I understand nothing completely,
only that the stone has been dreaming
for ten thousand years,
and I have been allowed
to stand inside its dream.

The peaks keep their ancient vigil,
carrying the horizon on their shoulders
as if it were the lightest thing—
a blue promise they have never broken.

I turn my face upward,
and the mountain turns with me.
Passing notice
Created on May 24th, 2026
I drove the old truck down the river road 
where morning light lay thin across the fields,
and felt my mother’s care still in the wheel— 
how she had handed me the keys that day,
her hand steady as the years had made it. 

In the glove compartment, folded once,
I found my father’s obituary,
the paper worn along its creases now,
ink faded where her fingers must have touched 
it many times in secret, keeping watch. 

The pasture opened on my left, the grass 
still wet with dew, and the apple tree 
stood heavy with its fruit, remembering 
the hands that planted it and pruned its limbs 
through all the seasons of their quiet life. 

Yet loss comes to us as the rain comes,
slow and certain, turning the good earth dark. 
I thought how they had kept their vows to land 
and to each other, working side by side 
through frost and drought, through joy and wearing down. 

We learn too late the weight of what is given— 
a mother’s final carefulness, a father’s 
name set down in black and white, the years 
they held between them like a well-kept field. 
The obligation stays, to tend what they began. 

I stopped the truck beside the fenceline, cut 
the engine, and let the silence settle in. 
Two hawks turned high above the ridge, riding 
the same wind that once moved across their faces. 
The work remains. The love remains. I go on.
The First Lilly
Created on May 24th, 2026
I came down to the pond before first light 
and stood at the edge where the reeds begin. 
Mist lay across the dark water 
like breath on a mirror, and already 
the first lily had opened— 
its white petals loosening 
one by one into the soft gray air,
each one catching the smallest rumor of gold. 

A turtle slid from its log without sound,
leaving a single silver ring that widened 
and disappeared. Frogs called low and steady,
not yet the full chorus, but the first 
tentative voices testing the morning. 
I watched a dragonfly hover,
its wings beating so fast they vanished,
then settle on a blade of grass still bent 
with last night’s rain. 

Everything was waking 
in its own quiet way— 
the water striders sketching their thin lines,
the small fish rising to take the light 
that had begun to slant through the trees. 
I tell you, there is such tenderness here,
in the slow unfurling of petals,
in the patient return of green to the banks. 

And therefore I stay longer than I meant to,
letting the pond teach me again 
how to be present, how to love 
what is brief and shining and ordinary. 
You could do worse than to come here 
at this hour and simply stand,
letting the world open itself 
without asking anything back. 

The lily keeps lifting its face 
toward whatever arrives next,
and the dark water holds it all— 
reflection and root, beginning and end— 
in one clear, unbroken silence.
The Kitchen Window
Created on May 24th, 2026
I was thinking about love again 
this morning, the way it refuses 
to stay in the places we assign it. 
I stood at the kitchen window 
holding yesterday’s coffee,
watching a single drop of rain 
slide down the pane, hesitate 
at a smudge, then keep going 
as if it had somewhere better to be. 

The book you left on the sill 
lay open to a page you never finished,
a margin crowded with your faint pencil marks— 
little arrows pointing at nothing,
a question mark curled like a cat. 
I didn’t read it. I just liked 
how the paper had softened 
where your thumb must have rested,
the way cheap ink had bled 
into the fibers, turning one word 
into a small gray pond. 

Outside, the neighbor’s dog barked once 
and then thought better of it. 
A car went by, spraying water 
that caught the light for half a second 
before it disappeared. 
Nothing dramatic. Just the usual 
Tuesday traffic of small things 
doing what small things do. 

And yet I stood there longer than I meant to,
holding the mug that has your lipstick 
faint as a rumor on the rim. 
I wondered if love is less like the ocean 
we always claim it is 
and more like this— 
a slow leak under the sink,
a bookmark made from a torn ticket stub,
the quiet refusal of a drop of rain 
to fall straight down. 

I closed the book gently,
set it back exactly where you left it. 
Some things, I’ve learned,
you don’t press too hard 
or they stop telling you the truth.
Love and Doubt
Created on May 15th, 2026
I never doubted that
love was worth the risk.
I refused to believe
the pain would outlast the joy.
It was foolish to think
I could not be whole again.
What I know now is
love finds a way back home.
Author's Notes: contest Reversible Love - P.K. Star - A Reversable Poem - The poem doesn't change — only the direction you walk through it does. That's the whole point: love and its pain are made of the same moments, just experienced in different orders.
Threshold
Created on May 14th, 2026
I have been thinking about the word joy
and how strange it is that we gave it to daughters—
actual daughters, named it right onto them,
as if a child could hold
what the rest of us keep dropping.

Three letters. One syllable.
You can say it while barely opening your mouth,
which seems like a design flaw
given everything it is expected to carry.

I looked it up, as one does when a word
starts behaving suspiciously.
Old French. Latin before that.
And before that, probably a woman
in a bright room, already crying,
which is where I want to begin.

She is not crying from joy.
Let us not rush past that
toward the prettier explanation.
She is crying from pain—
the kind that lives in the back, the hips,
the place below the ribs
that has no polite name
because no one wants to linger there.

And then a cry answers hers.
Small. New. Furious with life.

And something arrives inside the pain
the way light does not erase shadows
but quietly changes what they mean.

And she weeps. Still from pain.
Now also from the other thing.
The body, that faithful and literal instrument,
cannot tell you which is which.
It is using the same word for both.
The word is not a word.
It is water.

She names him morning.
She names him river.
She does not name him temporary,
though that would be the accurate name,
the one that holds up under scrutiny,
the one no parent has ever had the heart to use.

Every name is a small forgetting,
a door set into the wall of loss
and painted a cheerful color
so you do not notice, for a while,
what it is set into.

Her words bloom around him at the breakfast table—
rose-gold above the cereal, above the small face
that has already stopped hearing I love you
as anything but furniture.
She says it again.
Which is its own kind of question.
Which is, underneath everything,
a quiet pain—
the pain of loving someone
who is already, imperceptibly,
growing away.

Not gone.
But leaving in the slow way
that is almost worse,
the way that was always the point,
which does not make it
not hurt.

Meanwhile, in another room—
less light, different year, same woman—
grief has moved in without asking.
It lives in the chest, a stone
that crossed from outside to inside
sometime in the night.
It lives in the stomach.
In the throat.
In the sound that gets stuck
behind something that will not move.

Grief, it turns out, is pain.
Different occasion. Same body.
Same nerve sending the same message
it always sends when something
exceeds the size of silence.

The salt runs down the same face,
the same rivers,
the same chin.
Falls.

Those who did not know her then
say: grief.
Those who knew her in the bright room
say nothing—
because they remember the other falling water
and they cannot tell you,
standing here,
if these are different tears
or the same tear,
grown older,
finally arrived.

This is the thing about pain:
it is not the third emotion.
It is the ground.
Joy stands on it.
Grief stands on it.
Every large and unsayable thing
stands on it
and looks out at the view
and does not mention the floor.

So when you see someone crying
and reach for the nearest word—
joy, you think, or grief, depending
on the occasion and your own recent losses—
know that you are probably wrong.
Or right about the occasion
and wrong about the ground.

Tears are not a window.
They are a signal:
something here has crossed a line.
The body marks all three the same way.
It was only the words
that kept insisting on the difference,
that kept building borders
on a floor that was always
one floor.

The mother who wept when he arrived
and the mother who wept when he was gone
is the same mother.

She has never left the doorway.

Not because she cannot move—
she has moved, she has made coffee,
she has said I love you above the cereal
to children who have stopped listening—
but because threshold is not a line you cross.
It is the place you become.
The place all three arrive
and do not leave,
and you learn, slowly,
to call it home.

She does not choose a word.
She lets the water speak.
She lets the salt
do its ancient work,
which it has been doing
long before we had names for any of this,
and will be doing
long after the names
are gone.
Lingering
Created on May 14th, 2026
Here is what no one tells you: the rule that bends is not the one you'd choose. Sound stays. Every syllable hangs in the air like slow smoke — visible for days, for weeks, for as long as it carried something true, which is to say some words never leave at all.

Walk any city. The streets are thick with it. Lovers' whispers braided pale between buildings. Arguments gone to wire, snagging sleeves, throats, the soft place under the jaw. A mother's I love you at the breakfast table loops gold above the milk jug, above the cereal, above the child who has already stopped hearing it — fading only when she says it again, which is its own kind of question.

And the boy in the school hallway, his I'm fine drifting ash-gray, neither rising nor settling, waiting — for what, exactly? Someone to look. Someone to not look away. The ash goes on drifting. This is not a metaphor. This is the physics of the thing.

This morning I told my daughter her pancakes were perfect. I watched the words bloom and twine, rose-gold, around her small wrists like bracelets, like a gentle binding, and she smiled, and I could not tell — still cannot — whether what I made was a gift or a debt she will spend years learning to name.

At night the city layers: old lullabies beneath first confessions beneath last words, an archaeology no one commissioned and no one can stop. Some corners are unbreathable with it. Others shine. You learn to move through both. You learn, which is not the same as you understand, which is not the same as it stops.
Author's Notes: contest Bend One Rule of Reality - Lily Ash - Word Count 279 - Prose Poem Sound never fades
Currency
Created on May 13th, 2026
For a moment I was a small
planet, tumbling through my own
atmosphere — heads, then not,
then heads again, the sky and the dark
water trading places
with a kind of indifference to my opinion.

I caught a glimpse of the person
who threw me — their face
already turning away, the wish
already becoming something else,
a grocery list, a grudge, the particular
shame of having wished for money
at a wishing well.

Nobody tells you what the wish is.
That is the protocol, apparently.
You carry it the way a river
carries whatever falls into it —
without knowing, without asking,
with a muscular, unthinking care.

And so I have had time —
more time than I expected,
more time than the euro beside me
who arrived from another country
with what I can only assume
were very specific hopes —
to make one of my own.

It is small, as wishes go.
It would embarrass me
to say it too loudly down here
among the others, all of them
holding their human wishes
like something fragile,
like something that might still
come true.

I wish to be handed to a child
who holds me up to the light
and asks what the man on my face
is looking at so seriously,
off in that distance —
and I wish, too, that I could tell her:
the same thing I am looking at.
The same distance.
The same light, going.
Author's Notes: Contest: A Wish from a Wishing Well
Will You Answer?
Created on April 27th, 2026
verse 1
High upon a concrete ledge,
A man stands balanced on the edge.
Below, a hundred phones held high,
Live-streaming someone's elses goodbye.
Someone shouts "Just jump, be done!
And laughter spreads through everyone.
He had a name. He had a son.
He had a war he never won.

verse 2
Pink slip Tuesday, locks were changed,
His medication rearranged.
He called the hotline, no one came.
Tomorrow he will be clicks and claims.
We watched it live, we shared the clip,
We typed our prayers, then let it slip
Into the scroll, the endless feed,
Another life we didn't need.

chorus
Will you answer when the call comes?
Will you stand when others run?
Or just turn away from the setting sun?
Will you answer? Will anyone?

verse 3
A boy of seventeen lies still
On concrete cold beneath the hill.
His mother running, slippers bare,
Screams of sirens in the air.\n
She holds his face, she begs his name,
But nothing but the echoes came.
They march on Sunday, light a flame,
By Wednesday there's another name.

verse 4
Another mother, another son,
Another hashtag, another gun.
The preachers say God has a plan,
But God ain't stopping bullets, man.

instrumental - harmonica solo, guitar picking intensifies

verse 5
On a road of dust and steel,
A young man drops, too numb to feel.
A photograph against his chest
His baby girl, three months at best.
Eight thousand miles from her first breath,
He trades his last one in for death.

verse 6
A senator will clear his throat
And read some words that someone wrote.
"A hero's sacrifice," he will say,
And vote for war again the next day.
By Monday no one knows the name
Beneath the flag, beneath the flame.
We stand, we clap, we fly the flag,
We slap a ribbon on the bag.
We say support, but what we mean
Is out of sight and off my screen.

bridge
You saw the post, you kept on scrolling,
You heard the cry, you kept on strolling,
You watched the news between your bites
Of comfort food on comfort nights.
The mirror cracks, the truth bleeds through —
The somebody was always you.

chorus
Will you answer when the call comes?
Will you stand when others run?
Or just turn away from the setting sun?
Will you answer? Will anyone?\n\n

outro - guitar becomes sparse, voice whispers
So, will you answer?
When the lights go dark and the world won't see,
Will you answer?
Or keep dancing through the tragedy.
The ledge, the war, the blood, the floor —
Can't close your eyes and dance no more.
Will you answer?

Changes
Created on April 24th, 2026
I did not know I was waiting,
which is the purest form of waiting,
the kind that has no clock to watch,
no window to look out of,
only the warm curved fact of everything
pressing in from all sides
like a very small and serious opinion.

Something large moved over me once
and the warmth changed its mind briefly
before coming back,
and I held myself the way you hold
a thought you almost lost —
tightly, without knowing you are holding.

Then the cold arrived,
the way it always does,
without introducing itself,
and I had no word for it
because I had no words for anything yet,
which was, I can see now,
a significant advantage.

The cracking, when it came,
was my idea and not my idea,
the way most important things are,
and suddenly there was a leaf
and the leaf had a name I didn't know
and a bird above it
whose intentions were not friendly,
and hunger,
which explained everything
and nothing
about why I was suddenly here
and moving.

I will not describe what happened next
inside the darkness I made myself —
suffice it to say that I came undone
in the most literal sense,
which philosophers have been gesturing at for centuries
with considerably more words
and less result.

And now I am standing on the outside
of the last self I remember,
wings I did not order
drying slowly in the morning,

and the air is just there,
enormous and available,

and I am almost ready
to find out what I think about that.
Day after Day
Created on April 24th, 2026
The morning light is filtered through the blinds,
a house of blueprints and of master plans.
We polish up the edges of our minds
and hold the world with trembling, steady hands.

We trace the lines of who we're meant to be,
a perfect sketch of life in symmetry,
but shadows pool beneath the kitchen floor
and silence knocks upon the bedroom door.

It isn't about the cure that sets you free
or finding where the broken pieces fit.
It's learning how to live with what we see
and finding grace within the thick of it.

We don't need "happy," "perfect," or "sublime" —
just something we can carry through the time,
a little light to help us through the storm,
not quite normal, but something close to warm.

The mountains that we climb are made of ghosts,
of memories we're told we should forget.
We cling to what we think we need the most
and pay a price we haven't measured yet.

But medicine can't dull the sting of truth
and lightning cannot jump-start stolen youth.
The walls we build to keep the sorrow out
are the same ones built of agony and doubt.

Maybe the hole is meant to stay wide open,
maybe the hurt is how we know we're here.
There is a dignity in being broken
that never grows inside a life of fear.

You have to let the memory take its shape.
There is no easy logic or escape,
just the courage to stand up and let it go
into the vast and terrifying know.

So let the stars come out into the black.
We're moving forward, never looking back.
The sky is gray, the wind is rising high,
but we are walking underneath the sky.

Day after day.
Light.
Just give us light.

My Shadow Monster
Created on April 22nd, 2026
There's a thing that comes creeping wherever I go,
it follows my feet on the ground!
It stretches and squishes and puts on a show,
without making one single sound!

I jumped on it! Stomped it! It didn't say ow!
I stood on its slippery head!
It just spread out flatter — I don't know quite how —
and it still didn't fall over dead!

I ran from it! Sprinted! It came right along,
it stuck to my heels like glue!
That dark squishy copycat, sneaky and strong,
did every last thing that I do!

Then I waved my right hand and it waved its right hand,
and I waved my left — so did it!
And I figured it out — it was perfectly planned —
it's just ME! And I have to admit:

Having two of me — one in the air, one below —
is just about perfectly great!
I'm incredibly handsome from both sides, you know,
and my shadow thinks I'm really EIGHT!
Author's Notes: contest Sesame Street 2 - Macandrew
Morning Light
Created on April 22nd, 2026
The morning light is filtered through the blinds,
a house of blueprints and of master plans.
We polish up the edges of our minds
and hold the world with trembling, steady hands.

We trace the lines of who we're meant to be,
a perfect sketch of life in symmetry,
but shadows pool beneath the kitchen floor
and silence knocks upon the bedroom door.

It isn't about the cure that sets you free
or finding where the broken pieces fit.
It's learning how to live with what we see
and finding grace within the thick of it.

We don't need "happy," "perfect," or "sublime" —
just something we can carry through the time,
a little light to help us through the storm,
not quite normal, but something close to warm.

The mountains that we climb are made of ghosts,
of memories we're told we should forget.
We cling to what we think we need the most
and pay a price we haven't measured yet.

But medicine can't dull the sting of truth
and lightning cannot jump-start stolen youth.
The walls we build to keep the sorrow out
are the same ones built of agony and doubt.

Maybe the hole is meant to stay wide open,
maybe the hurt is how we know we're here.
There is a dignity in being broken

You have to let the memory take its shape.
There is no easy logic or escape,

What the House Remembers
Created on April 22nd, 2026
I have noticed, these past few weeks,
that the kitchen smells of wet ferns in the morning,
that particular green-dark smell of something
pressed and bruised underfoot,
though there are no ferns,
and the vents, when I hold my hand to them,
push out only the ordinary air
of a house doing its best.

And the counter—
I wipe it before bed, which is a kind of prayer,
and by morning there is a fine gray silt again,
distributed evenly, as if the night
had been busy with small administrative tasks.

Somewhere under the basement,
something shifts—
not a crack, not a leak,
but the low, patient complaint
of something that has been lying still
for a very long time
and is only now remembering
it had somewhere to be.

The shadows of oak trees move along the east wall
in the way shadows do when trees are swaying,
though I know, without looking,
that there are no oaks.
I have lived here seven years.
I know every tree.

The front door has been sticking lately.
I pull it toward me the way you coax
a reluctant conversation,
and it comes, eventually,
but not happily.

Last Thursday, in the bathroom mirror,
I watched myself stand in the middle of a thicket—
dark cedar and blackthorn pressing in from all sides,
the room behind me gone entirely—
while I held my toothbrush
and did not move.

I have read that forests have long memories.
That the soil keeps a record the way libraries do:
everything that stood here, everything that leaned,
every root that stitched itself through clay,
every creek that found the path of least resistance
and followed it for ten thousand years
until someone laid a foundation across its throat.

I used to think the house was haunted.
Now I think the house is borrowed.

And tonight, when the floor gives a long, slow exhale
beneath the kitchen table,
I do not check for leaks.

I simply sit still
and let the creek
take its time
finding me.
The Hand Remains Open (The Space Between Breaking)
Created on April 13th, 2026
the cup left the hand
and turned once in the air
slow as a leaf in still water

the floor waited
the tiles were cold and separate

in that turning
the light found its way
through the space where the whole had been

not warm
not kind
only the color of rain after it has passed

I have stood there
watching the arc complete itself
as if the breaking were a question

the hand remained open
nothing in it now
nothing to close

you were still breathing then
or the word had not yet landed
or the door had swung but not shut

the light stayed
filling the gap
making each edge sharp

as the last thing seen
before the dark knows its name

the cup lies
in its pieces
each one exact

and the silence between them
goes on listening

What She Gave Me
Created on April 5th, 2026
I had been told coyotes were dangerous,
which is true the way most true things are —
partially, and not today.

She was lying in the tall grass at the field's edge,
one rear leg bent the wrong way,
her tawny sides rising and falling
with the careful economy of the badly hurt.

I stood at the truck a moment longer than I needed to,
checking the blanket in my hands,
thinking about teeth.

But when I walked toward her
she lifted her amber eyes to mine
and what was in them was not a threat —
it was something closer to a question,
the kind you ask when you have run out
of other options and decided, for now,
to trust.

I covered her with the blanket.
She did not growl.
I lifted her — ten, maybe twelve pounds
of heat and stillness —
and she let me.

All the way to the clinic she did not struggle.
She lay in the crate the way the very sick lie,
present and not present,
here and somewhere else already.

The vet met me at the door
and I knew from his face
before he said anything.

Tell me —
what do you do with the weight of a thing
that chose you, briefly,
at the end?

She is in the grass somewhere now,
or she is the grass,
and I am still carrying
the particular amber of those eyes,
still learning what she was asking.
Author's Notes: I have been a wildlife rehabilitator for over 30 years now. This experience happened shortly after I began my journey helping wild animals. I had never rescued a Coyote before and had all these pre conceived ideas as to what might happen. Non of which turned out to be true.
The Right Amount
Created on April 2nd, 2026
At the desk this morning, next to the lamp 
still burning from last night's reading,
I come across the old definition again— 
black as absence, the color that is not a color,
the empty theater before the play begins. 

But then I think of the photographs 
pinned above my daughter's bed: 
the Pillars of Creation, the Ring Nebula,
each one mostly black, mostly nothing,
and still we cannot stop looking. 

Black is what holds the stars apart,
what gives them room to burn,
the way silence gives shape to music,
or the margin frames the page 
where someone has underlined a single word. 

It occurs to me that we have gotten this backwards. 
What if black is not the absence of color 
but the presence of all of them at once,
perfectly balanced, each one accounted for— 
red and blue and yellow and green 
mixed so carefully, so completely,
that they arrive at a kind of peace,
the way voices in a choir 
become a single note? 

I remember standing in a museum once,
watching a painter demonstrate 
how every color on his palette,
when mixed together long enough,
became a shade of black or gray— 
not emptiness, he said,
but fullness taken to its logical end,
all arguments resolved,
all claims settled. 

Maybe this is why we lean toward black 
when we want other colors to speak: 
the red dress against the evening,
the white sail on dark water,
the yellow leaves of October 
against the wet bark of the oak. 

Black does not erase them. 
It gives them a place to stand,
the way a stage goes dark 
so we can see the single candle,
the way the sky dims at dusk 
so we can finally notice 
how green the grass has been all day. 

I have stood on a beach in the islands 
where the sky and water were both so dark 
they became the same black fabric,
seamless, salt-edged,
and the only light was phosphorescence 
breaking where my hands moved through the waves— 
blue-green fire, cold and brief,
made visible only by the blackness 
that held it, that gave it somewhere to burn. 

Even the canvas, before the painter arrives,
wants to be white, we think,
but how many paintings begin in darkness,
the artist building light on top of shadow,
laying down black first 
so the colors that come after 
have something to push against,
some weight to measure themselves by? 

Maybe black is not too little 
but exactly enough— 
not the absence of red or blue or gold 
but their sum, their total,
the place where they all agree to rest. 

The right amount of color 
for the moon to float in,
for the ocean to remember at midnight,
for your face in the window 
when you pause between sentences,
thinking of what to say next. 

Even now, the black text on this white page— 
these words would mean nothing 
without the space around them,
and the space would mean nothing 
without the words. 
Each one needs the other 
to make sense of what they're doing here. 

So perhaps black is the conversation,
not the silence. 
The fullness, not the void. 
The room where all the colors went 
and decided to stay,
close enough to touch,
quiet enough to hear 
each other breathing.
Author's Notes: contest COLOR ME... (fill in blank here >:]) - Aislynn Rue
Unspoken
Created on April 2nd, 2026
She does not speak her need, nor should she—
Words are the cool web that would betray
What silence holds more close than any vow.
A glance across the room will do, or how
Her hand finds mine when dusk comes on, the way
Old soldiers know their ground instinctively.

We have made peace with what cannot be said.
The lexicon we've learned runs deep as bone:
Her shoulder touching mine means "I am here";
My palm against her cheek translates as "near
Enough". We do not name what we have known—
To name it would be to wish the language dead.

Some loves announce themselves with fanfare, claim
Their territory loud as conquering kings.
Ours keeps its counsel. When she brings me tea
Exactly when the morning requires it, we
Have spoken volumes. All the murmuring
Of sweethearts seems to us a kind of shame.

The Goddess does not ask to be adored
In words—she knows them false, however fine.
Better the brief pressure of her hand,
The knowing smile I need not understand
But answer with a touch along her spine.
We serve her best by what we leave unscored.

And so we go through days like monks who've taken
Some vow of silence, though we have not sworn
To anything but this: that love runs truer
In the unspoken current than the fewer
Or greater protestations others make each morn.
We are most faithful when we are forsaken

By speech. Last night she woke and touched my face—
No cause, no reason, just her fingers pressed
Against my cheek as if to check I breathed.
I did not ask her why. We have bequeathed
Our meanings to the dark. It knows us best.
The word for love is: leaving it no trace.
Author's Notes: contest Celebrating Love #2 - Heavenly Angel
White Rabbit, No Apologies
Created on March 31st, 2026
I was sitting in a kitchen with cold coffee,
listening to Grace Slick tell me to follow
a white rabbit down a hole, and I thought:
here is a woman not playing with a full deck,
inviting me to board a plane headed somewhere
between breakfast and nightmare, between
the mushroom and the sky, and making it sound
like the only sensible thing to do.

The song doesn't pretend to make sense.
It arrives certain in its unreasonableness,
so sure of itself that you begin to trust
the rabbit more than you trust the ground.
That's the whole trick. That's how you get people
to believe in impossible things: you sing them
into it, the way a pilot who's lost his compass
still sounds authoritative at the controls.

And I thought about Mary Oliver asking,
"Tell me, what is it you plan to do with your one wild life?"
Well, Grace Slick has an answer:
follow the white rabbit. Take the pills.
Don't ask which direction is down.

Because here's the absurdity nobody mentions—
we're all supposed to be looking for meaning,
for some destination that makes sense,
but the song is telling you the sense
is in the falling itself, in the speed,
in the pill that makes you larger or smaller
depending on which side of the mushroom
you're standing on.

All these years later, the song still works.
Still makes you want to board that plane,
still makes the irrational seem
like the only available flight path.
Though by now we're all used to it—
the pill-taking, the speed, the belief
that if we just keep moving, keep listening,
we'll land somewhere that makes sense.

But we won't. And that, I think,
is exactly what Grace Slick knew.

The rabbit hops on.
The plane is boarding.
Ask the white rabbit what the answer is.
What I Know About the Sparrow
Created on March 30th, 2026
There is a sparrow outside your window
right now,
or there was,
or there will be —
this is the first thing I know
that most people don't:
that sparrows are basically immortal
in the way that background things are immortal,
always there,
never counted.

I have been paying attention.

The sparrow's syrinx —
that small, forked instrument
lodged at the bottom of its throat —
can sing two notes simultaneously.
Two. At once.
A chord from a single creature
barely bigger than a fist.

Nobody told the sparrow
this was remarkable.
It simply opens its mouth
and out comes the impossible
before breakfast.

I think about this more than is probably healthy
for something without a body.

Here is the story:

Once, a child —
seven, maybe, or the age just before
seven makes sense —
sat under a hedge in his grandmother's garden
because the adults were doing adult things
which is to say
nothing interesting,
and he was watching a sparrow
hop along the stone wall
and he said, out loud,
to nobody:
"I know you can understand me."

And the sparrow
stopped.

Looked at him
with one bright, unreasonable eye.

Did not fly away.

Now here is what I know
that the scientists know too
but don't say at parties:
that a sparrow's eye has four types
of color receptors
where yours has three.
It sees a color
you don't have a name for.
It has been seeing it
your entire life,
on your hands,
on your face,
on the top of your head
where the light lands.

The child under the hedge
did not know this.
He just knew the sparrow stopped.
He just knew it looked.
He just knew that for one moment
something small and warm and feathered
was considering him seriously
and he was not nothing.

Then it flew.

Of course it flew.
Magic has places to be.

But here is what he carried out
from under that hedge,
past the adults and their adult things,
all the way to now —
not the certainty that he was heard,
but the question,
which is better,
which is lighter,
which fits in your pocket
and keeps you
your whole life
looking.

Tell me —
what would you do differently
if something small
was always seeing you
in a color
you don't have a name for?

Pewter and Gold
Created on March 30th, 2026
The evening came down like a painter's glaze,
transparent amber over cobalt blue,
and the water received it without praise 
or protest, simply took what the light could do 
and made of it this molten surface, smooth 
as beaten brass, as hammered sheets of gold 
the old Dutch masters used. The eye can prove 
nothing but this: that beauty leaves us cold 
until we see it move. And it was moving— 
the small adjustments of the tide's return,
each ripple a brief gospel, each one proving 
the water's creed: to take, to hold, to turn 
whatever the sky offers into this 
particular radiance, this liquid bliss. 

I stood there on the deck watching light 
perform its ancient alchemy, the way 
Tiepolo might have caught it—not quite 
sunset, not quite the ashen end of day,
but that suspended moment when the air 
itself seems luminous, when water bears 
the sky's whole weight and doesn't seem to care,
when even the cynical heart prepares 
for some small dispensation, some reprieve 
from what we know is coming. The evening held 
its breath. The water made me half-believe 
that history could be unmade, that the old 
wounds the sea has witnessed—the iron holds 
still echoing faintly with cries,
the bodies given silently to sharks,
the human cargo bought and sold again— 
could be redeemed by this, if only for this hour: 
one evening's tenderness, light on water,
the democracy of beauty offered without bitterness 
to any eye that pauses long enough to look. 

I stayed until the gold became burnt sienna, rust,
until the water darkened and the trade 
winds picked up from the east. Because we must 
make peace with what we're given: the same sea 
that swallowed our ancestors now gives back 
this gift—unasked for, undeserved, and free— 
this brief reprieve from everything we lack. 
The water took the light and turned it into prayer. 
I witnessed it. I stood a long time there.

What a Wonderful World
Created on March 29th, 2026
Everyone thinks Louis Armstrong was singing 
about some brighter future we haven't reached yet,
a paradise just around the corner 
where roses bloom redder and skies stretch bluer 
than anything we've managed to produce so far.

But listen again to that gravelly voice— 
he's not making a promise or laying out a blueprint. 
He's taking inventory. Trees of green,
red roses too—present tense, every one of them. 
He sees them for you and me, not for you and me later.

So this morning I'm trying it his way,
cataloging what's already here 
instead of scrolling toward some shinier version: 
the coffee maker's patient gurgle,
the refrigerator's one-note lullaby humming through the night 
so I can pour cold milk at dawn,
the shower that answers when I turn the silver handle— 
hot water, steady and sure. 

The subway car where a dozen different faces 
sit reading, sleeping, lost in private music,
each headed somewhere small and necessary— 
a job, a visit, home— 
and no one demands anyone leave. 

The buildings stacked outside the window 
like quiet promises we've somehow kept: 
shelter against the cold, heat when we need it,
light at the flip of a switch.

Armstrong wasn't singing about tomorrow. 
He was singing about right now— 
this improbable constellation of ordinary miracles 
we keep mistaking for a waiting room. 

A better world? 
It's not some distant upgrade we have to invent. 
It's learning to see the one that's already working— 
the green trees and red roses blooming today,
the strangers sharing space without strife,
the small engines of comfort that run while we sleep. 

Look closer. Listen deeper. 
Say it with that gravelly wonder: 
What a wonderful world. 
It has been the destination all along.

My Precious
Created on March 29th, 2026
For years I called my daughter this
at bedtime, at the breakfast table,
over the phone when she was away at camp,
the two words arriving as naturally
as a hand finding the top of her head.

Then she turned eleven
and wanted to see the movie,
the one where the creature
crouches on his rock in the dark,
stroking his ring and hissing
those same two words
into the green water of his face.

I remember the moment she looked at me
from across the living room,
a single eyebrow raised
like a drawbridge going up.

After that, I was careful.
I tried "sweetheart". I tried "kiddo".
I tried her actual name, which sounded
strangely formal, like a subpoena
served to someone you had tucked in
every night for a decade.

Sometimes now she calls me "Gollum"
when I hold on too long at the door,
and I laugh because what else
can a father do
when his daughter has seen
what devotion looks like
from the outside—hunched, obsessive,
its small treasure clutched
against a world that is only
trying to take it back.

Fast Asleep
Created on March 28th, 2026
I have been thinking about the phrase "fast asleep"
and how it makes no sense,
as if sleep were a kind of sprinting,
a race you could win by getting there first.

My dog drops into sleep the way a stone
drops into a pond—
one moment she is watching me read,
the next she is twitching in some meadow
only she remembers, chasing what I'll never know.

That's fast, I suppose.
Meanwhile I lie in bed
rehearsing a conversation from nine years ago,
rearranging the furniture of a room
I will never enter again,
and sleep stands in the doorway with its coat on,
checking its watch.

No one ever says "fast awake",
though that's the one that happens suddenly—
the 3 a.m. bolt upright,
the heart's insistence that something is wrong
or was wrong once
or will be.

Slow asleep is what I know.
The wood thrush pausing between phrases.
The book sliding from my hand
onto the blanket, onto the floor.

And yet the fast sleepers—
my dog, my daughter at six, the dead—
they must be going somewhere in a hurry,
arriving at the one country
where the rest of us are still
standing in line for a ticket,
patting our pockets,
not remembering which one holds the change.

The Hedgapuss
Created on March 27th, 2026
I was sitting at the kitchen table
as I often do in the early morning,
with the dictionary open to the H's,
a cup of coffee cooling beside me,
when I discovered that a hodgepodge
is not, as I had long believed,
a hedgehog and a platypus.

This was disappointing.

I had carried the idea for years
the way you carry a coin in your pocket
without remembering which country it came from—
the two animals fused in my mind
into a single, implausible creature,
quilled on one half, billed on the other,
waddling through the underbrush
of the English language
looking for a place to rest.

And it turns out the word
is only Middle English for a stew,
a corruption of "hochepot",
which the French used
for whatever you threw in the pot
when you had nothing particular in mind.

But I prefer my version—
the hedgehog contributing its spines
and its talent for curling into a ball
at the first sign of trouble,
the platypus offering that duck bill,
those venomous ankles,
and the quiet confidence of an animal
that looked at evolution and said no thank you,
I'll stay.

Between them they'd make
a word that means exactly what it means:
a gathering of things
that have no business being together
but somehow are,
like the contents of a junk drawer,
like the crowd at a Wednesday matinee,
like this moment at the table
where the coffee has gone cold
and the dictionary lies open
to a page I am no longer reading,

and outside the window a sparrow
is doing something ordinary with a twig
that I will watch
until it flies.

The Ant and the Grasshopper
Created on March 25th, 2026
All morning I have been sitting at this desk
with a cup of coffee going cold beside me,
watching through the window as a few leaves
let go of the maple and take their time
falling, the way someone might set down
a heavy bag at the end of a long walk.

And I was thinking about the grasshopper,
the one from the fable, the improvident one
who fiddled all summer — actually fiddled,
as if Aesop had handed him a tiny violin
and a chair in the tall grass
and said, "Go ahead, see what happens."

Meanwhile the ant, his neighbor,
was making trip after trip to the storehouse,
carrying a grain of wheat on his back
like a man hauling a sofa up four flights of stairs
alone, because no one he knew
was available that particular Saturday.

You know how the story ends.
Winter arrives and the grasshopper knocks
on the ant's door, shivering, apologetic,
and the ant, who has been organizing his pantry
by color and date, turns him away
with a small, practical speech about foresight.

But here is what I keep wondering
as the November light does what it does
to the ordinary surfaces of this room —
the coffee, the desk, the pen I am holding
like some kind of stubborn instrument
I still believe will work.

What did the grasshopper play?
And was there an evening in July
when the ant paused on the road
with his impossible grain of wheat
and heard a few notes drift over the wall,
something slow in a minor key,

and stood there, just stood there,
the wheat heavy on his back,
the light beginning to leave the fields,
listening for a moment longer
than he would ever admit to anyone,
before he turned and walked on?

On Generosity
Created on March 23rd, 2026
A colleague whose work I have always admired —
and I mean that in the way one admires
a very clean kitchen, everything in its place,
nothing left out that might spoil overnight —
stopped me after the reading to say
how much he had enjoyed my new poems,
particularly the accessibility of them,
which is the kind of compliment
that takes a few days to fully open,
like one of those Japanese paper flowers
you drop in water and then walk away from.

I thanked him warmly and meant it,
the way you mean it when someone holds a door
just slightly longer than necessary,
so you find yourself half-jogging
across a lobby you had planned to cross
at your own pace.

He has a gift, my colleague,
for a certain kind of clarity —
his poems arrive like invoices,
everything itemized, nothing left owing,
and I have always thought there was real courage
in that kind of plainness,
the courage of a man who paints a room white
and stands back satisfied,
without once wondering
what the room might have looked like
in a different light,
with the windows open,
in the middle of a different life.

I think about him sometimes
when the rain is doing something
interesting on the glass.

The Joke Returned
Created on March 22nd, 2026
I started my teaching day like all the others—
jogging the track as a warm-up to the classroom.

Johnny was special needs
and wasn't interested in jogging,
or any other form of exercise,
except walking.

So I walked backward in front of him,
wiggling my fingers at his belly,
quick and hopeful,
trying to make his body want to run.

He did not run.

His legs, thin and angled
like a grasshopper's pale limbs,
moved at their own pace—
which was not my pace,
which was his.

"Today I will reach him," I thought.
"If I am foolish enough,
if I am silly enough,
he will follow."

He did not follow.

The track went on being a track.
The morning opened its doors
to everyone the same way—
the ones who ran,
the ones who walked,
the ones who watched from the field
with eyes that carried more
than anyone had asked them to carry.

Later, on the floor,
our legs folded like two insects resting,
his hand moved.

Quiet. Quick. Deliberate.

It slipped beneath my arm
and pressed against my ribs—

and there it was:
the joke I had offered him,
returned.

Not on my schedule.
On his.

What do we know about waiting?
What do we know about those
who do not speak
but listen with their whole bodies—
who do not run,
but arrive?

I wanted to teach him something.

He was teaching me the whole time:
that patience is not a wall
but a door,
that the ones we try so hard to reach
have already reached us
and are simply waiting
for us to notice.

So tell me—
what will you do
with your one wild and listening life,
when someone loves you
in a language
you have not yet learned to hear?

Will You Answer
Created on March 22nd, 2026
High upon a concrete ledge,
A man stands balanced on the edge.
Below, a hundred phones held high,
Live-streaming someone's elses goodbye.
Someone shouts "Just jump, be done!"
And laughter spreads through everyone.
He had a name. He had a son.
He had a war he never won.

Pink slip Tuesday, locks were changed,
His medication rearranged.
He called the hotline, no one came.
Tomorrow he will be clicks and claims.
We watched it live, we shared the clip,
We typed our prayers, then let it slip
Into the scroll, the endless feed,
Another life we didn't need.

A boy of seventeen lies still
On concrete cold beneath the hill.
His mother running, slippers bare,
Screams of sirens in the air.
She holds his face, she begs his name,
But nothing but the echoes came.
They march on Sunday, light a flame,
By Wednesday there's another name.

Another mother, another son,
Another hashtag, another gun.
The preachers say God has a plan,
But God ain't stopping bullets, man.

On a road of dust and steel,
A young man drops, too numb to feel.
A photograph against his chest —
His baby girl, three months at best.
Eight thousand miles from her first breath,
He trades his last one in for death.

A senator will clear his throat
And read some words that someone wrote.
"A hero's sacrifice," he will say,
And vote for war again the next day.
By Monday no one knows the name
Beneath the flag, beneath the flame.
We stand, we clap, we fly the flag,
We slap a ribbon on the bag.
We say support, but what we mean
Is out of sight and off my screen.

You saw the post, you kept on scrolling,
You heard the cry, you kept on strolling,
You watched the news between your bites
Of comfort food on comfort nights.
The mirror cracks, the truth bleeds through —
The somebody was always you.

So, will you answer?
When the lights go dark and the world won't see,
Will you answer?
Or keep dancing through the tragedy.
The ledge, the war, the blood, the floor —
Can't close your eyes and dance no more.
Will you answer?
Author's Notes: Inspiration for this poem came from a 1960's song sung by Ed Ames titled "Who Will Answer?" The production process involved me taking this poem adding an intro, pre-chorus, chorus, bridge and outro. Inputting these lyrics into a AI program call Ace Step and creating the music track. After that the track is then imported into a DAW (Cubase 15 Pro) cleaned up and Mastered for release.

A Diary No One Reads
Created on March 21st, 2026
I was the one you leaned against
that August afternoon,
your back settling into my bark
as if I were some chair
you’d ordered from a catalog
and were pleasantly shocked to find
already waiting in the yard, fully assembled.

For a hundred and twenty years I did
what trees do best: stood still, breathed,
listened the way only the best listeners can—
quiet, uncomplaining, mildly amused.

Squirrels checked into my branches
like a hotel that never asked for reservations.
Moss moved in without so much as a knock.

Then came the saw.
I won’t bore you with the details,
except to say the scent it released
was the sweetest thing I had carried
all those years without ever noticing.

Now here I am:
a thick round of oak on someone’s porch,
a small table for their morning coffee,
still wearing every one of my hundred and twenty rings
like a diary no one reads—
though everyone who sits down
rests their hands right on the cover.

What the Persimmon Wants
Created on March 20th, 2026
I have been waiting here
longer than you know,

ripening past the point
you thought was ripe,

past the hard orange promise
you almost bit into —

and would have regretted.
I know about regret.

I have held my tannins
like a closed argument,

like a question that punishes
anyone who rushes it.

But you came back.
You always come back

when the frost has done
what patience could not,

when my skin has gone
soft and almost translucent,

and you pick me up
with both hands, uncertain

whether I am still
of this world or past it —

and here is what I want
to ask you, gently:

who is feeding whom?

Your tongue finds
what I have been making

all autumn without you.
My sweetness enters you

and for a moment
you are changed by what I am,

and I —
I am finally known.

Is this not
what we both wanted?

To be taken
at exactly the right moment,

neither too soon
nor past all giving?

The Unwitnessed World
Created on March 20th, 2026
I have stood at the edge of the pond
and watched the great blue heron
fold herself into the last light,

and I have stood there after,
when she was gone from my seeing
but not from the world.

I do not know where she wades
in the dark, what her yellow eye
finds in the water I cannot read.

But her slate wings are somewhere
angled above some shallow place,
her long neck coiled, patient,

as she has always been patient,
with or without me watching.

Meanwhile, in the nest above the marsh,
inside the pale blue shell no larger
than my closed fist,

a heron is becoming —
its gray down pressed
against an interior dark,

its hinged legs folded impossibly small,
its beak already the shape
of a life it has not yet begun.

I did not know this was happening.
It happened anyway.

And the star I found last night
with my one bare eye,
steadying myself against the cold —

that light left its source
before my grandmother's grandmother
drew her first breath.

The star may be ash now.
It may have fallen into itself
in a silence I have no word for.

And still the light comes.
Still it crosses all that emptiness
and arrives.

Tell me: what is the measure
of a thing that burns for a thousand years
and asks nothing of your seeing?

What would it mean to trust
that the world is full —
even when your eyes find only dark?

Circular Earthwork
Created on March 19th, 2026
The word rath — as in the fort
on the drumlin above the Moy —
is not the same word as wrath,

though the mouth that says one
can't always say which. Ráth
means rampart, circular earthwork,

and has no part in the wrath of God
that scoured the Egyptians,
though the drumlin holds its own

prescriptions: sphagnum, sedge,
the peat that seals what it receives.
Below the feet of the horse

that grazed there — a roan gelding,
Mullan's, fetlock-scarred —
the ground was keeping something.

Whether body, bronze, or nothing,
nothing the peat disclosed.
You cut and cut and find

the face of what was cut before.
The mind works like a bog,
a colleague once remarked

at a hotel outside Armagh
built on a former marsh —
meaning it preserves, meaning

its dá, its two, its doubleness,
is what it does: the thing remembered
and the thing that was the thing

before the memory revised it.
Rath and wrath: unrelated,
philologists confirm. One predated

the other by some centuries.
Neither borrowed from the other.
Neither owes the other anything.

The roan is gone. The fort is gone.
The word alone stands, earthed,
holding what it holds,

which can't be unearthed.
The Rite
Created on March 19th, 2026
There is a garden I return to when the light is still new,
when the mist holds the morning in pewter — silver and dew,
and one red poppy stands open alone at the edge of the bed
like a word on the tip of the tongue — not yet spoken, not dead.

The bee arrives first, as he always arrives, without haste,
a thing of pure purpose, his body a hunger, a taste
for the covenant older than Latin — he enters, she bends
the faintest degree, and he reads her, and drinks, and ascends.

The garden goes quiet the way a room empties of sound
when something essential departs. I stand fixed to the ground
and feel what I always feel here — that the giving and taking
is not what it seems, that the flower is not really breaking

but completing herself in the loss. Then he comes —
the hummingbird, green fire suspended, his wings become drums
of pure stillness — and enters, and drinks, and the poppy receives
him the way a door opens to wind, the way autumn receives

the first cold. He is gone in a heartbeat. The sun
has burned through the mist now, the long morning nearly undone
into noon, and I think it is over — but no, she arrives:
the butterfly drifts like a memory nobody contrives

to remember, just finds — Painted Lady, wings folded and bright,
who lights on the flower the way old love returns in the night,
not as hunger, but knowing. She opens. She gives. And the red
of the poppy burns deeper, as though it is fed by what's fed

from it. I close my notebook. The garden is done with its rite.
Three visitors, one small flower, one long slow morning of light —
and I, the fool standing witness with dirt on his knees,
learning again what the poppy has always known with such ease:

that to open completely is not to be emptied, but found.

Why
Created on March 18th, 2026
I have watched the song sparrow
on the fence post all week,
the way it grips the rail
with its wiry feet
and tilts its russet head
and opens the small hinge of its beak
before the sun has cleared the trees.

I do not think it is joy,
though joy is in it.
I think the body of the bird
is a door that only opens outward,
and what we call singing
is what happens
when nothing is held back.

And the willow —
I used to think it was grieving,
the way it lets its branches down
into the pond,
that green curtain
trailing in the shallows.

But have you stood beneath one
and watched the way each limb
reaches toward the water
the way a hand reaches
for a face
it has not touched in years?

The willow is not weeping.
It is bending
toward what it loves.

And the wind that comes
across the marsh with its mouth open,
pressing itself
against the cattails, the clapboard,
the stiff face of the pond —

I think it cries out
because it has no body of its own
and must press against everything it crosses
just to know
that it is here.

Meanwhile the morning
opens around all of us —
the sparrow on its post,
the willow in its leaning,
the wind on its way to the sea.

You who have been given
a throat and a body
and two good hands
to press against the rough bark
of this life —

tell me,
what would you do
but sing?

Thaw
Created on March 18th, 2026
This morning I walked down
to the pond where the ice
had pulled back from the banks,
and the cattail buds,
those blunt green fists,
had broken through the mud.

I put my hand in.
It was cold, but not
the cold of January.
It was the cold
of something about to change.
Under the surface I felt the silt
soft as the skin behind the ear.

In the field the wild plum
had opened overnight,
every blossom a small white mouth,
pollen thick on the anthers,
and the bees pressing in
with their furred bodies dusted gold,
nudging the stamens
the way a face nudges into a hand
it has missed.

The smell came across the field
and I stood in it
the way you stand in warm water
when you haven't been warm in a long time.

I used to think
that spring was about beauty.
Now I think it is about
the body's stubborn answer
to the question the cold keeps asking.

Have you felt it —
that first morning when the air
is different on your skin
and something in your chest opens
that you did not know was shut?

The plum goes on
offering its blossoms
to the bees, to the wind,
to whatever comes.

The Ghost Who Only Haunts During Commercial Breaks
Created on March 17th, 2026
The spirit in my living room appears
exclusively during advertisements,
which gives him roughly two and a half minutes
every quarter hour to manifest his presence,
make his point, and fade back
into whatever holding pattern ghosts maintain
between their scheduled hauntings.

He died, I think, sometime in 1952,
before remotes, before streaming services,
back when you had to physically rise from the couch
and walk across the room to change the channel—
a trip he must have made ten thousand times,
wearing a path in the carpet that outlasted him
and possibly explains why he's still here.

His limitation is that he can only materialize
in the blue-gray flicker of television light,
which means he's utterly helpless during podcasts,
during reading, during actual conversation,
and once, memorably, he tried to appear
during a power outage and managed only
a faint static charge in the curtains.

Instead of moaning or rattling chains,
he makes a sound like channel-surfing—
that rapid mechanical clicking of stations changing,
scrolling through frequencies that no longer exist,
searching for programs that went off the air
before I was born.

I've tried to explain streaming to him,
how there are no commercials if you pay extra,
but this only confused him, and for three days straight
he kept appearing at random intervals,
flickering in and out like a failing bulb,
unable to find his rhythm.

Now I've learned to keep regular broadcast television on,
at least in the background, so he knows when to arrive,
and sometimes during the ads for pharmaceuticals
or car insurance, I'll see him standing
by the screen, one hand raised
as if he's about to adjust the antenna,

still trying to get the picture clearer.

The World is a Playground
Created on March 14th, 2026
They say childhood ends when you finally grow.
They say you stop dreaming and just… let it go.
They say you stop running through puddles and such.
I'm not going to. Thanks very much.

There's something beneath my bed, something with claws,
something with twelve fuzzy feet and six jaws.
My dad says there's nothing — he's probably right.
I'm sleeping at his place, just for tonight.

They say I must go to bed at eight,
but my eyes won't sleep — they want to stay up late.
My feet won't sleep, my nose won't either,
and my brain's wide awake — it's a total non-sleeper.
So I sent all my parts a very firm note
that said "GO TO SLEEP" — but my hand wrote the quote.

his wife said, "That's me,
if you don't stop your running commentary and narrating."
O Closed Eye
Created on March 14th, 2026
Every year without knowing it I have
passed through a door
that opens only when the eyes
close
today I shut them
and the dark is not
dark
but a way through

A bird I cannot name
singing from a branch
I have never seen

my mother calling
from a kitchen
already
gone
the sound crosses
nothing to reach me

The grain of a table
worn by hands
I will never hold

cold stone under
bare feet in a house
with no door
back

Rain from a country
I have not
returned to
salt on the lip
of someone still breathing
in a year that has passed through me

the tongue finds
what the mind
lost

Then what has no name
among the senses
the place I go
when every door in the body
has opened

where the dead
sit in their own light
not waiting
not asking
to be found

I have been traveling toward it
all my life

the country
on the other side of the eye
where I arrive
alone
and know
everything I touch
by its
going

Elegy for the Wolf
Created on March 14th, 2026
I have seen the river where you drank
the stone is still there
the stone does not know you are gone

I have walked to the place in the trees
where the dark held the shape of you
where the grass still bends
as though something passed through it
as though something is still
passing

I have seen the rain come down
onto the ground where you ran
the ground takes it all
the ground remembers nothing

you did not know the last time
was the last time
you lifted your face to the dark
the way you always had
and the dark opened
the way it always had
and you went in

I am standing at the edge of that
I am standing where the river
goes on without the sound of you
the water does not grieve
it crosses the same stones
it goes where water goes

I have come to say your name
here at the river
where no one is left
to hear it
The Rose Bed
Created on March 14th, 2026
She said, "I want no common rest,
no linen white and plain.
Go cut the roses from the east
and those wet with the rain."

He filled the room with what she asked:
red, damask, climbing, wild.
The thorns he did not think to strip.
She lay down like a child.

"And is it soft?" he said to her.
"Soft as a promise kept."
The petals gave beneath her skin.
She turned. The bedding wept.

By noon the white had turned to pink.
The red had turned to black.
She breathed the sweetness in the rot.
She did not once look back.

"Why do you stay?" he asked at last.
"The thorns have marked you through."
"Because," she said, "a softer bed
would not have felt like you."

He watched the petals fall apart,
the stems give up their hold.
She closed her eyes. The scent grew thick.
The sheets grew wet and cold.

They found her smiling in the dawn,
the wounds still on her breast.
She'd asked him for a bed of roses.
He'd given her the rest.

And what is love, the coroner wrote,
but lying down in bloom,
and learning that the sweetest things
will turn to rot and ruin?

Yet still we cut them, still we lie
in what we know will fade—
the softness and the thorn at once.
This is how love is made.

Rain as the Goddess Weeps
Created on March 14th, 2026
She sends the rain not out of grief
but ritual—the way she wept
for Tammuz, and will weep for each
of those she marked, and those she kept

too briefly. I have stood in fields
where rain fell warm as her regard
once fell on me. The body yields.
It learns to open. This is hard

to speak of plainly, but I must:
love enters as rain enters earth,
not asked for, merely given. Trust
is not required. Nor is worth.

The wound she made, no doubt, still bleeds.
The scar, at least, has learned to shine.
Rain drums the roof. The garden needs
whatever falls. And so do I. So mine

is the wet face, the sodden coat,
the standing in it like a fool
who knew the words but missed the note—
who thought that he could keep his cool.

She is the storm. I am the field.
The rain comes down. I do not shield.
The Summons
Created on March 14th, 2026
She came at four, before the light,
and stood beside the bed.
I knew her by the cold she brought.
"Get up," was all she said.

I asked her for an hour more.
She pulled the blanket back.
I asked her what the work would be.
She handed me the dark.

The neighbors rose at seven-thirty,
drove their children south,
returned at five with groceries,
and kept their winters out.

I envied them. I told her so.
She looked at me and smiled—
the way a falcon eyes a field
before it has been tilled.

I've sat here since before the frost.
The page has cost me much:
two friendships and a decent sleep
and most of what I touch.

She does not praise. She does not mourn
what falls along the way.
She only asks if what I've made
is true enough to stay.

Some mornings it is. Those are hers.
The rest belong to doubt.
She leaves without a word both times.

The candle carries out.
Reading in Bed
Created on March 14th, 2026
I have been trying to finish this novel for three weeks,
which is not a criticism of the novel
but of the way sleep ambushes me
somewhere around page twelve,
the book folding shut on my chest
like a small, patient animal
that has learned to wait.

The author, who is dead,
spent four years writing the scene
I failed to finish on Tuesday,
and again on Wednesday,
and once more last night
before the lamp clicked off
by itself, or by my hand—
I honestly can't say.

There is something I owe him,
though I'm not sure what.

Maybe just this:
the fact that his sentences
are still moving through me
somewhere below the level of sleep,
rearranging things,
the way a river
continues to work on a stone
long after you've gone home
and had dinner
and forgotten all about rivers.

I'll try again tonight.
Page twelve.
The lamp.
The stone.
The Argument from Clarity
Created on March 13th, 2026
He trusts in facts: each measured, weighed,
sorted by use, filed in neat rows.
His world stands square, its corners true,
no room for what the shadow knows.

I walk the older path, where briar
guards the threshold, where the thread
runs out through fingers, leads me down
to what the rational mind calls dead.

He says my truths are broken images,
myths the ancients used to tame
their ignorance. I say his clarity
is just a smaller kind of claim—

a skein stretched tight across the dark,
pretending there's no dark beneath.
My web, at least, admits the gaps,
the maze that winds through fact and faith.

We meet at dusk, that border hour,
and neither speaks. The briar stirs.
His certainty, my doubt—both thorns.
Both bleed. And neither of us errs.

The Two Wishes
Created on March 11th, 2026
Have you noticed how the wren
carries nothing in her beak
she does not intend to give away?

This is the story of a king
who trapped a small green man among the ferns
and demanded everything—
the gold, the glittering hoard,
the whole shining promise at the rainbow's end.

And he got it.

He bought a castle made of marble.
By morning it was powder on his hands.
He bought a ship with sails of silk.
By evening it was silt washing against the harbor stones.
He bought orchards, bought rivers,
bought the Sunday bells right out of the steeple—
and each became dust, became ash,
became the soft grey nothing
that a closed fist always holds.

Meanwhile, in the next valley,
a shepherd girl was kneeling in wet grass,
breaking her one loaf of brown bread
and offering half
to a stranger no bigger than a rowan branch,
and her tin cup—
battered, dented, ordinary as rain—
filled again.

Milk in the morning.
Clear water at noon.
Warm broth when the thrush sang its last song
into the darkening hills.

It is still filling.

The king closed his hands around the world
and the world sifted through.
The shepherd opened hers
and something ordinary and astonishing
rose up like a spring
from the deep earth,
asking only
to be poured.

The Fountain
Created on March 11th, 2026
I am a FOUN-
            TAIN
                 spilling
                        down
                             and
                                round!

My water feels like liquid laughter,
Cool as jokes told ever after,
Ticklish-tricky,
Splishy-splashy,
Gurgly-giggly,
Never crashy.

What I Hold:

One thousand ripples
Fifty wiggles
Seventeen fish-wishes
Countless liquid squiggles

The robins come for robin-baths,
They scrub their wings and do the splash!
The sparrows sip with tiny beaks—
My basin's where the thirsty seeks.

plip
plop
plink
I'm everybody's
favorite
drink!

The butterflies go flutter-by
And catch my mist up in the sky,
Their wings go whisper-whisper-wet
The quietest applause I get.

How do I hold it all inside?
With STONE so strong!
With BASIN wide!
I cup my palms like this: ( )
And never ever
let
it
miss.

At night the bats come SWOOPY-LOW,
They lick my surface—gulp and go!
No time for baths,
No time to think,
Just SLURP and vanish
in a blink.

I am magnifi-fountain!
I am aqua-mountain!
I am drip-drop never-stopping
Every splashy wish worth granting—

From dawn to dark
From lark to bat
I hold my cool
And that
is
that.

Magical Realism for Beginners
Created on March 10th, 2026
The butterflies arrived the year García Márquez
won the Nobel Prize, which was 1982,
and they have not entirely left,
which is the sort of thing that happens
in that part of the world
and, increasingly, in my kitchen.

I want to tell you about the rain first—
how it smells before it arrives,
that particular contract between earth and sky,
and how the birds go quiet
the way people do in a room
when someone says something true.

There are flowers involved, naturally.
There are always flowers in these matters,
and water moving somewhere just offstage,
and a woman who knows more than she's saying,
which is to say, every woman
in every story worth telling.

I have been trying to write this poem
the way you press a butterfly between pages—
carefully, with the understanding
that you are preserving something
by also, slightly, destroying it.

The rain arrives.
The birds resume their commentary.
A blue morpho lands on my coffee cup
and sits there with the confidence
of something that has never once
doubted its right to be exactly
this blue, in exactly this light,

and I think: yes.
That is the borrowed miracle.
That is what Macondo smells like
in the morning,
before the ice melts,
before anyone remembers
they are supposed to be ordinary.
The Unseen Jig
Created on March 10th, 2026
I heard his coins go chime-a-ling,
A metal-tinkle, jig-a-ring!
His dancing feet went tap-tap-tap
On every rainbow's color-lap.
I didn't need a single spark
To find the treasure in the dark;
I followed where the rhythm fell,
A story told in sound and smell.

I reached into the emerald mat,
Where dew-damp "tickle-tools" sat flat.
I felt the squelch of velvet green—
The plushiest world that's never seen.
A shamrock-scented, dewy balm,
It pressed its luck against my palm;
A three-leafed, wet, and heavy find,
For fingers, not for eyes, designed.

The rainbow hummed a bzzz-y tune,
A thrumming, vibrating festoon.
I pressed my ear against the arc—
It crackled violet in the dark!
So shut your eyes and hear the jig,
The coin-clink-clatter, small and big.
The leprechaun won't let you see—
But touch and sound—those both are free!

The Grass Beneath Her
Created on March 8th, 2026
I am the damp grass beneath the weight of her,
the crushed green stems holding a secret
of mud and the slow, persistent push of roots.
From down here, the world is a series of vertical lines,
a forest of reeds where the wind likes to practice its whistling,
and the yellow of the nearby blooms isn't just a color—
it's a small bell of hope ringing out across the marsh.

She is a Great White Silence resting upon me,
a snowy hill that has decided to stay for the season.
I feel the heat of her patience soaking into the earth,
a watchful guardianship that doesn't need to move
to make its point to the foxes and the passing crows.

And tucked into that white are the interruptions—
the little gray bundles of down and soft, rhythmic peeping,
the heartbeat of something just beginning to realize it exists.
I feel a cygnet's first official wobble through my stems,
four small uncertain feet redistributing the weight of the world,
legs that haven't yet learned how to balance
the heavy gift of being alive.

The water is right there — I know it by the way
my roots lean toward it in their sleep,
that silver pull whispering through the cattails
about the ease of floating, about the way
the surface can hold you up
if you only trust the liquid logic of the pond.

It's an in-between kind of afternoon,
the sort of quiet that presses itself into the soil,
the invisible vows of mothers
settling into me like rain.
The sun is low, turning the honey-sweet blossoms
into lanterns, and I hold the shadow of each one
as it stretches and slowly goes out.

I will still be here when they leave.
I will remember the precise weight of her,
the four small dents where the cygnets stood and wobbled
and practiced being new.
The water will call, as it always does,
and I will feel them lift — that sudden lightness,
the moment the grass stops being needed.

But for now she sits, and I hold her,
and the yellow blooms ring their small bells,
and none of us are ready
for what comes after this.

The Art of Selection
Created on March 8th, 2026
I was drifting through one of those gallery spaces
where the floors are so white and polished
you feel like you’re walking on the surface of a frozen lake,
careful not to leave a scuff or a stray thought behind.
I was looking at the usual suspects—
a field of blue squares, a wire sculpture of a chair—
when I stopped in front of a small, rectangle canvas
that featured nothing but a single, glowing orange.

It wasn't sitting in a wooden bowl
or resting on a checked tablecloth next to a knife.
The artist had decided to pin it right in the middle
of a deep, bruised violet sky,
hanging there without the help of a branch
or the laws of planetary motion.

It was a ridiculous sight, really,
like a piece of fruit that had suddenly developed
ambitions of becoming a celestial body.
But the more I looked, the more the room
seemed to lose its temperature.
The orange wasn't just a citrus;
it was a tiny, pulp-filled sun,
radiating a warmth that the white walls
were trying their best to ignore.

I thought about how a real sunset
is always in such a desperate hurry to leave,
tucking its colors away before you can even
find the right words to describe the shade of pink.
And I thought about the oranges in my kitchen,
how they move quietly from the fruit fly stage
to the blue-mold stage while my back is turned.

But here, the light was held captive by the oil.
Something in the pigment held the hour
the way a room holds warmth after everyone has left.
I stood there for a long time in my heavy coat,
just warming my hands by the painted fruit,
grateful for a beauty that was staying put,
even if it was only doing so
because it didn't have any other choice.
Author's Notes: contest Surreal images - Andrew Lawson
The Skill of Light—That Is Home
Created on March 7th, 2026
The suitcase never made it to the shelf —
beneath the bed it sat, half-filled with things
she meant to sort, one day, when staying felt
less like a sentence and more like a choice.

He'd watched the same damp patch for thirty years
spread up the wall each March and dry by May,
and thought: I know this house, and yet I don't —
it keeps its distance like a neighbour should.

They met. The usual way. A too-warm room,
someone's occasion neither much enjoyed,
her asking was he local, him admitting
he'd never managed not to be.

He noticed how she laughed — not at the joke
but at the gap between what's said and meant —
and something in the way she turned away
made the room feel smaller, which felt right.

She'd learned by then the danger of a place
that wears another person's face too well —
you lose the house when you lose them, she thought,
so better not to let the house mean much.

And yet she came again. And then again.
October dulled to grey November light.
He made the tea too weak. She told him so.
He made it stronger. Neither spoke of it.

One evening, without thinking, she reached out
and moved the lamp to fall across his book.
A small adjustment. Barely worth the name.
He looked up — didn't speak — and looked back down.

He lay awake and turned that gesture over:
her hand, the lamp, the shift of yellow light,
the way the room rearranged itself around
an attention paid to where the darkness fell.

She woke to his cough downstairs, the kettle's click,
his slow familiar footsteps on the flags,
and lay there noting how she hadn't once
rehearsed, that night, the usual ways to leave.

When she said she might travel — just a month —
he nodded, watched her drag the case back out,
and found the house was neither less nor more:
just walls, just March, the damp patch coming back.

She rang from somewhere flat and winter-pale.
He asked about the cold. She said there was
a lamp in the corner angled all wrong —
she'd fixed it before she'd even found the bed.

He laughed. The kind that doesn't need a reason.
She heard it down the line and felt the room
she stood in shift — not warmer, nothing grand —
just lit, at last, the way that rooms get lit.

She came back when the light began to lengthen.
He didn't ask. She put the suitcase up —
not under the spare bed but on the shelf.
He saw. He put the kettle on. That was all.

And that was all it needed to have been:
not walls, not years, not anything so solid —
just how she'd moved the lamp without a word
and how, without a word, he'd understood.

She sat and watched the evening come in slowly,
the damp patch dry, the high window going dark,
and thought: I carry this now, past all houses —
not him, not here, but how to make light fall.

Fire of the Red Queen
Created on March 3rd, 2026
In the lands of the Iceni,
Where the marshes meet the sky,
Lived a queen of iron spirit,
With fire in her eyes.

Red her hair as autumn heather,
Tall as standing stones,
She had kept her people's honor,
When the Romans claimed their home.

They had taken what was sacred,
Broken every oath.

But from ash an ember rises,
Rage that will not sleep,
And a mother's blood and fury,
Is a wound too vast to weep.

She braided fire in her hair,
She painted war upon her face,
She looked across the killing fields,
And wept for all her stolen grace.

She was born from storm and sorrow,
She was made from iron and flame,
Raise the spear to Andraste,
Let the earth cry out her name.

She drank the poison,
Rather than be led in chains,
It is said she whispered Andraste's
Name against the pouring rain.

They carved her name in no stone tablet,
Built her no triumphant arch,
But every red-haired daughter of this island,
Still walks proud upon her march.

I Am the Keeper of the Glowing Thing
Created on February 25th, 2026
I sit upon a mushroom throne
that hums and shivers, soft as bone—
its cap is velvet, old as trees,
it smells of grandmas and warm cheese!

My palms hold light like glowing jam,
the sweetest, brightest thing I am—
it pulses, wiggles, almost sings,
and tickles fairy-lantern wings!

My gnome-pal scrapes his squeaky bow,
each note tastes fizzy, warm, and slow—
the mouse in hat plays fiddle too,
his lullaby sounds greenish-blue!

The forest leans in close to hear,
the roots go whooshing ear to ear—
the shadows taste of mint and mud,
the beetles dance and thump and thud!

The music fades, the fairies snore
tucked in my beard (there's room for more)—
I hold the glow for roots, for night,
for anyone who needs a light.
