Mid-Leap
Created on February 24th, 2026
He hammers shoes from dusk to dawn,
then sets his hammer-rhythms on
his heels—and does the sean-nós-stomp
through clover-pots, stone, and sun-streaked air—
the bog and swamp? His spirit’s there!

His foot lifts up—
will it come down?
He beckons with his toe at town.
A clover’s stuck between his lace,
spreading luck all over the place!

The craobh goes TWEEDLE-
EEDLE-EE,
the stones remember, so does he—
each step the same as hammered nails:
tap-tap-tap where the music spins!

His súil goes wide,
his súil goes bright,
he’s dancing all the old ones’ night—
and when his foot comes down at last,
the ground remembers every blast!

And from the arch—black as a púca’s grin—
a shadow taps its foot... and joins the din.

So It Goes with Frosting
Created on February 23rd, 2026
Dohanos watched him, standing close,
The flour, the sweat, the practiced dose,
A man who piped what war could not—
The beautiful, the calm, the got.

Gus Volkening's hands made a ring
Of ivory sugar, offering
What governments forgot to give:
A reason, sweet and white, to live.

Pvt. Stall wore olive drab,
The color of a world gone mad,
They handed him this frosted all—
Nice, nice. So very nice, Pvt. Stall.

June '45. The guns went mute.
The ovens hummed their warm salute.
In Westport, Connecticut, the support
Was sugar. And it was. Of a sort.

Witch Way did she Go?
Created on February 22nd, 2026
"Which way did she go?" asked the troll with the squint,
His eyebrow as bushy as bark on a flint.
"She zoomed past like thunder on tires of oak!"
And left a big cloud of frog-scented smoke.

"The WITCH way!" his partner cried, pointing his claw,
At chrome-plated cauldron the fastest they saw.
Her broomstick had handlebars, fuzzy and green,
The wildest of witches they'd ever had seen!

She hollered at treetops, she honked at the moon,
Her potion jars rattling a clattering tune.
The frogs in her saddlebag croaked out a song,
While mushrooms and moonbeams went bouncing along.

The trolls stood there gaping, their mouths open wide,
As glitter and bat-wings flew out from each side.
"I waved UP," said the first troll, "but she flew right past."
"Wave DOWN," said the second, "or we'll be her repast!"

Before Anyone Told You About the Sun
Created on February 21st, 2026
Let me tell you something about mornings,
and how I used to think the light arrived
like a gift left on the doorstep—
something you didn't ask for
but were glad to find.

I was wrong about that.

Consider the 9th-grade high school hallway,
which is its own kind of weather,
the lockers like closed faces,
the floor a long uncertainty
you have to cross every single day
wearing your actual body,
aware that older kids own the walls
and you are still learning the geography of brave.

Here is what nobody mentions
in the literature on sunrise:
the dark doesn't wait to be rescued.
It thins because you moved through it.

You texted someone first.
You sat down next to the kid doing geometry at lunch,
the one nobody sat next to.
You raised your hand
not because you were sure
but because your hand went up anyway,
brave and slightly embarrassed,
like a bird that forgets it knows how to fly
and then remembers mid-fall.

That's when the shadows lose interest in you.
That's when the birds begin their editorial.

And later—
maybe weeks, maybe the length of one good friendship—
you'll notice the sky has gone completely gold,
and you'll think: when did that happen,

not knowing yet
that you were always
the one making the light,
the small and ordinary sun
of your own Tuesday morning,

shining, as suns do,
without being asked.

What the Nose Knows
Created on February 20th, 2026
I have been thinking about the quilt again,
the one folded in the cedar chest
like a letter I keep meaning to answer.

Someone stitched a wedding dress into the corner—
all that ceremony reduced to a square,
the vows hemmed in at last.

Next to it, a child's painting of a horse
that looks more like a table with ambitions,
which is to say: accurate, in its way.

Here is what I didn't expect:
the mouse.

Not the mouse of fairy tales,
not the mouse of sympathy cards,
but the actual mouse, nudging patches
with the one thing it owns outright—
that pink nose, damp and specific,
a small lamp in a large dark.

It doesn't know what a wedding is.
It doesn't know what joy is, technically.
And yet it pushes. Rearranges.
Finds the seam between sorrow and satin
and follows it, the way we follow
a half-remembered melody into a room
we thought we'd locked.

The nose is the color of something
I can't name without embarrassing myself.
Sunset, maybe. The inside of a shell.
The moment before you decide to say yes.

I used to think the fragments meant failure—
all those saved pieces, all that careful folding,
the chest that holds what couldn't be kept whole.
But watching the mouse in its small insistence
I see something about tight spaces
and the patient work done inside them.
Its nudging isn't loss or ruin;
it simply moves what is there.
A quilt is not a wound.
That pink, in the right darkness,
is enough to steer by.

What are you doing with your pieces?
The ones in the chest. The ones
you packed in tissue and forgot about,
the ones that still smell like the original occasion.

The mouse isn't asking—
only acting.
Yet that nose—a compass, a wick, a very small lantern—
keeps pointing, as always,
toward the thing you almost threw away.

Parched
Created on February 19th, 2026
I’ve stood out here for eighty years,
With dust and beetles in my ears.
The sky is blue, the sun is bright,
It’s frankly a disgusting sight.

My leaves are crisp, my twigs are brittle,
The clouds aren't helping—not a tittle!
I’m praying for a heavy pour,
To wash the squirrels from my door.

My bark is itchy, roots are dry,
I’m glare-ing at a heartless sky.
I’ve raised my limbs in desperate plea,
For some humidity for me!

My rings are tight, my sap is thin,
I want a supervisor in!
I’ll stand here stiff and gnarled and gray,
Until a storm comes through my way.

On the Back of a Sparrow
Created on February 18th, 2026
I don't know how I got there exactly,
the way you never know in dreams
how you came to be standing in your old school
wearing only one shoe,

but there I was, gripping the warm brown feathers
of something no bigger than a fist,
and we were rising—
past the gutters, past the neighbor's oak,
past the particular cloud that always looks
like a philosophy professor I once had.

And here is the strange thing,
the thing worth mentioning over coffee:
all of it came loose.

The unpaid bill, the unanswered letter,
the thing I said in 1987 that still wakes me at 3 a.m.,
the general shapeless dread
that follows me around like a dog
who has chosen the wrong owner—

all of it simply
slipped from my pockets
and fell back into the world
where it apparently belonged.

I remembered then what Emily knew,
that hope had been flying around in a small body
this whole time,
singing without words,
asking nothing,
keeping us aloft
in the most unlikely of ways—

not an eagle, not an angel,
just a sparrow,
doing what sparrows do,
which is more than enough.

Two Teddy Bears on Market Day
Created on February 16th, 2026
You know how sometimes you look out the window 
and the ordinary street has decided to be ridiculous 
just for a moment, as if life got tired of being itself 
and put on a little costume? 

That's what happened this evening in the village 
when two teddy bears appeared on the cobblestones,
walking side by side like old neighbors returning from errands. 
One carried two bulging bags of groceries— 
you could hear the faint clink of jam jars knocking together,
a small percussion section announcing abundance— 
the other held a bouquet of yellow tulips 
bright as fresh paint against limestone walls. 

Their fur was the same soft gray-brown 
as the weathered cottages they passed,
so they seemed less like visitors 
and more like the village itself had grown legs 
and gone shopping. Their sweaters— 
one forest green, the other oatmeal— 
blended with the moss and the stone,
as if the landscape had dressed them 
before letting them out the door. 

From down here at paw level,
the cobblestones rise like a miniature Alps,
each rounded peak a small challenge 
for their polished boots to navigate. 
The wicker handles bite gently into plush paws,
the baguette crust rasps against velvet pads— 
a little friction, a small conversation 
between the baker's hard work and their grip. 

And the silence—oh, the silence of fur 
is something humans can only envy. 
No footsteps to announce them,
only the soft scritch-scritch of wool sleeves 
rubbing as they waddle along,
and the low, gravelly hum 
the taller one makes, vibrating deep 
like the village settling into dusk. 

The tulips aren't just flowers; 
they're tiny lanterns the second bear carries 
to push back the gray shadows creeping in,
while the groceries speak of pantries and suppers,
of ordinary evenings that somehow matter. 

I watch them go, these stitched ambassadors 
of sturdiness and softness,
and I wonder if that's the secret we've been missing— 
to dress warmly against the damp air,
to carry both the bread and the brightness,
to belong so completely to a place 
that your fur matches its stones. 

Maybe that's why they look so content,
so quietly amused at the whole arrangement. 
They know the village provides the setting,
and they provide the heart that carries it home. 
Author's Notes: contest Image Prompt The Teddy Bear Shopping - Oméga.á.m. - Image provided by host. Poem written to the image.
On the Gift of Scorn
Created on February 16th, 2026
How long I wandered, pen in hand, half-blind,
Through gardens where no serpent dared to coil,
Where praise fell soft as Eden's fruitless soil,
And comfort wrapped my vision like a rind.
Then came your words—a flame to loose and bind—
That scorned my verse as derivative toil,
As ego's mask; a cluttered, worthless foil:
The grit I lacked, at last, for pearl refined.

For what is light that never knew the deep?
What triumph rises where no battle came?
You gave me chains, and taught my hands to break them;
Your bitterness became the mine I keep,
The fuel I lacked to kindle up my flame—
So thank you, friend; I've learned at last to take them.

The Witchfool's Brew
Created on February 15th, 2026
The witches found their recipe book,
"New England Toadstool Soup!" they cheered,
they didn't give the page a look—
just read it upside down (how weird!).

The first witch cried, "ʇʍǝu ɟo ǝʎǝ!"
and plopped in eyes—KERPLUNK! KERSPLAT!
The soup turned green and winked with glee,
the eyeballs blinked and stared back flat!

The second squinted, "ǝoʇǝlʇsᴉɯ!"
She dumped in toes, all gray and thick,
the cauldron burped and overflowed
with gooey, oozy, goopy sick!

The third witch read "sƃuᴉʍ ʎɹᴉɐɟ!"
and tossed in wings that flapped around,
the pot exploded purple high—
and chicken feathers hit the ground!

They flipped the book, then screamed, "OH MY!
We've been reading upside down!"
They looked down at their soup and sighed—
the health inspector's coming to town.

The Kitchen That Wasn’t There
Created on February 14th, 2026
I was back in my mother's old kitchen,
the one from before the remodel,
before she died, before any of that—
the yellow wallpaper with its faint tea stains,
the smell of dish soap and Maxwell House.

She was standing by the counter, maybe fifty,
wearing that blue cardigan I'd forgotten about
until right then, the one with the wooden buttons
I used to spin when I was small enough
to stand at her hip while she cooked.

We were talking about something ordinary—
whether it would rain, or if the Hendersons
still had that loud dog—when she looked at me
and said, as casually as you might mention the time,
"You know I've been dead for three years now,"
and I said, "Yes, I know," the way you'd agree
that it's Tuesday, or that the milk's gone bad.

Then somehow we were folding towels together,
the white ones with the blue stripes,
standing side by side at the kitchen table
the way we must have done a hundred times
when such things were still possible.

Neither of us spoke, but I could feel
the warmth coming off her hands
when we both reached for the same corner.

When I opened my eyes, my bedroom
seemed too bright, too insistent,
the furniture all wrong somehow,
refusing to be anything but real.

I lay there trying to hold onto it—
the yellow walls, the specific weight
of those towels in my hands.

I wanted to tell someone, but what would I say?
That my dead mother and I folded laundry
and neither of us found it strange?

Now I'm sitting here with my coffee
watching the morning arrange itself
into the usual shapes and sounds,
and all I can think about
is how carefully we lined up those corners,
how we both knew exactly what we were doing
and never said a word about it.

The Committee
Created on February 12th, 2026
I watched them assemble at the glass—
a tribunal of ducks, their orange beaks
like stamps waiting to approve or deny
my request for entrance. I had come
bearing stale sourdough in a plastic bag,
thinking this would be enough,
but they wanted something more.

It was the way they pressed forward,
white feathers overlapping like tiles
on a crooked roof, each neck craned
to get a better look at me standing there
in my ridiculous human shoes.
One blinked. Another shifted weight.
The huddle tightened.

I thought of all the committees I have faced—
the silent panels, the skeptical faces,
the forms I forgot to fill out in triplicate—
but none quite like this, where the only question
seemed to be whether I understood
the terms of engagement, whether I grasped
that bread is not a right but a negotiation.

They were waiting, I realized,
for me to make the first move,
to tear the bag open and scatter proof
across the water. But I stood there,
frozen by the intensity of their staring,
those dark eyes beneath the feathered brows,
and wondered who was really on trial.

The video doorbell would show only this:
a man holding bread, a wall of white and orange,
and the long moment before anyone moved—
that pause where power shifts hands.
Then my wife's voice from inside: "Honey,
those are geese. The ducks are around back."
I had been reporting to the wrong committee all along.

The House Speaks of Its Karass
Created on February 12th, 2026
I am seventy-six years old.
For a person, that is a long time;
For a house like me, I’m just getting settled.
So they say.

I’ve lived long enough to develop voices.
They start at the foundation and work their way up.

The floors talk to nobody in particular.
They just talk.
They say things like:
"Remember the child who spilled the milk in 1952?"
And
"Remember the couple who danced here before the war?"

The war was always before or after something.

My walls have invented a word for themselves:
"sunder-bind."
It means: things that hold other things apart
While keeping them together.
The walls are very proud of this.

The ceiling doesn't talk to anyone.
It just listens and sags,
Which is what ceilings do
When they get old enough to understand
That everything ends up on the floor eventually.

The air inside me has opinions.
It remembers every breath,
Every argument about money,
Every "I love you" said in the dark.
The air says: "I am the true historian here.
The rest of you just stand around."

But the air is pretentious.

A table and four chairs live in my kitchen.
They form what I call a "granfalloon"—
A group of beings who think they're connected
By something meaningful
When really they're just
Occupying the same space.

The table disagrees.
It says they are a "karass"—
Truly connected by invisible wires
Of purpose.

I don't know who's right.
I'm just a house.

What is old?
Old is knowing that paint always peels,
That people always leave,
That the pipes will freeze in winter
No matter what you do.

Old is not minding anymore.

The child who spilled the milk in 1952
Came back last week.
She's seventy-three now.
She looked at my walls and said,
"This place is falling apart."

I wanted to say:
"So are you, lady.
So are we all.
Nice, nice, very nice."

But houses can't talk,
Except in poems like this,
Which are "varnish-truths"—
Harmless untruths
That make you
Brave and kind and healthy and happy.

So they say.
Author's Notes: contest This Old house. - Rick Slottow
The Shoulder of the Road
Created on February 12th, 2026
I admire the patience of gravel—
how it keeps to the margin of things,
not road, not ditch,
just the narrow strip where the asphalt frays
and the weeds take over.

You know the place—
where engines sputter and blink their warnings.
Where someone pulls over
to cry about something
they cannot cry about at home.

A family stops to unfold a paper map,
the father leaning on warm metal,
squinting as if the horizon might confess.
Sometimes it’s only one car, hazards ticking,
a blue glow of a screen across a face
lit by messages better left unopened.

The gravel receives it all—
flat tires, urgent calls,
the long exhale of someone
who sits gripping the wheel
and cannot remember
why they started driving in the first place.

We build these in-between places
and call them nothing.
Yet the shoulder holds us
when we can’t hold ourselves.
It keeps the small evidence of our pausing:
a cracked taillight lens,
a receipt gone pale with sun,
a single shoe stiff with rain.

It is always three in the morning there.
The shoulder keeping watch
while we decide
whether to signal and merge
or let the engine idle a little longer—
the world tearing past
at seventy miles an hour,
and us, for once, unmoving.

Even as the backdraft of a passing truck
shudders the glass and rattles the frame,
reminding us, for one violent second,
how thin the metal is between us and the world.

The Misfit Parade
Created on February 11th, 2026
A hum-hum-hums inside my chest,
A radio that never rests.
While briefcase people rush on by,
I'm tuned to a PECULIAR sky.

I walk through alleys, brick and gray,
Past spray-paint names that fade away.
I feel like I'm a stranger-guest,
Un-easy in my own skin-vest.

Then BEAR appears beside my feet,
With heavy paws and furnace heat!
A raven perched up-on a post,
They know me like a friendly ghost.

No questions asked, no forms to fill—
They sense my blue, they feel my chill.
The raccoon grabs my scattered thoughts,
The fox keeps step through parking lots.

I wasn't lost, just out of place,
Till creatures came and matched my pace.
This wild-tribe found me by my sound—
No map required to come around!

On Being Bamboozled
Created on February 11th, 2026
I want to tell you about the time I was hoodwinked
by a lie so elaborate, so beautifully constructed,
that when the truth finally revealed itself
like a magician pulling back a velvet curtain,
I found myself applauding.

It was the kind of deception that required blueprints,
a three-act structure, supporting characters
who had clearly rehearsed their lines.
The kind of bamboozling that takes real commitment,
the way a good novel takes commitment,
or a marriage, or learning to play the bassoon.

I was befuddled, yes, and certainly flummoxed,
hornswoggled in the classical sense,
but here's the thing—I was also impressed.
The architecture of the lie was stunning.
Each detail fit into the next like Russian dolls,
or those ships they build inside bottles,
and I couldn't help but admire the craftsmanship.

You know how sometimes you watch a movie
and halfway through you realize you've been misdirected,
that the real story was happening in the background
while you were focused on the wrong thing?
It was like that, only I was living in the movie,
wandering around with my popcorn and my certainty.

And when it all came tumbling down—
when the house of cards became just cards again,
scattered across the table in no particular order—
I wasn't even angry.
I was too busy being dazzled
by how long someone had held their breath underwater,
how many plates they'd kept spinning,
how they'd managed to make me believe
that up was down and Tuesday was Friday
and the cat had been a dog all along.

There's an art to being fooled this thoroughly.
It requires a certain surrender, a willingness
to trust the teller of the tale.
And maybe that's the real trick—
not the lie itself, but the invitation to believe,
the temporary gift of living in someone else's fiction,
even if the gift turns out to be empty-handed.

The Boot-i-ful Lady
Created on February 7th, 2026
She's planted in a boot-i-sole,
No longer on a walking stroll.
Her skin is leath-ery and creased,
Her wandering has been de-leased.

The laces wind like vein-y thread,
A garden for her ankle-bed.

A boot-an-ist growing happily.

Does she miss the step-by-step?
Or love her root-ed rest and pep?
She's grown into her leather home—
No need to walk, no need to roam.

She's part-ly boot and part-ly she,
A plant-ed human happily.
The sole-mate that she'd always sought—
A place to stay, forever caught!

The Performance
Created on February 7th, 2026
There's a particular expression I've perfected
over the years, a kind of theater of disappointment
I put on when someone mentions the event
I didn't attend, the gathering I somehow managed
to avoid through a combination of legitimate excuses
and what I can only describe as strategic forgetfulness.

"Oh," I say, letting my face fall just enough
to suggest genuine regret without overdoing it,
"I completely forgot that was last night."
And then I listen—really listen—
as they describe the cramped room, the warm wine,
the conversation that circled endlessly
around someone's remodeling project,
the strange cheese that nobody touched.

Inside, though, there's a small celebration happening,
a quiet parade moving through the chambers of my heart,
led by the part of me that spent the evening
in slippers and reading glasses, a book open on my lap,
the cat asleep on the arm of the chair,
the world reduced to the pool of lamplight
and the gentle tick of the clock on the wall.

I've learned that sympathy is the currency
we use to purchase our solitude,
that a well-timed "That sounds wonderful,
I'm so sorry I missed it" can be traded
for another evening of exactly this—
the dog at my feet, the tea gone cold,
the blessed absence of anywhere I need to be
or anyone I need to become for a few hours.

"Next time for sure," I say, meaning it
the way we all mean the kind things we say
to make the world feel a little softer,
already knowing that next time
there will be another book I haven't finished,
another evening when the couch
will be exactly where I left it.

Morning at the Meadow's Edge
Created on February 1st, 2026
The light was breaking through the pines
when a deer and a fawn stood just beyond
the clearing where I'd stopped to watch
the world begin again.

How still they were, breathing the morning,
their dark eyes holding secrets we forget—
that we belong here, walking this earth
beneath the indifferent and magnificent stars.

The mother's ears turned, catching sounds
I couldn't hear, her body a question
the forest answered with silence.

The fawn, all legs and newness,
stepped closer to where grasses bow
and the dew makes everything glow.

Tell me, what else could matter
in this moment when time slowed
and two wild ones allowed me to see
what patience looks like, what grace means?

They didn't need my wonder,
but they permitted it—
these creatures who know without knowing
that the world is generous and hard
and that we must love it anyway.

When they left, moving like water through trees,
I stood in the emptied space they'd blessed,
grateful for what I'd been given:
this one morning, this brief meeting,
this reminder that we are not alone
in our fumbling toward beauty.

Emily's Ghost
Created on January 30th, 2026
I used to think words were safer kept inside,
tucked away like good china in the cabinet,
the kind you only bring out for special occasions
that never quite arrive. You know the ones—
spotless, unused, gathering dust in the dark
while you eat off paper plates.

But Emily Dickinson, buttoned up in Amherst,
said something strange about this.
She claimed a word is dead when it's said,
though some people think the opposite is true.
I'm with the some people, though not
for any reason Emily would have approved of.

A word inside your head is like a goldfish
in a bowl—safe, sure, but also going nowhere,
swimming the same small circle day after day.
Say it out loud and suddenly it's in the ocean,
which is to say it's in trouble, picking up
barnacles and plastic bags and someone else's meaning.

This is what we mean by living, I think—
getting passed around like a rumor at a party,
arriving at the other end of the room
completely different from how you started,
carrying fingerprints and lipstick stains
and three versions of the truth.

Emily was right that the word begins its life
the moment it leaves your mouth, but she forgot
to mention how exhausting that is, how it wanders
from ear to ear getting more confused,
until finally it collapses on a couch somewhere,
meaning everything and nothing, tired and alive.
Author's Notes: contest Emily Dickinson Quote - Mrs Campbell
So It Goes with Wings and Roots
Created on January 29th, 2026
A painted wing seeks something sweet,
A flower waits on rooted feet,
The dancer lands, the holder sways,
And so they spend their summer days.

The thirsty one drinks deep and long,
The patient one breathes dusk like song,
A drifting scent through evening air,
A fragrance hanging soft and fair.

The restless one keeps fluttering wide, 
The rooted one holds earth with pride,
But neither minds the way things are,
One staying close, one flying far.

They need each other, that's the trick,
The still one steadies the one that's quick,
The one who moves, the one who stays,
Connected in peculiar ways.

One gathers glimmers from the skies,
One grounds them where the quiet lies,
And through the bond of wing and root
A deeper meaning finds its route.

Author's Notes: contest Butterfly among the Flowers - PinkFaerie5 - WC - 132 - Image credit Betty Thomas

Hullabaloo in the Quiet Hours
Created on January 20th, 2026
I want to tell you about the bear I saw last Tuesday,
ambling through the produce section in a floral sundress,
the kind with tiny buttons down the back
that no bear could possibly fasten alone.

This was Baloo, naturally, because what other bear
would pair a hulla—that flowing Arabic garment—
with such magnificent nonchalance,
reaching one enormous paw toward the honeydew melons?

The cashiers barely looked up from their registers.
The manager continued stacking cans of soup.
Here was hullabaloo in its truest form—
not the commotion you'd expect, but its opposite,

a sloth bear humming something low and drowsy,
some ancient lullaby about moonlight and mangoes,
while shoppers wheeled their carts around him
as if bears in dresses were simply part of Tuesday.

I suppose this is what it means to break a word apart—
to find the hulla nestled against the baloo,
the fabric against the fur, the chaos
transformed into the gentlest kind of song,

the kind that asks nothing of you
except to notice how a bear can wear his strangeness
with more grace than most of us manage
in our sensible shoes and matching socks.

He paid in exact change, I should mention,
counting out quarters with surprising delicacy,
then shuffled toward the automatic doors
still humming, still absolutely himself,

leaving behind only the faint scent of honey
and the distant notion that maybe
all our hullabaloo is just waiting
to become a lullaby.
Author's Notes: contest Funny sounding word prompt#1 - Andrew Lawson - This poem isn’t redefining the word; it’s showing what happens when the expected noise is met with a lack of reaction rather than escalation.
Tusk-any Pub
Created on January 19th, 2026
An elephant, walrus, and manatee strolled in,
Two with mighty tusks, one with nubs smooth, all set to begin.
They ordered pot pies, steaming, golden, round,
The best Tusk-any fare that could be found.

The elephant reared up, tusks curved like scimitars grand,
"These prove I'm built for the Tuscan land!"
The walrus nodded, tusks gleaming, thick and bold,
"Mine match yours, mate—we're the Tusk-any gold!"

The manatee just settled deeper in the chair,
No tusks to flash, no boasts to share.
"I've got nubs, instead, and that's just fine—
I'm here for the crust, the gravy, the wine."

While the tusked two dreamed of vineyards and sun,
The smooth-faced one devoured every crumb.
Tusks may impress with their curve and their gleam,
But nubs let you eat without making a scene.

So here's the real moral, fresh from the pub:
Tusks bring the swagger, but nubs bring the grub!
The tusked ones talk Tuscany till voices are hoarse—
The manatee just smiles and orders seconds, of course!
Author's Notes: contest elephant walrus and manatee eating pie - PinkFaerie5 - WC 169 - Artwork credit John Weber 
Retired Elf, Entry-Level Babysitter
Created on January 14th, 2026
I planned my elven twilight years,
With dragonets—no fuss, no fears.
A peaceful post. A gentle breeze.
Instead: burned beard and singed-off knees.

They climb me like a climbing wall,
Use tail as rope (I always fall).
My hat's aflame, my shoes are toast—
I'm not a GUARD—I'm just their POST.

The red spots on each toadstool cap?
Not nature's paint—a fire-y zap!
When dragonets begin to sneeze,
They torch the mushrooms, grass, and trees.

"Dignified," the job ad said.
Now I'm a jungle gym instead.
With scales that cling and claws that grip—
Retirement? HA! What a trip.

No personal space, no quiet time,
Just dragonets who love to climb.
I dreamed of calm, of rest—then whoosh!
My eyebrows? Gone. My pride? All mush.

Bug's Big Night
Created on January 13th, 2026
For ages I've been slapped and swatted,
chased from picnics, doors slam-shutted,
rolled-up papers whacked my kin— 
but tonight the fun begins! 

They sent a card with glitter glue,
"Dear Mosquito, come join the crew!" 
Red carpet rolled across the lawn,
standing ovation—me, the dawn! 

No swatter waves, no screams of "Eek!",
they clap and cheer, they kiss my cheek,
they pour me punch (a tiny cup),
and toast the buzz I've stirred them up. 

I land on cake, they shout "Hooray!",
the band plays tunes in my own way— 
but wait—oh clever twist so sweet— 
they've lit the bug-zap light to greet!

Lamb-bent
Created on January 12th, 2026
Between the dark and light,
Between the day and night,
The sky goes shimmer-soft,
The colors swirl aloft—

Not blue,
Not black,
Not quite this,
Not quite that.

A bird? A bat?
A tweeter? A squeaker?
The dawn gets weaker-stronger,
The dusk stays longer-shorter.

Almost-words on almost-lips,
Doorway pauses,
Threshold trips—

Standing
in
the
in-between,
Half-seen,
Dream-screen,
Might-have-been!

Caterpillar
wriggle-waiting,
Teenager
hesitating,
Friendship
maybe-mating—

Everything that's
Glimmer-glowing,
Never quite
All-the-way showing,

Flicker-light,
Shimmer-bright,
Lamb-bent
Through the night!

Assume
Created on January 11th, 2026
I've been thinking about how fragile they are,
these moments we believe are made of something solid—
the last time I carried my daughter upstairs,
her weight against my chest like a promise
I thought would be there tomorrow.

But it wasn't made of stone or steel,
that moment. It was made of glass so thin
you couldn't see it, couldn't feel
the delicate membrane between now
and never again.

One night I lifted her, and she was light enough.
The next week, or the next month—I honestly
can't remember—she walked herself to bed,
and just like that, the glass shattered
so quietly I didn't hear it break.

That's the fragile part, I think—
not that things end, but how easily
they end. How little it takes.
One missed phone call becomes
the last conversation with your father.
One unremarkable Tuesday becomes
the final morning you'll wake in the house
where you learned to walk.

These moments aren't protected by anything.
No frame around them saying "Pay attention."
No bubble wrap, no warning label:
Fragile. Handle with care.
They arrive bare and exposed,
breakable as an egg balanced on a fingertip,
and we move through them
like bulls in a china shop,
shattering things we can't replace.

This morning I held my coffee cup—
the one with the chipped handle my son made
in third grade, lopsided and perfect—
and I thought about how fragile this is too.
Not the cup itself, though yes, that too,
but this: him still being young enough
that his crooked pottery means something,
me still being here to drink from it.

How many mornings left? I don't know.
But I know the number isn't infinite,
know that somewhere ahead there's a morning—
ordinary, unremarkable—when I'll use this cup
for the last time, set it in the sink,
and the fragile thread connecting me
to his small hands pressing clay
will finally snap.

And I won't know. That's the thing
that undoes me. The fragility isn't just
in the moment itself but in our blindness to it,
how we can't see the hairline cracks forming,
can't feel the structure weakening
until after it's already gone.

My mother used to call on Sundays.
Every Sunday, regular as breathing,
until one Sunday she didn't,
and that rhythm we'd built over decades,
that pattern I thought was permanent as bone—
it turned out to be made of spider silk,
so delicate that one pulled thread
and the whole thing dissolved.

I wonder sometimes what I'm breaking right now,
what fragile last time I'm living through
without knowing. Maybe it's the last autumn
I'll rake leaves with my neighbor,
the last time this particular dog
will rest his head on my knee,
the last ordinary evening I'll spend
believing I have more ordinary evenings coming.

We build our lives thinking we're working with concrete,
but everything—everything—is glass.
And we don't get to know which parts
will shatter today, which will hold
another year, another decade.
We just keep moving, keep reaching,
keep assuming there's time,
while the fragile moments accumulate around us
like a museum full of priceless things
and us, stumbling through in the dark.

So sometimes now I try to move more carefully.
Not all the time—I'd go mad with that much attention—
but sometimes, when I remember,
I pick up whatever I'm holding
as if it might be the last time I hold it.
The coffee cup. The phone. My wife's hand.

I try to notice the weight, the temperature,
the specific configuration of this moment
that will never come again.
I try to treat it like what it is:
something made of crystal,
something that will break,
something already breaking
even as I hold it.

Peace, Considering Its Palette
Created on January 11th, 2026
They tell me peace arrives in amber—
honey held to light, they say,
but I ask: whose honey? Whose light?
The amber that preserved the insect
also killed it. Is this what we mean
when we speak of preservation?

Someone insists on indigo,
the color of evening that suggests depth,
but I want to know: who decided
indigo meant peace and not the approaching dark?
Who gets to name the hour when fighting ends
and call it sacred?

I have been told it comes in silver—
not the bright coin but the tarnished spoon,
and here is my question: is peace
what remains after we've given up,
or what we chose? Is there a difference?
Does surrender by exhaustion count as treaty?

The documents are signed in black ink.
The blood that paid for them is red.
Between these colors, what spectrum
do we inhabit? And who calculated
the exchange rate—three lives for this silence,
ten thousand for that handshake?

My neighbor says peace is brown,
the brown of turned soil, and I wonder
what's buried there. Whose bones
compose the richness we call fertile?
What grows from graves we've agreed
not to mention?

Is peace worth its cost? But who
am I asking—the ones who paid it
or the ones who merely write the check?
The mother who sent her son, or the son?
The border that moved, or the house
that stayed where it was, suddenly foreign?

Tell me: is peace the ochre creek
carrying what the mountain releases,
or is it the mountain itself, still standing
because it can afford to let go?
Is peace a privilege of elevation,
a vista available only to those already above?

Some speak of pale rose, the moth's wing,
beauty that accepts its brevity—
but does peace have the luxury of being brief?
Can the occupied afford to call anything
temporary and still call it peace?
Who gets to choose when the moth unfolds?

What does peace mean in a language
that has no word for it, in a throat
that has forgotten how to shape
the sound of not-afraid?
Can you translate peace into the grammar
of those who've only conjugated war?

I don't want metaphors. I want to know:
is peace boring because it's actual rest,
or because we've stopped paying attention
to who still can't sleep?
Is peace quiet, or have we just
stopped listening for certain voices?

The stone in my palm is grey, patient,
carved by water over centuries—
such soft persistence, such slow work.
But who has centuries? Who gets to wait
for peace to arrive like erosion,
and who needs it now, immediate as breath?

Maybe peace is the color of admission:
we don't know what it looks like
because we've rarely seen it whole.
We've seen pieces—an afternoon, a year,
a single street where, for a moment,
nothing burned. We called that peace

and wondered why it felt
incomplete.
Author's Notes: Contest / The Colour of Peace
Campaign Promise at the Microphone
Created on January 10th, 2026
My fellow citizens, today I stand before you
asking not what your country can do for you,
but what you can knock off the counter for your country.

They say we have no personalities. They say we're all the same.
I say: watch me ignore you in seventeen distinct ways.
Each one a precious snowflake of contempt.

Let me be clear: under my administration,
there will be a mouse in every pot, a bird at every window,
and a red dot we can never quite catch but will chase anyway
because hope, my friends, is what makes us great.

My opponent wants "calm" and "predictable behavior."
My opponent is a dog person. Need I say more?

Jobs, jobs, jobs. I will create thousands of positions
in the lucrative field of knocking pens off desks.
In the growing sector of sitting on laptops during Zoom calls.
In the vital industry of working the pole—
the scratching pole, that is. With the dignity and grace
of a trained professional. Tips appreciated.

And let's talk infrastructure:
I will ensure every home has a cardboard box,
no matter how small, no matter how illogical.

The litter box situation? Unacceptable.
I propose a complete overhaul. Premium sand. Self-cleaning.
Located where YOU can't see it but I can access it
with maximum theatrical urgency at dinner parties.

And to those who say we do whatever we want anyway—
that we're ungovernable, untrainable, impossible—
I say: Exactly. That's why you need me.
I am the chaos you can count on.

A vote for me is a vote for authentic representation.
Finally, someone who openly admits
they only showed up because they heard the can opener.

Together, we will redistribute the warm laundry.
Together, we will occupy the boxes, no matter the size.
Together, we will sit exactly where you need to be,
and we will not move, because this is our time.
If I fits, I sits. That's not just a slogan—that's a promise.
The Forest Zână
Created on January 10th, 2026
I met a princess in the wood,
barefoot where the tall trees stood.
Her eyes so wide with won-der-ment,
I paused to see where magic went.

She cupped an orb of swirl-ing light—
a galaxy of color bright.
"I'm vrăjitoare of this place,"
she whispered with a flowered grace.

The forest hummed a tune so old,
her layered skirts of green and gold
swished 'round her as the light beams played.
A zână born, not made, but made!

She offered me that glowing sphere—
"It holds the seasons of the year,
the whispers of Carpathian trees,
and all the ancient mysteries."

I watched her dance on moss and stone,
this princess-witch who called woods home.
Then off she twirled through dappled shine—
a magic moment, briefly mine.

Reprehensible
Created on January 9th, 2026
I have been thinking about the word "reprehensible",
how it arrives at the breakfast table
dressed in formal wear, refusing toast,
insisting we acknowledge its moral superiority.

Four syllables marching in polished shoes,
each one more pompous than the last,
not like the casual words we use—
"bad" or "wrong" or "pretty messed up"—

no, this word demands we pause,
adjust our posture, clear our throats
before announcing that someone has committed
a grievous breach of civilized conduct.

And yet I find myself oddly fond of it,
the way it clutches its pearls,
gasps at our everyday transgressions
as if each one deserves a formal trial.

Meanwhile, I once ate the last cookie
and blamed it on the dog,
surely a "reprehensible" act,
though I prefer to call it opportunistic,

or perhaps understandable, given how good
that cookie looked on the kitchen counter,
catching the afternoon light,
unguarded and glistening with chocolate chips.

This is what I love about our grand vocabulary—
how we build these elaborate courtrooms of judgment,
these cathedrals of moral disappointment,
and then go on living our small, cookie-stealing lives,

the serious words hovering somewhere above us
like disapproving clouds that never rain,
magnificent in their stuffiness,
waiting for us to measure up,

or simply to notice them there
in their Victorian waistcoats,
while we stand in our kitchens at 3 a.m.,
guilty and hungry and wonderfully human.
The Coffee Cup
Created on January 7th, 2026
Forget the dozen roses wrapped in cellophane,
the heart-shaped boxes of chocolates at the drugstore,
the restaurants booked solid with couples
trying to prove something to each other with prix-fixe menus.
I want to tell you about the kind of love
that happens on a Wednesday in February
when she's still half asleep in her bathrobe
and you're pouring coffee into her favorite mug—
the one with the faded picture of a lighthouse
that neither of you can bring yourself to replace.

It's not the love of Valentine's commercials,
not the kind that needs violins or moonlight
or declarations shouted from rooftops,
not the love that arrives once a year
in red envelopes with somebody else's words inside.
It's quieter than that, more like the sound
of two people breathing in the same room,
the small music of ordinary life.

This is the love that knows
how you take your eggs, which side of the bed
you prefer, the exact temperature you like the house
before you're willing to get up.
It's the love that hands you your reading glasses
before you even realize you're squinting at the newspaper.

Sometimes I think real love is just this:
the way she laughs at your jokes even when they're terrible,
how you both reach for the remote at the same time
and somehow never argue about what to watch,
the fact that she saves you the last piece of pie
even though you both know pie is her weakness.

So let them have their Valentine's Day—
the teddy bears clutching satin hearts,
the couples dressed up and uncomfortable,
the pressure to be romantic on schedule.
Give me instead this everyday devotion,
love, it turns out, is not so much a thunderstorm
as it is the steady rain that makes things grow—
patient, persistent, showing up day after day
to water all the small moments
until they bloom into something
you can't imagine living without,

like that lighthouse mug between her hands,
still warm, still holding something necessary,
still here.

The girl cats all swoon,

A dandy without any care!

To the Reader I'll Never Meet
Created on December 30th, 2025
I don't know your name or where you're sitting
as you read this—maybe at a kitchen table
with coffee going cold in a mug beside you,
or on a bus, the city scrolling past the window
like a film you've seen before but can't quite place.

Maybe you're the type who dog-ears pages,
or maybe you're more careful than that,
the kind who uses proper bookmarks,
those thin slips of paper that mark
where you stopped believing, or started again.

I imagine you have a favorite coffee cup,
chipped perhaps, or perfectly intact,
and that you've held it a thousand mornings
while the kitchen filled with light,
thinking about everything and nothing,
the way we all do when we're alone
with our thoughts and our caffeine.

You've probably stood in a doorway somewhere,
deciding whether to stay or leave,
your hand on the doorknob like it was
the lever that could change everything,
which, of course, it was.

And I bet you've lain awake at three a.m.,
listening to the house settle around you,
all those small sounds that mean nothing
and everything—the refrigerator's hum,
a tree branch scratching at the window,
the particular silence that exists
only in the middle of the night
when you're the only one awake
in what feels like the entire world.

Here's what I know about you without knowing you:
you've lost something you can't name,
found something you didn't know you were looking for,
and spent more time than you'd admit
wondering if anyone else feels
the strange weight of being alive,
of walking through days that are both
utterly ordinary and somehow impossible.

You're reading this, which means
you're still curious, still willing
to spend a few minutes with a stranger's words,
and that, I think, is worth something—
this small agreement we've made
across whatever distance separates us,
you there with your cooling coffee,
me here with my cooling coffee,
both of us wondering
if the other knows what it means
to be so alone together.

Soundtrack for a Long Marriage
Created on December 30th, 2025
It arrived a year late to our wedding,
that song—"Precious and Few"—
climbing the radio charts in 1972
while we were already learning
how to share a bathroom sink,
how to sleep through each other's breathing.

I like to think Climax wrote it for us anyway,
that soft-rock prophecy with its gentle guitar,
the way it promises moments together
will be precious and few—
not the marriage itself, mind you,
but those crystalline instants
when you look up from the ordinary
and remember you've chosen this.

The song understood something
we were just beginning to learn:
that a life together is mostly
coffee cups and grocery lists,
but punctuated by these small gems—
a glance across a room,
a hand reaching for yours in the dark,
the way her laugh still catches you
off guard after fifty-some years.

"Precious and few are the moments we two share,"
the chorus insists, and it's right,
even when you've shared breakfast
eighteen thousand times,
even when you know every story
she's going to tell at dinner parties.
The song knew the math—
that abundance and rarity
could somehow be the same thing.

Now it's 2025, and when that melody
drifts from a supermarket speaker
or an oldies station in the car,
I don't hear nostalgia exactly.
I hear a song that arrived
just in time to narrate the truth:
that we are still here,
still precious, still few,
still learning what Climax
tried to tell us all those years ago
about the quiet arithmetic of love.

The Butter Cow Manifesto
Created on December 30th, 2025
The Butter Cow Committee convened at dawn,
with clipboards clutched and worried faces drawn.
"No margarine!" they cried. "We must be pure!
Six hundred pounds of butterfat, for sure!"

They churned the cream with patriotic zeal,
declaring only dairy makes it real.
"Trans fats are terrorism!" someone screamed.
The crowd erupted. Someone's child ice-creamed.

They brought the blocks in coolers, five feet tall,
and sculpted udders in the Varied Hall.
"She's ripening!" a judge announced with pride,
while families pressed their noses to the glass divide.

The cow stood golden, glistening, refined—
a monument to what the heartland's refined.
No shortcuts here, no substitutes, no fakes:
just cream and salt and Iowa's high stakes.

"Remember ghee!" the keynote speaker roared.
"Clarified commitment! Truth restored!"
They wept. They clapped. They bought a corn dog meal.
The butter cow just stood there, stoic, real.

So bring your children, teach them what is true:
that excellence means never misconstruing
a polymer for product of the teat.
(The cow begins to soften in the heat.)

Where Have All the Flowers Gone (Reprise)
Created on December 28th, 2025
I remember the Kingston Trio asking
"where all the flowers gone,"
which seemed like a reasonable question at the time,
though a bit agricultural for my taste.

But lately I've been wondering
about something less botanical—
where did all the compassion go?

Not that I had it marked on a map,
a little red pin stuck in the heart
of some midwestern state,
with a sign that said "Compassion: Exit Here."

Maybe it's in the back of a closet somewhere
with the fondue set and the bread machine,
things we were excited about once
and then forgot we owned.

Or perhaps it slipped between the couch cushions
along with seventeen cents and a broken watch,
waiting to be found during the next archeological dig
we call spring cleaning.

I ask the internet where compassion went
and it shows me fourteen articles
about why I'm asking the question wrong,
three videos of cats doing adorable things,
and an ad for noise-canceling headphones,
which feels about right.

My neighbor tells me compassion moved away,
couldn't afford the rent anymore.
The guy at the coffee shop thinks
it got a better offer in Canada.
My mother insists it was never really here,
that I'm romanticizing the past,
which is probably true.

But I could swear I saw it once,
back when we used to look at each other
instead of through each other,
back when someone else's suffering
felt like an invitation to help
rather than an inconvenience to scroll past.

The Kingston Trio sang about flowers
picked by young girls, gone to young men,
gone to soldiers, gone to graveyards,
the whole beautiful terrible circle of it.

But where does compassion go
when it leaves the room?
Does it die like a flower?
Does it wait in the graveyard
for us to come back and claim it?

Or is it here all along,
buried under our to-do lists
and our arguments about whether
we should even have to care,
waiting for someone—
maybe you, maybe me—
to finally bend down,
brush off the dust,
and remember what it was for.

The Assembly Instructions for Forever
Created on December 28th, 2025
Forever arrives in a cardboard box 
marked FRAGILE—yet inside, only Nows: 
hundreds of them, identical as checkers,
none more important than the next.

You'd think there'd be blueprints,
some cosmic diagram showing 
which Now connects to which,
but there's only a note that says: 

"Some assembly required. 
All parts already included."

The mad thing is, you can't save them up. 
Each Now insists on being opened immediately,
used completely—then it's gone,
not broken, just transformed 
into the glue that holds the others.

I tried to peek ahead once,
to see what Forever looks like finished,
but Forever's funny that way. 
It only exists in the building,
one Now clicking into the next.

And if you stop to admire your work— 
well, that's just another Now too,
equally forever as the rest.

So here we are: mud creatures 
constructing eternity 
from moments that won't keep,
and somehow—somehow— 
it all holds together beautifully,
or at least it holds.
Author's Notes: contest The Emily Dickinson quote contest #3 - Andrew Lawson -  "Forever is composed
of nows ...."
For Franz Schrader, Who Mapped the Heights
Created on December 25th, 2025
Have you stood where the fog
wraps itself around the mountain,
where stone rises through cloud
like patience rising through doubt?
Franz Schrader knew this—
how to read the earth's body,
how the Pyrenees held their secrets
in folds of rock and ice,
how Mont Blanc wore its snow
the way wisdom wears silence.

He had no diploma, only eyes
that could see what mattered—
the way a glacier moves,
imperceptible as prayer,
the way each contour line
on his careful maps
was a love letter to the world,
saying: this matters, this
endures, this must be
protected and known.

Tell me, what does it mean
to measure a mountain?
Not its height alone, but its being—
the fog drifting through pine,
the meltwater beginning
its long journey down,
the osprey circling above
where bucolic earth meets
the deep blue invitation of sky.

He understood what we forget:
that inner peace and cosmic harmony
are not separate things—
that when you stand
at the meeting place of river and peak,
fog settling into the valley like breath,
you are measuring yourself
against something that will not yield,
something that teaches
by simply being what it is.

This morning the mountain
is half-hidden, half-revealed,
and I think of Schrader climbing,
his hands cold, his vision clear,
drawing what he saw
so that we might see it too—
not just the rustic beauty,
the magnificent indifference of stone,
but the truth that to know a place
is to love it, and to love it
is to fight for its survival.

What gift is this,
to walk the earth with such attention,
to let the mountain enter you
the way fog enters the forest,
completely, without asking permission?
The glaciers are melting.
We know this now as he knew it then.
But today, on this Christmas morning,
let us also know the mountain
is still here, still rising,
still teaching anyone who will listen
that we belong to this world,
river deep and mountain high,
and it belongs to us.
This Mermaid—Could I Call Her a Siren?
Created on December 25th, 2025
I'm told that a mermaid is sweet and benign-ish,
While a siren will lure you to death with her siren-ish.

But honestly, what's the distinction between the two species-ical?
They both have tails and make sailors act quite unreas-ical.

A mermaid sits brushing her hair looking pretty and vain,
A siren sits singing her song of doom and of pain.

But if I saw either one while sailing the seven seas-io,
I'd probably drown just the same, whether mermaid or siren-io.

So call her whatever you like—the difference is merely semantic-al,
When you're sinking beneath the waves, drowning is pretty un-romantic-al.

The moral: whether siren or mermaid, the ending's identic-al—
You're dead either way, so the labels are just rather pedantic-al.

The Little Christmas Train Of
Created on December 23rd, 2025
The little Christmas train starts up again,
pulling out from the station of what I meant to say,
chugging past the wrapped boxes and nativity scene,
through the tunnel of chair legs draped with tinsel,
heading somewhere important, I'm sure of it.

I watch it gather speed under twinkling lights,
this determined traveler with a clear destination—
the perfect words waiting at the end of the line,
the right way to explain about the cards I forgot to send,
the apology that's been sitting in the depot all week.

It's picking up momentum now, rolling past the coffee table,
through the crossing where I should mention the poinsettia,
connecting that somehow to what I wanted to say about dinner,
the freight cars linking up beautifully—
Christmas memories, goodwill, reconciliation—
all the pieces finally coming together.

But wait, there was something about the children,
wasn't there? Something about their toys, or maybe
it was the wrapping paper, or was it—
the train slows down near the tree,
starts to drift, losing steam.

What was I saying? The track ahead goes fuzzy,
the station I was heading toward disappears in fog,
all those carefully loaded boxcars of meaning
suddenly uncoupled, rolling backward,
the engine stalled somewhere between
what I meant and what I'm saying,

and now I'm standing here with my hand out,
reaching for the caboose of a thought
that's already left without me,
all that precious cargo of silent-night wishes
and peace on earth scattered along an empty track
I can't quite see anymore.

What were we talking about?
Oh yes—trains.
The Illusion of an Illusion
Created on December 22nd, 2025
This morning I caught myself pretending
to be too busy to call my mother,
which was odd because I wasn't actually busy—
I was just sitting here eating cereal
and scrolling through pictures of other people
pretending to be happy at brunch.

So I was faking being busy
to avoid admitting I was faking being busy,
which is like wearing a disguise
to a costume party where everyone's already in disguise,
and suddenly you're the most honest person in the room
by being twice as fake.

You can see the problem.

My neighbor waves at me every morning
and I wave back, both of us performing
the theater of neighborliness,
except yesterday I realized he might actually like me,
which means I've been faking a fake friendship
that turned out to be real when I wasn't looking,
like those times you practice a conversation in your head
and then the person says exactly what you imagined
and you have to pretend you're hearing it for the first time.

The dog, at least, seems genuine
when he wags his tail, though I suspect
even he is performing some ancient contract
between man and wolf, pretending to love me
for the kibble, except now the pretending has gone on
so long that it's become the truth,
which might be the secret the dog knows
that I'm still trying to learn:

that if you fake something perfectly enough
and long enough, eventually the mask
grows into your face, and you wake up one day
to discover you've become
the person you were only trying to look like,
standing in your kitchen, genuinely enjoying your cereal,
for real this time, or close enough
that the difference no longer matters.

A Christmas Prayer for the Forgotten Ones
Created on December 22nd, 2025
Look—the sparrow with the broken wing
is still singing from the bare branch,
its small body trembling in December wind.
Who will notice it there, listing sideways,
offering its ragged song to the cold sky?

And what of the stray dog limping through the alley,
its ribs showing, its eyes searching every face
for one hint of kindness, one gentle word?
Tell me, who has time to stop, to kneel down
in the dirty snow, to touch the matted fur
and say: you belong here too?

Listen—the cat with the torn ear,
the rabbit shivering in the roadside ditch,
the old horse standing alone in the frozen field—
each one is breathing, each one feels
the sharp edge of this world, the ache
of being forgotten, of being afraid.

And yet, look how they persist,
how the dog still wags its tail when a shadow passes,
how the sparrow keeps singing its small prayer,
how the horse lifts its gray head to the morning sun
as if it still believes in blessing,
as if it still trusts that somewhere
there is a warm stall, clean water,
someone's kind hand on its neck.

What can we offer them this Christmas
but our attention, our open hearts,
our willingness to see them—
not as problems to be solved,
but as fellow travelers on this difficult journey,
as creatures who know, just as we do,
what it means to be cold, to be hungry,
to wonder if anyone will come?

You do not have to fix everything.
You do not have to save them all.
But you can pause in your rushing,
you can meet the eyes of the dog in the alley,
you can leave food for the stray cat,
you can speak gently to the injured bird.

Tell me, isn't this what prayer is—
this noticing, this stopping, this bearing witness
to the ones the world hurries past?

And maybe this is the Christmas blessing,
that we learn to see what we've been taught to ignore,
that we practice the difficult art of caring
for the broken-winged and the forgotten,
for all the soft animals of the world
who wait in the cold for someone to remember
that they matter, that they belong
to the family of things.

The Bear on the Number Nine Bus
Created on December 19th, 2025
A bear boards the bus in London town,
He squeezes through the folding door,
His fur is thick and grizzly brown,
He shuffles past a dozen more.

The passengers all shift aside
And clutch their papers, glance away,
They dare not stare with British pride—
Perhaps someone invited him today?

He sits beside a nervous man
Who coughs politely, checks his phone,
The bear begins to lick a can
Of honey with a happy groan.

The driver calls out "Mind the gap!"
The bear gets off at Trafalgar Square,
A woman whispers, "What a chap,"
But no one dares to say "There's a bear!"

For asking seems incredibly rude—
Perhaps he's someone's special guest?
And so they choose their solitude,
Assuming someone knows him best.

Ode on Peter Rabbit's Christmas Transgression
Created on December 18th, 2025
Of disobedience first I sing, and of that youthful hare
Who scorned maternal warnings wise and cast aside all care,
And ventured forth on Christmas Eve when starlight blessed the snow
Into McGregor's garden-realm, forbidden long ago.

What moved thee, Peter, tender soul, to break thy mother's word?
What appetite for earthly root within thy breast was stirred
That made thy timorous feet to tread where mortal peril lay,
Where man's dominion, harsh and stern, o'er frozen garden held sway?

Beneath the holy night there stood that plot in silver's gleam,
Where carrots shone like garnets bright, and cabbages did seem
As emeralds encrusted white in solemn, tempting rows—
A paradise of winter's store beneath the moon's cold glows.

With rapture unrestrained he ate, as Adam did consume
The fatal fruit in Eden's bower that sealed our mortal doom.
But lo! The keeper of this plot, McGregor fierce and fell,
Espied the small transgressor there and raised a wrathful yell!

Then fled poor Peter through the beds where winter cabbage grew,
His jacket lost—as bright and fair as heaven's vault of blue—
His shoes abandoned in his flight, like Eden's exiled pair
Who left that garden paradise in sorrow and despair.

He squeezed beneath the garden gate, his trembling spirit vexed,
While sisters virtuous at home their evening meal came next—
Through obedience they supped in peace on bread and wholesome fare,
As Peter shivered in his bed, repentant in his lair.

Yet when the morn broke radiant forth on Christ the Savior's birth,
His mother came with healing herbs of gentle, tender worth.
With chamomile and loving kiss upon his fevered head,
She spoke forgiveness, mercy's balm, and grace where judgment fled.

His sisters brought him Christmas sweets and held him close and warm,
Though they might justly scorn the fool who braved McGregor's storm.
Instead with charity divine they shared their festive store—
Thus mercy conquers rigid law, as Heaven taught before.

The jacket dried beside the hearth, the shoes in order placed,
And Peter learned that though we fall, we may through love be graced.
On this most holy Christmas morn, let every heart discern:
Redemption waits in home's embrace for all who would return.
Author's Notes: After a couple weeks of studying John Milton and looking at the contest theme's I came up with this. Don't normally write in this form but what the heck might as well give it a try. Image my creation
The Tree Finder
Created on December 17th, 2025
He found it leaning against a dumpster
behind the strip mall on Fourth Street—
a Charlie Brown tree if there ever was one,
three feet of crooked pine with exactly seven branches,
the kind of tree that makes you think
someone tried to throw Christmas away.

But Marcus saw something else entirely.
He saw the tree his mother used to decorate
with construction paper chains and popcorn strings,
back when their apartment had heat
and she hummed carols while cooking.

So he carried it back to the underpass
where the others had already settled in for the night,
their sleeping bags arranged like mismatched ornaments
beneath the concrete sky,
and he planted that sad little tree
in a coffee can filled with rocks and snow.

No one said anything at first.
Tommy kept his eyes on his book,
the one he'd been reading by flashlight for three weeks.
Rosa pulled her coat tighter.
Even the pigeons seemed to hold their breath.

Then Marcus started hanging things—
a bottle cap that caught the streetlight just right,
a blue ribbon from God knows where,
somebody's lost mitten, still clean enough,
a photograph of a dog that wasn't anyone's dog
but could have been.

By midnight, others joined in.
Rosa contributed a small bell from her purse,
the kind that jingles when you move.
Tommy tore a page from his book—
just one page, the one with the happy ending—
and folded it into a star.

They didn't sing carols or hold hands
or pretend this was the Christmas
they'd once known in houses with lawns
and families who remembered their names.

But they sat in a circle around that Charlie Brown tree,
and someone opened a can of soup to share,
and Rosa's bell kept ringing in the wind,
and for one night, under the hum of traffic above,
they were people who had a tree,
which made them people who had something,

which made them, for a moment,
less alone than they'd been yesterday,
or might be tomorrow,
when Christmas was over
and the tree would go back to being trash,

but tonight—tonight it was theirs,
crooked and impossible and somehow
still reaching up toward the light.

Winter Errand
Created on December 16th, 2025
A child walks the field's white edge
in red coat and pointed cap,
her pack weighted with something
no scale could measure.

She does not hurry.
She does not pause.

The grasses bend
under snow's patient instruction;
holly berries keep their crimson—
still, since October.
The girl slips between them,
water through stone,
slow, purposeful;
the stone never asks why.

What does she carry?

Perhaps a wish
spoken into December air,
then forgotten;
or the names of those
who will wake alone
on Christmas morn—
each a small coal,
a smoldering promise
she guards in the night.

Or maybe simply the knowledge
that someone must walk
between the world's asking
and its answer.

She is small.
The field is wide.
The snow continues
its patient instruction.

I watch from my window
as she moves across the white expanse.
Her feet make prints
the snow will erase by evening,
but for now they are there—
small marks of passage,
evidence of errand.

Perhaps she carries
the memory of kindness,
redistributing what we've forgotten
we once gave freely.

Or perhaps she is simply cold,
walking home through fields,
her backpack full of schoolbooks
or stones she collected,
or nothing at all.

Does it matter?

The snow falls.
The child walks.
Somewhere a door will open.

And whether her burden is magic or mundane,
she carries it
the way the holly carries its berries,
the way the snow carries itself
from sky to earth—
without asking why,
without asking if anyone notices.

She walks.
The field receives her.
The winter holds both
in its white palm—
patient and impartial
as all true giving must be.

By evening she will be gone.
The snow will cover her tracks.
But something will remain—

not evidence,
not proof—

more like the feeling
that someone passed this way
carrying something necessary,
something we didn't know we needed
until we noticed its arrival:

a warmth without source,
a door left slightly open,
the sense that we've been
quietly tended—

the way one tends
a small flame
in a large darkness,
so others might see
by its light,
though the light itself
goes unnoticed,
as essential and invisible
as breathing,
as silent and persistent
as this snow,
as necessary
as this girl,
this mission,
this faithful crossing
between what we've forgotten
and what we most need
to remember.

Dragon Claus
Created on December 15th, 2025
Old Santa Claus woke up and sneezed,
his nose was red, his throat was squeezed,
"I'm sick!" he cried, "I'm cold! Achoo!
I wish I was a dragon too."

He fell asleep and dreamed of fire,
of burning hot and climbing higher,
and when he woke, what did he see?
Green scales where his arms should be!

His beard had turned to fiery breath,
his sleigh would surely burn to death,
but Santa—now called Dragon Claus—
just grinned and didn't even pause.

He grabbed his sack with claws so bright,
and flew off on his own, that night,
delivering gifts with fiery cheer,
the bestest Santa of the year.

The kids all cheered as down he flew,
and softly landed on the ground.
Beside the tree, what met his view?
Cookies—toasted  perfectly brown!

Looking Up 'Collective
Created on December 12th, 2025
I was sitting at my desk this morning,
my coffee going cold in its mug,
when I decided to look up the word 
for a group of sharks and jellyfish.

A smack, it turns out. 
A smack of jellyfish.

A shiver of sharks— 
now that one I understand, 
the cold fear running down your spine 
when you see that fin 
cutting the water.

I imagine the medieval scholar 
who compiled these,
perhaps a little drunk on mead,
looking out his window 
at the world’s beautiful strangeness 
and thinking, "Yes, that’s exactly what they are—" 
a shrewdness of apes, 
as if they’re all sitting around 
in tweed jackets, discussing philosophy.  ,
a prickle of porcupines,
an unkindness of ravens.

And here I am, centuries later,
my coffee now completely cold,
realizing that language 
is just our way of trying 
to organize the chaos,
of giving names to the mystery 
of things moving together.

Huh?
Created on December 10th, 2025
I was walking past the post office 
on Thursday 
when I heard a woman say 
to someone on her phone— 

you don’t know what you don’t know 
but you need to know 
that you don’t know— 

and I stopped right there 
on the sidewalk 
like a wind‑up toy 
that had wound down. 

Huh, I thought,
and then again—huh— 
the sound of a mind 
catching itself mid‑stride. 

It was one of those sentences 
that eats its own tail,
a snake made of appetite,
the kind of thing you’d find 
in a fortune cookie 
written by a philosophy professor 
having a bad day. 

I wanted to chase her,
ask her to say it again slowly,
etch it on my hand,
but she had already turned the corner,
taking her koan with her 
like a purse 
clutched tight. 

You know how sometimes 
you open the refrigerator 
and forget what you were looking for,
so you stand there in the cold light 
hoping the mustard 
or the milk 
will remind you? 

That’s what this felt like,
only bigger— 
standing in the refrigerator of the universe 
with the door wide open,
light pouring out,
and no idea 
what I came for. 

My dog doesn’t worry about this. 
His knowledge is a perfect circle: 
dinner at six,
squirrels are the enemy,
the mail carrier is a fiend. 

He doesn’t lie awake 
wondering about the things 
he hasn’t even thought 
to wonder about. 

But we do. 
We’re cursed with this— 
this ability to know we don’t know,
and worse,
to suspect there are entire libraries 
we haven’t found the door to yet,
vast, silent shelves 
of everything we’ll never learn. 

So I stood there,
probably looking foolish,
turning the sentence over 
like a Rubik’s cube,
until a kid on a skateboard 
slid past me 
and I realized I was blocking traffic,
lost in the very thing 
I was trying to understand— 

which seemed about right,
like the only honest place to be: 
standing still on the sidewalk,
knowing just enough 
to know I don’t know enough 
to know what I don’t know— 

and somehow—
somehow,
that felt like progress.

The Shadow's Reply
Created on December 9th, 2025
I was walking down Elm Street talking to myself
as usual, you know how it is,
asking whether it's better to be the thing
or the shadow of the thing,
when from somewhere behind me
a voice answered, clear as a church bell,
"Both are equally temporary."

I stopped and turned around
but there was only the usual cast of characters:
a woman wrestling with grocery bags,
a golden retriever who looked like he had opinions,
and a row of mailboxes standing at attention.
No one seemed to have noticed my private conversation
with the afternoon air.

Half a block later, still thinking about this
as I passed the bakery where the air
always smells like possibility and cinnamon,
I asked myself why we spend so much time
trying to be solid when we're mostly made of light
and memory anyway,
and again that voice, closer this time,
whispered, "Because even shadows need somewhere to stand."

That's when I noticed it—
stretched out before me on the pavement,
a perfect replica of my confusion,
following my every step like a loyal dog
who had finally learned English.
It had been listening all along,
my quiet companion, my silent twin,
my own darkness given a voice.

"Next time," it seemed to say,
as I watched it dance across a cracked sidewalk,
"just walk toward the sun.
I'll handle the rest."

The Magic of Christmas (Song)
Created on December 7th, 2025
The lights are blinking in the trees,
each bulb a tiny star,
and children press their noses close
to  windows near and far.

Inside a youngster's dreaming mind,
the world turns silver-gold,
where reindeer truly fly at night
through glittering, icy cold.

The magic swirls like snowflake dust
in every girl and boy—
they count the sleeps till Christmas comes
and Santa brings them joy.

Oh, Christmas light, you shine so bright,
through children's eyes and memory's sight,
we're keepers of the flame tonight,
passing wonder down, holding magic tight.

But grown-ups feel it differently,
this bittersweet delight,
remembering when they believed
with all their childish might.

We wrap the gifts at midnight's edge,
our fingers taped and sore,
we string the lights and bake the sweets
just like our folks before.

There's aching in our happy hearts,
a melancholy glow,
we're keeping magic alive for them
though now we're in the know.

And maybe that's the secret gift we never understood—
we don't lose Christmas when we grow,
we just learn how to give it back,
to light the path for those below,
becoming stars ourselves at last,
the magic-makers in the snow.

Oh, Christmas light, you shine so bright,
through children's eyes and memory's sight,
we're keepers of the flame tonight,
passing wonder down, holding magic tight.

Yet when we gather 'round the tree,
all sparkly, warm, and bright,
we're one big family of stars
that twinkle through the night.

The children gasp at every gleam,
the teens pretend they're cool,
but everyone still holds the dream—
that's Christmas's golden rule.

Oh, Christmas light, you shine so bright,
through children's eyes and memory's sight,
we're keepers of the flame tonight,
passing wonder down, holding magic tight.
Christmas Through the Window
Created on December 7th, 2025
I have been watching the children press their noses
against the cold glass of December,
their breath fogging up small circles of wonder
where they draw stars with their fingertips.

You know the look—that particular widening
of eyes when they see the lights strung along the eaves,
as if each bulb contains a separate universe,
a tiny captured sun they could hold in their palms.

Inside a child's mind, Santa Claus is as real
as gravity or toast, and the tree in the living room
is not merely decorated but enchanted,
a portal standing in the corner by the couch.

Meanwhile, we adults are busy performing
our own kind of magic, the sleight of hand
that turns credit card statements into joy,
the vanishing act of staying awake past midnight
to assemble toys with instructions written
by someone who clearly hated us personally.

There's a tenderness in our exhaustion,
a strange ache when we remember being the ones
with our noses pressed to glass, back when December
was a month-long held breath, when time moved
like cold honey, and Christmas morning
was the only morning that mattered in the entire history
of mornings since the beginning of time.

Now we are the ones hiding presents in the attic,
eating the cookies left out for someone else,
writing "From Santa" in handwriting we try to disguise,
as if our children were handwriting analysts
working for the FBI of shattered illusions.

But here's the thing about Christmas—
it asks us to believe again, not in flying reindeer
or men in red suits, but in the architecture of attention,
the way a family can gather in the glow of colored lights
and become, for a moment, exactly what they hope to be:

The child amazed by ribbon and paper,
the teenager pretending not to care but caring deeply,
the parents trading glances over coffee cups,
speaking a language of knowing looks and small nods,
the grandparents watching it all like filmgoers
who've seen this movie before but still tear up
at their favorite scenes.

We string these moments together like lights on a wire,
bright and fragile, plugged into something larger
than any of us can name—not magic exactly,
not religion precisely, but that rare frequency
where wonder and memory and hope
hum together in the key of cinnamon and pine,

and for once, we all agree to leave the ordinary world
standing outside in the cold while we huddle together
in the warm conspiracy of December,
pretending we don't know that the magic
is just us, loving each other the best way we know how,
which, if you think about it,
is the most astonishing trick of all.

The Golden Rule of Christmas
Created on December 7th, 2025
The lights are blinking in the trees,
each bulb a tiny star,
and children press their noses close
to windows near and far.

Inside a youngster's dreaming mind,
the world turns silver-gold,
where reindeer truly fly at night
through glittering, icy cold.

The magic swirls like snowflake dust
in every girl and boy—
they count the sleeps till Christmas comes
and Santa brings them joy.

But grown-ups feel it differently,
this bittersweet delight,
remembering when they believed
with all their childish might.

We wrap the gifts at midnight's edge,
our fingers taped and sore,
we string the lights and bake the sweets
just like our folks before.

There's aching in our happy hearts,
a melancholy glow,
we're keeping magic alive for them
though now we're in the know.

Yet when we gather 'round the tree,
all sparkly, warm, and bright,
we're one big family of stars
that twinkle through the night.

The children gasp at every gleam,
the teens pretend they're cool,
but everyone still holds the dream—
that's Christmas's golden rule.

Hard to say Harder to eat
Created on December 6th, 2025
I'm going to say it—fruitcake—
there, I said it, the word my brother whispers
like a curse when he sees the package arrive,

the dreaded F-word of December,
and my mother actually shushes him
as if it might hear and take offense.

It sits on our counter like contraband,
dense as a physics problem, dark as a secret,
and we circle it the way you'd circle a UFO

that landed in your kitchen.
My sister claims it's a doorstop with delusions,
my father says it's tradition, which is just

a nicer word for something you can't get rid of.
And me? I'm cutting a slice on Christmas Eve—
our one-gift-early ritual—holding this

boozy, studded thing on my plate,
this edible obscenity, and I honestly
cannot tell you if I'm eating it or it's eating me,

if this is pleasure or some kind of
slow-motion wrestling match with nostalgia.
The candied cherries glow like warning lights.

My brother said it tasted like
a Christmas tree fell into a vat of rum
and got fossilized, which isn't wrong,

but he had two pieces.
My mother says Aunt Rita would be hurt
if we didn't try it, even though

Aunt Rita has been gone for six years
and I'm starting to suspect this is the same
fruitcake, just making its annual rounds,

My father nods approvingly.
Is this love? Is this loathing?
Is this just me trying to digest

the weight of December itself,
all its dense sweetness
preserved in a loaf-shaped paradox

that tastes like both yes and no,
like every complicated feeling I've ever had
about things that show up year after year

whether we invited them or not—
this forbidden word sitting there between us,
daring us to take another slice,
and somehow, impossibly, we always do.
