Santa's Audit
Created on December 6th, 2025
I want you to imagine Santa Claus
perched on the roof of Henderson & Associates Tax Services,
his sleigh tilted across the shingles,
and in his gloved hand—not his famous list—
but a roll of toilet paper.

One of the reindeer, let's say it's Prancer,
has positioned himself on the chimney top,
hunched and focused with that look of concentration
animals get when nature calls at inconvenient moments,
and you begin to understand this isn't a social visit.

Apparently, even flying reindeer need a facilities break
after half a billion houses,
and what better place than above the office
that's been sending Santa certified letters for years
about his "questionable tax status"
and "failure to report livestock depreciation."

So here's the perfect marriage of necessity and revenge—
the chimney that usually receives gifts
receiving instead a different kind of delivery.

Nostalgia
Created on December 6th, 2025
Sixty-eight years, but I am still
that eight-year-old, standing before
the stacked logs where my dog Chipper disappeared,
chasing the brown flicker of squirrel
into darkness between oak and ash.

I called his name the way a child calls—
believing voice alone could pull him back,
that love was loud enough to move through wood.
But only silence answered,
and I knew with sudden certainty:
the squirrel had eaten him.

This was how the world worked then—
things vanished absolutely,
and I could save them if I tried hard enough.
So I began dismantling the pile,
log by log, my hands learning
the weight of each piece,
my voice growing hoarse,
tears starting their own logic.

When the last log lay aside
and no Chipper emerged,
I sat down in the clearing I'd created,
sobbing into the space where he should have been.

The walk back taught me how grief moves—
a few steps forward, then turning
to scan the empty field behind me.
Forward. Turn. Call his name.
Again. Again.

The tears made the path strange.
I took a wrong turn, suddenly lost
in the landscape I'd known all morning.
Only my grandmother's white house,
distant as a star, pulled me forward—
head down, voice breaking on his name.

The gate wavered through my blurred vision.
I was still calling, still turning back
to search the field's expanse,
when he exploded from the bushes—
tail wild, fur warm under my hands,
whole, impossibly alive.

I cried then, too.
Different tears that meant:
you came back,
the world gave you back,
I don't have to carry
this breaking anymore.

What remains is not the mountains
I imagined in those hills,
not the vastness I invented,
but this: the specific weight of logs,
the exact distance between loss and gate,
the way Chipper's warmth felt
under my eight-year-old fingers,
still learning what it means
when something beloved
disappears,
and then—unasked for, unbargained—
returns.
Vigil at the Manger
Created on December 5th, 2025
Beneath the window’s trembling light
The hush of evening sways the air,
Where hay and straw recall the night
A colt was born without despair.

A horse stands patient near the stall,
His breath—mist soft against the cold;
No stallion’s kingly trumpets call,
Yet here his warmth feels brave, feels bold.

Light glints off frozen fence and pine,
The manger holds its quiet claim;
Soft bells, unstrung, recall the shrine,
While angels mute still speak his name.

He does not gallop far or flee;
His freedom found in this small room;
Against the dark he still might be—
The spark that glows beyond the gloom.

The Day I Saw What I Could See
Created on December 4th, 2025
Today I thought I saw a saw
that saw the things I couldn’t see,
it buzzed through breakfast, toast and jaw,
then calmly turned and looked at me.

“I saw you SEE,” the saw announced,
“you saw me SAW that crooked tree.”
I blinked, my eyeballs bounced and bounced—
who saw which seeing, it, or me?

I saw a sea of eyes that saw,
a see of seas that said, “We’re free!”
We saw right through my teacher’s law
of “Only see what’s there to see.”

Now every time I think I see,
I ask, “Or did I see a saw?”
The more I see, the less I’m sure—
and that’s the best sight of them all.
The Highland Cow Who Guarded Clouds
Created on December 4th, 2025
A shaggy cow of clan and cloud,
she hums through Highland wind and sun,
her fiery coat proclaims out loud
that winter’s work is nearly done.

At dusk a ghostly sister gleams,
a snow-white wisp of sith-born grace,
she drifts from mist and feeds on dreams,
then helps a farmer mend his place.

With solemn, star-black, stubborn stare,
a bull conducts the hills like choirs,
their hooves keep time in frosty air,
their horns hang halos, hooked with wires.

Saints bless the kids who brush her tail,
“Care well,” they say, “and you will thrive,”
she grants them cream in every pail,
the wild old ways kept half-alive.

Now tourists snap her gentle grin,
our hairy hilltop mascot, wow—
they call her “fluffalo of tin,”
but she just guards the sky. And cows.

Mrs. Claus on Christmas Eve
Created on December 3rd, 2025
She settles in her favorite chair,
The sleigh bells fading in the air,
A steaming mug clasped in her hands,
While Santa flies to distant lands.

She thinks of children fast asleep,
Of promises her husband keeps,
And in her mind the presents dance—
Each toy and doll in wild romance.

The sugarplums go whirling round,
Not candies, no—but joy she's found
In knowing that by morning's light
A thousand hearts will shine so bright.

She sips her cocoa, sweet and warm,
And dreams of gifts through snow and storm,
Her own delight: to sit and see
The magic of his delivery.

The reindeer hoofprints in the snow,
The cookies nibbled—all for show—
She smiles to think what parents feel
When Christmas morning makes it real.

Our First December
Created on December 3rd, 2025
I want to tell you about the argument
we had on Christmas Eve about the tree,
not a real argument, more like a negotiation
between her family's insistence on white lights
and my childhood allegiance to the colored bulbs
that made our living room look like a carnival
had crashed into a forest and decided to stay.

We stood there in the Target aisle for twenty minutes,
holding two different boxes, each of us
silently computing the cost of surrendering
this particular piece of our past,
as if the wrong choice might erase
every Christmas we'd ever known before we knew each other.

In the end, we bought both.

Now our tree looks like it can't make up its mind,
white lights on the left side fading into green and red and blue
on the right, a luminous compromise,
or maybe a metaphor for marriage itself—
this daily practice of making room
for someone else's memories inside your own.

She hangs her grandmother's glass angel
next to my ridiculous wooden Rudolph
whose nose fell off in 1987 and was glued back on crooked.
I watch her arranging the ornaments,
moving them slightly, then moving them back,
the way she's learning the shape of our shared life,
testing the weight of what stays and what can be let go.

Last night we tried to decide whose family recipe
to use for Christmas cookies, and ended up
making both batches, her mother's spritz
dusted with sugar, my aunt's Mexican wedding cakes
dissolving on the tongue like edible snow.
The kitchen smelled like both our childhoods at once,
a scent that had never existed in the world before.

This morning, our first Christmas morning as a we,
I woke to find her sitting by the tree
in my old bathrobe, drinking coffee,
looking at the presents we'd wrapped for each other
with the concentrated attention
she usually reserves for crossword puzzles or maps.

When I asked what she was thinking,
she said she was trying to memorize this moment—
the particular angle of December light
coming through our window, the sound
of the radiator clanking to life, the way the cat
had chosen to sleep on top of her present instead of mine.

She said she wanted to remember this
as the first of everything we'd build together,
the first in a long catalog of mornings
that would someday feel inevitable,
as if they'd always been ours.

And I realized that's what we're doing here—
not abandoning the Christmases we had before,
but planting them like seeds in the same soil,
waiting to see what new species might grow
from this careful grafting, this deliberate cross-pollination
of her history and mine.

The tree blinks on and off,
half in white, half in color,
neither fully one thing nor the other,
but something we made together from scratch,

which is, I suppose, exactly what we are now.

Christmas Morning, 1967
Created on December 2nd, 2025
I want to tell you about the year
my father woke us at five in the morning,
not because he was excited about the gifts
but because he wanted us to see the deer
standing in our backyard, eating the apples
that had fallen and frozen under the snow.

We stumbled downstairs in our pajamas—
my brother still half-asleep, my mother
saying, "Bob, it's five in the morning,"
but going to the window anyway—
and there they were: three of them,
so close we could see their breath.

My father said, "This is better than any present,"
and my mother said, "The turkey needs to go in by eight,"
and my brother said, "Can we open gifts now?"
which is how every Christmas morning went
in our house—all of us wanting something different
from the same day.

We opened presents after breakfast,
which my mother insisted on, even though
my brother and I would have survived on candy canes.
My father always pretended he didn't know
what was in the packages, even when he'd wrapped them himself.
My mother cried every year at something small—
a card, a scarf, once just the way the light
came through the window.

Now I do the same things with my own children.
We eat at two o'clock because that's when
my mother always served dinner. I make
her stuffing recipe, which is really just Pepperidge Farm
and a lot of butter. My daughter sets the table
the way my mother taught her. My son asks
every year if we can open gifts before breakfast,
and every year I say, "After we eat."

Some traditions are just stubbornness
wearing a festive hat. But some—
some are the way we stay connected
to the people we've lost, the way we tell them
we remember, we're still here, we're still
doing it your way because it was a good way,
because you taught us that Christmas
isn't about the presents under the tree
but about standing together at five in the morning
watching deer eat frozen apples,
about crying over small kindnesses,
about getting up year after year
and making the same stuffing, setting the same table,
telling the same stories, which is its own kind of prayer,
its own kind of attention, its own way of saying:
this matters, you mattered, we are still
a family, and this is how we know it—
by gathering, by eating, by remembering,
by standing at the window together
and seeing what we can see.

Say What?
Created on December 2nd, 2025
Of course you can listen and not hear—
I do it every morning when my wife
tells me something important about her day
while I'm reading the box of cereal,
deeply invested in riboflavin content.

My ears are working fine. The sound waves
travel through the little bones with their medieval names—
anvil, hammer, stirrup—like a construction crew
building a house I'll never move into.

It's the difference between the dog
who tilts his head at every noise
and the same dog an hour later
when you're calling him to come inside
and he's pretending to be fascinated by a leaf.

I've listened to entire symphonies
while thinking about my grocery list,
attended lectures where I nodded
at all the right moments, took notes even,
but couldn't tell you the subject
if you offered me money.

Hearing requires a different kind of attention—
it's the door opening instead of just the knock,
the message instead of just the medium,
the song instead of the background hum
while you're doing dishes.

My daughter figured this out at seven.
She'd stand in front of me, put her hands
on either side of my face, and say,
"Dad, I need you to hear me, not just listen."
Which was alarming and wise.

The truth is, we listen to a lot of things:
traffic, television, the hum of the refrigerator,
our own thoughts playing on repeat
like a radio station we forgot to turn off.

But hearing—that's the hard part.
That's when the sound actually reaches
whatever it is inside us that understands,
that responds, that changes shape
because of what it took in.

So yes, you can listen and not hear.
We're all experts at it, masters of the polite nod,
the encouraging murmur, the "uh-huh" and "I see"
while we're a million miles away,
standing in our own small rooms,
listening to nothing but the echo
of our own listening.
How Can You Mend a Character Flaw
Created on December 2nd, 2025
I've been watching those home improvement shows
where they sand and seal and repaint everything,
where a man with a tool belt can fix a sagging porch
in under thirty minutes, minus commercials.

How can you mend a character flaw?
I want to know if there's a hardware store for that,
an aisle between plumbing and paint
where they sell whatever it takes to stop me
from saying the wrong thing at dinner parties.

The Bee Gees were asking about broken hearts,
which seems straightforward—all that tender
talk of tears and years and living lonely.
But nobody writes ballads about being chronically late
or interrupting people mid-sentence.

I tried affirmations once, standing in the bathroom
saying I am punctual, I am present, I am kind
to my reflection, which looked back at me
with appropriate skepticism, toothpaste on its chin.

My friend swears by therapy. He goes every Thursday,
comes back talking about patterns and triggers
and the inner child, who apparently has a lot to answer for.
Mine is probably still in there somewhere,
forgetting to return the neighbors rake, losing gloves.

Some days I catch myself being patient,
listening without planning my response,
and I think this is it, I've cracked the code,
I'm fixed, I'm whole, I'm finally that person
who remembers birthdays and sends thank-you notes.

Then Tuesday happens, or Wednesday,
or any day that ends in Y,
and there I am again—foot in mouth,
promise broken, apology forming on my lips
like a song I know all the words to.

How can you mend a character flaw?
The honest answer is probably very slowly,
with setbacks and small victories,
with the patience of someone sanding down
a table that's been warped for decades.

Or maybe you just learn to introduce yourself accurately:
Hi, I'm someone who tries, who fails,
who shows up flawed and keeps showing up.
The Bee Gees never got their answer either,
but they sure made it sound beautiful, the asking.
Hatred Lives in a Cracked Cup
Created on December 2nd, 2025
I found it this morning on the kitchen counter,
the cup with the hairline fracture running
from rim to base, the one I should have thrown out
months ago but kept using anyway.

You know the kind—it seeps.
Not dramatically, not all at once,
just a slow dark stain spreading across
whatever surface you set it on.

That's where hatred lives, in ordinary vessels
compromised by the smallest break.
It doesn't announce itself with trumpets or manifestos.
It pools quietly beneath the morning coffee,
beneath the herbal tea you drink for your nerves,
beneath the water you offer a guest
who doesn't notice the cup is damaged.

And you keep filling it, don't you?
Even though you know. Even though every time
you lift it to your lips, you can feel
the rough edge where the porcelain split,
the place where the crack wants to widen.

Hatred doesn't need much room.
Just that initial fissure, that first allowance.
Then it moves through the clay like infection,
patient as geology, turning the whole thing
brittle, making everything it holds bitter.

I suppose you could say throw it away,
get a new cup, start fresh.
But we both know it doesn't work like that.
The cabinet is full of cracked cups.
We've been drinking from them so long
we've learned to ignore the taste,
the way our fingers come away damp
with what we pretended wasn't leaking.

Don't tell me about repair. Don't mention glue.
What's broken stays broken, and what seeps through
will find the table, then the floor, then the foundation.
One day you'll wonder why the whole house smells like poison.
I'm telling you now: it started with the cup.

Father Frost's Snow-White Crew
Created on November 30th, 2025
Old Father Frost said, "Listen here,
I'm gatherin' polar bears and deer,
the snowy owls with feathers white,
the ermines prowlin' through the night.

"I'll call the hares who hop and play,
the white wolves howlin' far away,
the arctic foxes, sleek and sly—
we'll freeze the world, just them and I!

"We'll frost the fields and chill the air,
we'll sprinkle snowflakes everywhere,
we'll paint the trees with icy gleam,
and freeze up every lake and stream."

"We'll numb your nose and freeze your toes,
we'll even frost your summer clothes!
We'll make the temperature drop low—
we'll bury everything in snow!"

But then one bear said, "Not today,
I'm tired of cold—I'm runnin' away!
I want some warmth and want some sun"—
and all the other creatures run!

Poor Father Frost just stood and glared,
his snow-white army up and scared.
But winter came on time, you see—
it never needed them. Or he.

Practicing Being Light
Created on November 30th, 2025
I have been looking out the kitchen window again,
the one over the sink with the chipped white frame,
and I hope you won’t mind if this time
I imagine myself as a hummingbird.

Because this morning I woke up heavy again,
my thoughts thick as oatmeal,
and I wanted to be something
that couldn't carry much—
no briefcase of worry, no pockets full of ought-to,
just enough weight to be real,
just light enough to leave.

One second a man in his slippers,
the next  a small, green fever in the air,

a jittering jewel headed for the fuchsia,
those pink paper lanterns that line the porch,
then over to the red salvia—
flames frozen on their stems—

and down to where the lawn relaxes
into a soft, low meadow,
each blade beaded with morning,
the whole world smelling like it has just
washed its face.

From this angle,
hovering over the columbine’s purple cups,
you see how generous the flowers are—
how they keep opening and opening,

how the air itself seems to ring a little
as your wings hum against it,
a tiny engine of vowels and consonants,
whirr and blur,
the b and r of bright, brief, breath.

Flying, it turns out,
does not feel like flying in your dreams—
no slow, solemn lifting over the town—
but more like being the nervous thought
that flits from one good idea to the next,

never quite landing,
just touching each thing lightly—
this pink, that gold,
this small blue bell of morning glory
swinging faintly as you sip.

And then there is the other part of the job,
the night shift they don’t mention
on the nature calendar,
right between “migrate” and “mate.”

You pick a twig,
a thin, unimpressive one,
and you sit down on it the way you might sit
on the edge of a motel bed,

and slowly you let the day drain out of you—
the sugar, the color,
the quick silver of your own attention—
until your temperature drops,

your heart, that frantic drummer,
trades its sticks for felt mallets,
and you cool into a tiny, feathered stone
hanging from a branch.

They call it torpor,
which sounds a little like a sleepy torpedo,
but really it is just rehearsal,
a nightly practice of almost leaving.

And maybe that is why,
back at the sink with the water running,
I feel a small pang for this creature
who spends the bright, brief hours
scribbling his name on the air
in loops of invisible ink,

then hangs himself quietly in the dark,
trusting the cold, quiet universe
to remember, by morning,
how to turn him on again—

the way you and I,
who are not nearly as light,
still lie down each night
in our larger, heavier beds

and hope for the same
unreasonable mercy.

The Little Tree
Created on November 29th, 2025
She carried the seed in her pocket for three days
before she found the right place to put it down,
pressed it into the dark earth with her thumb,
and began her watching. Every morning
she came with her small watering can,
tilting it just so, the way you might
offer water to someone you loved
who could not ask for it. When the rain
fell hard, she held an umbrella, herself getting
soaked through. When the sun blazed
its indifferent fire, she made shade
with her own body. And when winter came,
the first winter, she wrapped the thin trunk—
no thicker than her wrist—in an old blanket,
tucking the edges as if putting a baby to sleep.

But what do we know of the earth's wild rhythms
when we are small? What do we understand
of the necessary losses? In autumn
the leaves began their bright dying,
gold and orange and rust, and then
the falling, the terrible falling. She stood
beneath her tree and wept. She tried
taping the leaves back on. She tried
speaking to them. She didn't know
that the tree was doing what trees do,
that it was preparing, not perishing.

Her mother found her there at dusk,
kneeling in the leaf-litter, and knelt beside her.
"Look," she said, and showed her daughter
the buds already forming, tight and green,
the tree's promise to itself. "It's making itself
ready," she said. "It's making itself
new." And the girl looked up
into the bare branches, into the sky beyond,
and saw—maybe for the first time—
how beautiful the tree was in its starkness,
how strong, how willing
to let go of what it had been
to become what it would be.
How it trusted the world enough to stand there,
naked and unafraid, waiting for spring.

Seventy Christmases
Created on November 29th, 2025
I want to tell you about the boy who could barely walk
but got a car anyway—a Murray Jet Flow Drive Woody station wagon,
cherry red with wooden side panels painted on,
chrome details catching the colored lights from the tree.
Christmas morning, 1955, and he's fifteen months old,
toddling on unsteady legs across the living room
until he sees it there, gleaming like a promise
someone made on his behalf to the future.

Watch him climb in, all determined limbs and focus,
the way toddlers attack the world—
no hesitation, no doubt that this vehicle
parked between the presents and the pine
was meant for him, that his wobbling walk
has prepared him for this, his first commute
around the coffee table, past the radiator,
bumping gently into the armchair
where his grandmother sits with her coffee,
laughing at this tiny driver who just learned
to stay upright and now wants wheels.

He doesn't know he's supposed to be amazed, doesn't know
this is the first entry in a ledger that will fill
with seventy years of torn wrapping paper and ribbon,
the encyclopedia of trees—flocked and fresh-cut,
artificial and fragrant, the Charlie Brown specimen
from that apartment in '73, the one that fell over twice,
the towering Douglas fir the year his own kids were small
and believed in everything, including him.

Let me catalog the Christmases for you:
the year his feet first flew, pedals whirring
perfect eights into the carpet; the year the
living room held a miniature storm, humming
on its tracks; the year of socks, a mosaic of small
griefs he learned to walk upon; the year he offered
a future, small and shining, in a velvet universe;
the year dawn found him a weary architect of a
miniature world; the year the dog, a furry philosopher,
taught them about impermanence and turkey;
the year three stores dissolved into the single
glow of his daughter’s understanding;
the year his name was a forgotten lyric
on his mother’s lips, though the carols remained;
the year the table’s algebra changed,
a silent subtraction; then another;
the year the absences themselves became the guests of honor.

Here's what nobody tells you about Christmas—
it's the same movie playing on a loop,
but you keep switching roles: first the child,
then the parent, then the one who remembers
being both, who stands at the window
watching grandchildren track snow across the carpet
and thinks about time, that patient engine,
how it moves you forward whether you're ready or not,
how you keep pedaling even when the wheels
are too small to carry you anymore.

December 2025, and somewhere in a box
marked "fragile" in his mother's handwriting,
there's a photograph of a boy in a pedal car,
his hands gripping the steering wheel,
his face pure concentration, beneath a tree
in a house that no longer exists
on a street where different families now
hang different stockings, tell different stories
about what matters most.

But here's the thing about that Murray Jet Flow Drive—
it was a station wagon, which is to say
a vehicle designed to carry people,
and if you look closely at that photograph,
you can see them all there in the back seat,
crowded in like it's the most natural thing in the world:
his grandmother with her coffee, his mother
adjusting the bow on a package, his father
checking the focus on the camera, his wife
who doesn't exist yet, his children
who are still decades away, his grandchildren
who are impossible to imagine, all of them
passengers in a car too small to hold anyone
but somehow holding everyone.

And maybe that's the real gift beneath the tree—
not the car itself, but what it carried,
not the Christmases we remember
but the ones we became, the way love
doesn't diminish when we divide it
among the living and the dead,
the present and the past,
the child we were and the person we've become.

So when you plug in the lights this December,
when you set the table and fold the napkins
and wonder why we keep doing this,
why we insist on this annual pilgrimage
to the same rituals, the same songs,
the same hope wrapped in different paper,
remember the boy in the pedal car,
how he climbed in without asking where it would take him,
how he trusted the wheels would turn,
how everyone he would ever love
was already riding with him,
and how, after seventy trips around the sun,
he's still there, still driving,
still carrying them all.

The Vanishing Point
Created on November 29th, 2025
You're asking the wrong question, friend,
sitting there with your poems spread across the kitchen table
like tarot cards you can't quite read.
You want to know if the work is good
before you've finished making it good,
which is like asking if the bread will rise
while your hands are still kneading the dough.

I'll tell you what I think, though I'm just a guy
who writes poems in the morning and walks his dog in the afternoon,
which might make me the least qualified person
or the most, depending on your mood.

The difference between da Vinci and Van Gogh
isn't that one got roses at the curtain call
and the other got a bullet and a wheat field.
It's that both of them kept working
when the work was all they had,
when the canvas was the only mirror that didn't lie.

You say if you alone read it, it's vanity.
But I bet that you bend over a notebook
the way a jeweler examines a stone,
adjusting each facet, testing how the light
moves through the words. That's not vanity.
That's faith—the kind that doesn't require
an audience or a panel of judges in robes.

Sure, we send our poems out into the world
hoping someone will catch them like fireflies in a jar,
but the poem was complete the moment you finished it,
the way a tree falling in the forest makes a sound
whether or not the philosophers are listening.

The value isn't independent of you—it's just
independent of their recognition,
which is a different animal entirely.
You'll write a thousand poems that nobody reads
and they'll still be poems,
the same way a bird sings in the morning
whether or not you're awake to hear it.

So keep your hands in the dough, my friend.
The bread will rise or it won't,
but the rising was never the point.
The point was that you needed to make something
that didn't exist before you made it,
and that need—that quiet, persistent need—
is the only validation that ever mattered.

When Polar Opposites Collide
Created on November 28th, 2025
The Polar Bear came trudging through
With fluffy coat and walking stick, too,
A Christmas tree upon his back—
But wait! The GRINCH began to attack!

The Grinch was POLAR opposite—
He HATED joy, he HATED it!
He grabbed the tree right off the Bear
And cackled, "Christmas? NOT THIS YEAR!"

The Bear just smiled (which made Grinch madder),
Pulled out ornaments from his pack-ladder,
And hung them on the Grinch's nose—
Bright shiny bells and silver bows!

The Grinch yelled, "STOP! You're breaking rules!
Christmas is for silly FOOLS!"
The Bear said soft, "That's where you're wrong—
Christmas isn't stuff. It's SONG."

He hummed a tune, so warm, so true,
The pine trees swayed, the snowflakes flew.
The Grinch's heart went THUMP-THUMP-CRACK—
He dropped the tree and gave it BACK.

"I guess," he mumbled, "I was mean.
You're POLAR opposite—serene."
The Bear just winked and said, "That's right.
Now help me hang these Christmas lights."

Matilda's Climb
Created on November 28th, 2025
Matilda climbed to find out WHY
The grown-ups all forgot to fly,
Why everyone just stayed below
And said, "That's life," and "That's the show."

She passed a crow in business shoes
Who said, "Go back! You're gonna LOSE!"
A squirrel moaned, "It's way too HIGH!
What if you fall? What if you DIE?"

A pigeon sighed, "I tried before—
Got halfway up, my wings were sore.
Turned back around, went home instead.
Now look at me—I'm safe," he said.

Her hands were cramping, knees were bent,
She wondered what the climbing meant.
But still she grabbed each leaf and stem—
She didn't want to end like THEM.

At last—the Giant, ten feet tall,
With gentle eyes and not mean at all.
It whispered soft, "What scares you, kid?"
She said, "That I'll stop. Like they all did."

The Giant smiled. "Then you're not small.
The only ones who truly fall
Are those who never leave the ground.
Keep climbing, kid. That's how you're found."

What Endures
Created on November 26th, 2025
In Abydos, wrapped in linen fine and old,
Queen Weret rests beside her cat of gold.
The priests had carved her name in stone with care,
and inscribed  the feline's name right there.

Three thousand years the darkness held them close,
the queen who ruled, the creature she loved most.
Her tomb contained the oils, the bread, the wine,
but extra space was made for the feline.

The cat wore beads of turquoise at its throat,
had golden claws and silk Egyptian coat.
A bowl of alabaster by its head,
as if it might still need to be fed.

Today the scholars come to contemplate
this woman's power, her choices, and her fate.
They study hieroglyphs and royal seals,
debate what ancient queenship reveals.

But in the museum gift shop down below,
it's stuffed toy cats that steal the entire show—
while Weret's postcards gather dust on shelves,
her cat's the one that practically sells itself.

Two Sounds
Created on November 26th, 2025
She learned to make cereal before she could read,
standing on a chair to reach the box,
the sound of Rice Krispies hitting the bowl
the only conversation in the kitchen at dawn.

Her mother on the couch where she'd been all night,
needle on the coffee table catching morning light,
and you might notice how still the woman looked,
like a corpse arranged in the shape of sleep.

You might think a child would cry or scream or beg for help,
but she was practical even then—

she knew the difference between sleep and stupor,
knew which one allowed for questions
and which did not.

Her father came and went like violence,
unpredictable and always damaging.
When he was there, the air turned toxic,
became the kind of atmosphere before a storm when animals grow nervous and birds stop singing
and the sky turns the color of a bruise.

You can almost hear their voices rising through the walls—
her accusations,
his denials,
the sound of his hand meeting her face,
which is sharper than you'd think,

a crack like a bone breaking.

She would sit on her bed with a book she pretended to read,
her small finger frozen on a single word for an hour,
listening to the architecture of their hatred,
the blueprint of a marriage built on nothing but a bottle of whiskey and carelessness one August night
when she was conceived but never wanted.

They used to tell her she was unplanned,

as if that explained the way they looked through her rather than at her,
as if "accident" was another word for ghost.

The night it ended, she was eight.
She had made herself toast for dinner—
butter and cinnamon sugar, which she thought was fancy—
and she was sitting at the table when he came home.

She knew the signs by then:
the way he gripped the door frame to keep from falling,
the vacant way his eyes found nothing,
the heavy stumbling of his footsteps.

Her mother was on the couch.

Of course she was on the couch.

She watched him cross the room,
watched his hand go back,
watched it come forward,
and this time there was something else—

the gun.

Always in his jacket.
Coming out like a terrible magic trick,
the kind where everyone knows the ending but watches anyway
because looking away would somehow make it more real.

Two sounds.

That's all it took.

Two sounds and the smell of something burning that she later learned was gunpowder,
and then a silence so absolute and so complete
it felt like the world had stopped breathing.

She doesn't remember calling anyone.
She doesn't remember the police arriving
or the neighbors or the questions or the blanket someone draped around her shoulders.

She remembers the toast on her plate,

how she'd only eaten half,
how the butter had hardened into a small yellow pool,
and thinking that she should finish it,
that wasting food was wrong,

that this was the kind of thing that mattered.

Now when people ask about her childhood,
she tells them it was quiet,
which is the worst kind of lie because it's true.
She leaves out the mornings making cereal on a chair,
the nights listening through walls for the sound of her own death,
the final evening when the silence finally became permanent.

Some kinds of abandonment happen slowly,
over years of not being seen or held or loved.
Hers just happened to end in gunfire,
which you might consider a mercy
if you didn't know what came after.

But she knows.

She knows that sometimes, early in the morning, she pours herself a bowl of cereal
and listens to the small sound it makes,
and she's back in that kitchen again,

a girl on a chair,

learning that she would have to raise herself
because no one else was coming,
learning that love was something other children received,
learning that survival was just another word for loneliness.

And now, decades later, she still stands on chairs she doesn't need to stand on,
still makes toast for dinner when the world feels too large,
still flinches at the sound of footsteps in the hall,
still carries that eight-year-old girl inside her like a stone,

still wakes at dawn to silence,

still waiting for someone to pull up a chair beside her and say,
"You were not an accident. You were wanted. You are loved."

But no one ever does.

And she has learned, the way all abandoned children learn,
that some hungers never get fed,
that some wounds never close,
that some rescues never come,

and that the girl on the chair is still there,

still eight years old,

still standing,

still alone.
Transparent
Created on November 26th, 2025
What are transparent poems?
are they the ones you can see right through
like the parents who aren't there anymore—
transparent, the ones who've crossed over.

My father is transparent now.
I can hold my hand up and watch the light
pass through where he used to be,
through the space at the kitchen table,
through the driver's seat of the car,
through the doorway where he'd stand
asking if I'd checked the oil, locked the back door,
remembered to call my mother.

Is that what makes a poem transparent—
when you can see straight through the words
to the shape of what's missing?
The way a window is really just an organized absence,
a hole we've agreed to call architecture?

I asked my friends what transparency means in poetry
and they say clarity, they say truth,
they say a poem that doesn't hide behind metaphor.
But I think they're wrong. I think transparency
is what happens when something becomes so thin
with leaving that light passes through it without stopping,
without even noticing there was something there to illuminate.

My mother is becoming transparent too.
Not gone, but going—each day a little more see-through,
her voice on the phone a little more like wind,
her memory of my childhood a little more like a window
someone forgot to put glass in.

And here's what keeps me up at night:
if a transparent poem is one no one sees,
and transparent means the parent who has moved through,
then what am I becoming as I watch them go?
What does it mean that I can feel myself
growing more transparent, standing here
in the autumn of my life, understanding finally
that we're all just light on our way through something,
that being seen was only ever temporary,
that the most honest poem might be
the one that disappears completely
the moment you finish reading it—

Would you even know it had been there?
Would you feel the space where it used to be?
Or is that the point: that transparency
isn't about being invisible,
but about becoming a window
through which someone else might see
what's on the other side of being here at all?

Unashamed
Created on November 25th, 2025
This morning I was thinking about my neighbor
who waters his roses every evening at six,
how we wave to each other across the fence,
how neither of us mentions the cross
hanging in my kitchen window or doesn't hang in his.

And I want to say this clearly: I am not ashamed.
I believe in God, in Christ, in something larger
than the circumference of my own understanding.
I say this the way I'd tell you my name,
not as a challenge, not as an invitation
to agree with me, but as a fact
about who I am, like having blue eyes
or preferring coffee to tea.

The truth is, I pray every morning,
sometimes on my knees, sometimes standing
at the sink with my hands in dishwater,
and I'm not embarrassed by this anymore
than I'm embarrassed by loving my wife
or crying at movies about dogs.

But here's the thing—and this matters—
my believing doesn't require your believing.
The sun comes up whether you notice it or not,
and my faith works the same way,
quietly persistent, not needing your validation
or your conversion to be real.

I've learned to carry it openly but softly,
the way you'd carry bread to a friend—
offering, not insisting, leaving it wrapped
on their doorstep if they're not home,
never once suggesting they've failed
by not being hungry at the same time I am.

My grandmother used to say grace
at every meal, her voice rising like a hymn,
and I do the same when I'm alone,
but when friends come over who don't believe,
I simply pass the potatoes, grateful they're here.
I won't hide who I am, but I won't weaponize it either.

What I'm trying to say is this:
I am unashamed of the cross in my window,
unashamed of the Bible on my nightstand,
unashamed to say I believe in salvation,
in grace, in something holy moving through
the ordinary hours of an ordinary life.

But I am equally committed to this:
giving you the room to believe differently,
to find your own way or no way at all,
to stand in your truth while I stand in mine,
the space between us not a battlefield
but a bridge we can both walk across
without abandoning what we carry.

What saddens me is not that we disagree,
but that somewhere we were taught
belief must be either hidden or hostile,
whispered in shame or shouted in judgment,
when maybe it could be something else—
present, honest, unapologetic,
and still kind enough to let you be.

So I'll keep my cross in the kitchen where you can see it,
keep waving to my neighbor at six,
keep believing in something vast and loving
that holds all of this—the roses, the fence,
the waving, your doubt, my faith—
and I'll keep thinking that maybe,
just maybe, there's room enough
in this universe for both of us to be right
about what matters most,
even if we never agree on what that is.

Differences?
Created on November 24th, 2025
Between us there is a here and a there—
but where is the where when we both start to stare?
If I step toward you, am I a-go or a-come,
a-near-ly-there-um with my heart going thrum?

If you walk toward me, are you arriving or late,
or partly departing your old single state?
Are you just leaving your lonely behind,
or finding a both-of-us state of mind?

Suppose we’re two beetles on one broomstick,
tiny tick-tack feet going clickety-click,
each little inching an almost hello
till broom, bug, and bravery all start to glow.

Or squirrels on opposite sides of a tree:
you scoot up, I scamper down, silly as can be.
We swirl in circling squirrulations around
till—same branch, same bark, same meeting-ground.

Or think of two raindrops on one window wide:
one slides from the left and one from the side.
Both slipping and tripping in gravity’s giggle,
bumping into one drop with one happy wiggle.

So maybe love’s less about who first began,
and more about who stays to share the plan.
Less about first move and more about next to,
less “Who chased whom?” than “I met you, you met me too.”

Call it you-coming-to-me, or me-going-to-you;
call it a both-ways, two-ways, true-way view.
Either way, as our near and our far coalesce,
the distance between us goes out of business.
Seventy–Five Miles, Sixty–Two Wonders
Created on November 24th, 2025
At 7:05 
a ladybug clocks in 
on the windowsill— 
tiny red suitcase 
already packed 
with black polka–dots

and that 
is the beginning.

Outside,
the morning is mostly milk 
with a teaspoon of sun 
stirred in. 
A bus sighs. 
Clouds commute 
in slow white boots.

We leave our street 
like a shy snail 
leaving its own 
perfect spiral 
of yesterday. 
The mailbox 
waves its red flag 
as if it knows 
we might not come back 
the same.

I count the steps 
from porch to corner: 
one, two, three,
until they become 
too many 
and melt 
into simple 
going. 

Journey is a number 
that refuses 
to sit still.

By lunchtime 
we have traveled 
exactly 
62 small astonishments: 

1. A dog 
spelling its name 
in looping leash–curls. 
2. A cracked egg of a cloud 
leaking light. 
3. An ant parade 
hauling a crumb 
the size of Kansas.

4–13. 
Puddles pretending 
to be pieces of sky. 

14–29. 
A swamp of summer air 
where frogs practice 
their burp–tenor scales. 

30–47. 
Cornfields combing 
the wind’s hair 
straight back. 

48–61. 
Roadkill mysteries,
fencepost hawks,
a billboard whispering 
EAT HERE 
to no one in particular. 

62.
The sudden thought 
that maybe 
my own bones 
are suitcases too.

The bus hums 
its low song. 
My brother sleeps,
mouth open,
a small cave 
for dreams. 

I map raindrops 
on the glass,
naming each drip 
a different future: 

this one 
astronaut,
this one 
beekeeper,
this one 
friend I haven’t met 
yet.

Miles make us longer,
I decide— 
not taller 
exactly,
just more stretched 
between where we were 
and where we’re 
not yet. 

The ladybug reappears 
on my sleeve 
(same spots,
different place). 
It walks my arm–road 
like a red caboose,
losing track 
of the window,
finding track 
on my skin.

By late afternoon 
warm light 
leans heavy 
on barns and backs. 
We’ve collected 
so many new smells— 
diesel, dill pickles,
wet gravel,
old creek— 
the air feels crowded 
with invisible luggage.

A sign announces: 
75 MILES TO GO. 

Seventy–five,
a staircase 
made of maybe. 
Each mile 
another question: 

Will the cousins 
still like us? 
Will the house 
remember my height 
on the wall? 
Will the river 
know my toes?

The road unrolls,
a long gray tongue 
tasting evening. 
Ditches deepen into blue. 
Cows become 
dark commas 
in the pasture,
pausing the sentence 
of the day.

We finally arrive 
where the map 
said we would,
but my legs 
keep buzzing 
with leftover distance.

Uncles appear 
with lemon–slice smiles. 
Aunties stack stories 
taller than suitcases. 
The river 
is exactly itself— 
cold, moss–mouthed,
unimpressed 
with our odometer.

And I think:

A journey is not 
how far we go 
from home,
but how many 
little strange things 
we agree 
to carry 
inside us— 

until one morning 
we wake up,
open our eyes,
and realize 
we ourselves 
have become 
the somewhere else 
someone 
is traveling 
toward.

Reflection
Created on November 23rd, 2025
The lake this morning holds two faces—
mine looking down, and the one
the water makes of sky. Both shimmer,
neither quite true. I think how the word itself
does this: turns inward to mean thought
folding back on thought, then outward
to mean light returning from a surface
it cannot enter.

I remember my father taking me fishing once
at a pond so still it looked like a photograph of itself.
He pointed at the dragonflies touching down,
said, "Look at that," as the whole sky shivered—
which is what fathers say
when they don't have the words for beauty.

I wanted to ask him if he saw his own face down there too,
floating among the lily pads,
but I was eight and didn't know how to talk
about the strange way water makes you see yourself
as part of the landscape, your features
mingling with clouds and branches.

Now I understand he must have felt it too—
the lake doing what the mind does,
taking in the world each morning
in the truck on the way to work, sending it back
slightly altered, tinted with worry and wonder.

The window does this too. Right now
my transparent face is superimposed on the aspen,
so that I seem to be sprouting leaves from my ears.
It's the kind of thing that makes you consider
how thinking is just a more complicated version
of reflection—light hitting the surface of consciousness
and returning, carrying some image of what it touched.

Maybe that's why we use the same word for both:
the mind and the lake are doing the same work,
receiving what comes to them and offering it back
with their own particular slant, their own
quiet commentary on what they've been given.

I think about this as I watch the tree
sway slightly in the breeze, my face swaying with it,
and I feel a tenderness for the word itself,
for the way it holds both meanings so easily,
as if it always knew they were the same thing—
the world returning to us, and us
returning to ourselves, both of us changed
by the meeting, both of us grateful
for the surface that made it possible.
'I Love You'
Created on November 23rd, 2025
You say I don't say it anymore,
and this is true.
The words have become like old keys
I no longer reach for,
hanging on their hook by the door.

But I opened that door for you this morning
before you could balance the bag of groceries against your hip,
my hand already knowing
the exact moment the weight would shift.

Is this not a sentence?

Yesterday, I asked about your day.
Not while looking at my phone,
not while moving toward the next thing,
but standing in the kitchen's
slant afternoon light,
my face turned fully toward yours
the way a plant turns toward sun—
patient, attending, open.

You told me about the meeting, the difficult email,
the small victory no one else would think to ask about.
I nodded.
Such a simple motion,
my head tipping forward,
and yet I meant it to carry everything across the room
that the room needed carried.

Some nights I kiss you
without preamble or reason,
my mouth finding yours
while you're reading,
while you're washing your face,
while you're standing at the window
watching the last light
drain from the oak's upper branches.

These unasked-for gestures—
are they not a kind of speech?

The philosophers say language is what separates us
from the animals,
but I think of how the cat
brings the injured bird to the doorstep,
how the wolf touches its nose
to the neck of its mate,
and I wonder if we have it backward—
if the truest communications
are the ones that bypass the mouth entirely.

I folded the laundry yesterday.
This is such a small thing
you may not have noticed.
The clothes stacked by color,
the socks matched and rolled,
each article returned to its particular drawer.
My hands moving through the warm cotton,
making order, making care visible
in a way that will be undone by evening
and need doing again tomorrow.

Isn't this the nature of devotion—
the repetition that never asks to be noticed?

I have stopped saying the words,
yes.
But watch:
I bring you tea without your asking.
I warm the car on cold mornings.
I move your book from where you left it
to the table beside your reading chair.
I leave the light on when you work late.

Every day, these small embassies
I send across the border between us,
carrying their wordless treaties,
their quiet negotiations of tenderness.

Perhaps what I mean when I don't say "I love you"
is too large for three words anyway.
Perhaps it needs a door held open,
a question asked and answered,
a mouth that finds another mouth
simply because my body remembers
what it wants to say
and knows, finally,
that saying and doing
are not separate countries
but two names
for the same home.
The Grammar of Staying
Created on November 23rd, 2025
In the first month of loving you
I began setting things down more carefully—
the glass on its coaster,
the knife in the sink facing inward,
the book aligned with the table's edge
as if the world might learn from my hands
how to keep from breaking.

You were making tea.
Steam rose between us, a small, invisible river.
I watched how the water
took the shape of each cup
without argument, without defense—
how it gave up its heat
to whatever it entered.

Love is this simple, I thought,
and already knew it was not.
The kettle reddened the burner into a circle.
A circle is a promise
that has forgotten where it started.
We live in its forgetting.

Outside, the oak stood considering its leaves.
It has done this for a hundred years—
the slow invention of shade,
the brief experiments of green,
then the months of standing
with nothing to show,
yet still it remains, rooted,
offering the empty shape of its branches
to every arrival of light.

I do not know how to be that steadfast.
In the nights when you turn away in sleep,
your back is a small mountain range.
I lie awake, measuring its distance,
learning the slow art
of loving what cannot be crossed
by wanting alone.

On the counter, a loaf of bread cools.
You taught me to wait
until its crust no longer burns the hand.
If you cut too soon, you said,
the inside will tear,
the crumb will not yet have become itself.
I think of all the words I have hurried
from my mouth toward you—
how many of them were still dough.

I want to keep you.
The sentence feels wrong
the moment it forms.
Stones keep.
Mountains keep.
What lives refuses that stillness.
Even the pebble in my palm
moves slowly toward dust.

So instead I practice another grammar:
not keeping,
but staying alongside.
Like the creek below the house
that does not hold the stones in place,
only passes and passes,
polishing them into something
that can bear being touched.

Some days, love is easy to find—
your hand reaching for mine
without looking,
as if the body had grown
a new sense, quiet and accurate
as a root finding water.
Other days, it is a shy animal
beneath the house—
I hear it moving,
I leave a bowl of milk at the threshold,
I do not call its name.

If there is a secret to not losing you,
it is not in holding tighter.
It is in learning from the oak,
from water, from bread, from the shy animal—
to offer what I can:
shade, and heat,
a crust that yields without collapsing,
a bowl set down and left in peace.

You pour the tea again.
The cups fill and cool between us.
Nothing in the room explains
why my chest feels newly made,
why each small thing—
your wrist turning,
the faint chip in the rim of the cup—
seems both ordinary and unbearable.

I do not know how long we are given
to attempt this carefulness.
I know only that tonight, again,
I will smooth the sheet beside you,
listening to your breathing
as if to a distant weather,
and will place my hand, lightly,
where your back becomes a hillside—
not to claim it,
only to feel, for another moment,
the moving earth I have been allowed
to stand upon.
The Witch's Raven
Created on November 23rd, 2025
The witch stirs cauldrons of moon-bright goo,
Concocting cloudstorms, midnight dew.
Her fingers dance like spider-webs,
Braiding spells from tidal ebbs.

The raven pecks at shiny things,
Collecting secrets, bottle rings.
His eyes are dark as magic ink—
He steals the words before you think.

Alone, she brews but can't re-call
Which pinch of star, which cricket's crawl.
Alone, he hoards but cannot speak
The shimmer-language that they seek.

Together now! She stirs, he caws!
They mix the moonlight's silver laws.
Her pot goes BOOM! His wings go CRACK!
They've spell-invented Dark-to-Back!

The stars rain down in twinkling spree,
As witch and raven laugh with me.
Their bond unbreakable, you see—
A coven born from witchery.

A Field Guide to Falling in Love
Created on November 17th, 2025
It is not something you can do in traffic
or while parallel parking,
though I have seen people try.

You need a table, preferably one
that has hosted a few arguments
and knows how to keep a secret.

Sit down. This sounds obvious
but most of us prefer to remain standing,
poised like sprinters at the starting line.

Clear a space between the salt shaker
and yesterday's mail—
not much, just enough for one small thing
to step forward and volunteer.

Today it might be a coffee mug
with a crack running down the handle
like a river on a crude map.

Notice how it holds liquid anyway,
how it refuses to quit being useful
despite its injury.

This is what I mean by paying attention—
not the grand orchestral sweep
but the stubborn mug that won't retire.

Now think of her standing here last Tuesday
holding this exact cup,
telling you about the car trouble
or the dream with the impossible staircase.

You weren't really listening.
You were watching the way
she wrapped both hands around the warmth,
the way some people hold onto railings.

This is step one, though calling it a step
implies you have a choice,
as if you could turn back now
and find the stairs still there behind you.

Step two: admit that you've been leaving things—
a book, a sweater, a comment
about the weather that lasts too long—
the way birds drop twigs, building something
neither of you has agreed to name.

By step three you'll notice
your body has developed opinions
about which direction is north.
North being wherever she happens to be standing.

This is not magic. This is just
the inner ear recalibrating,
the heart doing what hearts do
when they encounter evidence of another heart.

Someone should have warned you
that love is mainly a series of observations—
the way he reads cereal boxes,
the angle of her handwriting,
how they apologize to furniture when they bump into it.

You collect these details
the way some people collect stamps,
mounting them carefully in the album of your attention.

Step four might be the hardest:
let them see you without your clever lines prepared,
without the armor of your best stories.
Just you, standing in the kitchen at midnight,
eating cereal from the box like a confession.

If they stay, if they grab a handful too
and lean against the counter
as if they've been doing this for years,
you're in trouble.

The good kind of trouble,
the kind that requires rearranging
your entire idea of Tuesday.

I could tell you about step five—
the negotiations with fear,
the small treaty signed between
your desire to run and your inability to move—

but you're probably already there,
standing in your kitchen,
holding the mug with the crack,
thinking about texting something
you'll revise four times before sending.

Here's what I know:
falling in love is less about the falling
and more about the moment you realize
you've been walking on air for three weeks
and forgot to panic.

It's deciding that the crack in the cup
isn't a flaw but a history,
and history is just another word
for "everything that led to now."

So make the coffee.
Send the text.
Let the mug hold what it's meant to hold,
despite the fracture, because of it.

This is the final step,
the one that repeats every morning:
choosing to believe
that broken things can still carry love
from one person's hands to another's,
still warm, still enough.

The Beautiful .
Created on November 17th, 2025
I want to tell you about the period,
that small black dot we've been placing
at the end of our sentences since grade school,
how it sits there like a tiny stone
that holds everything in place.

You might think I'm talking about punctuation,
and I am, but I'm also talking about
the period at the end of summer
when the light begins to slant differently
and you notice the first yellow leaf
spinning down from the maple tree.

Or the period at the end of a phone call
with someone you love, that moment
when you've both said goodbye
but neither of you has hung up yet,
and you can hear them breathing
on the other end of the line.

There's a period at the end of childhood,
though you don't see it coming—
one day you're riding your bike
down the center of the street
with no hands, and the next day
you're worried about the mortgage.

I think about the period my father placed
at the end of his stories, the way he'd pause
and look at us around the dinner table,
making sure we understood
before moving on to dessert.
Now there's a period at the end of his voice,
which I can only hear in my memory,
getting fainter each year.

But here's what makes the period beautiful:
it's not really an ending, is it?
It's more like a breath between thoughts,
a momentary pause before the next sentence
rushes in with its cargo of words.

The period is the shape of completeness,
a perfect circle, nothing missing,
nothing left unsaid—or at least
nothing more that needs saying
right now, at this particular instant.

I suppose that's why calligraphers
take such care with their periods,
pressing the nib down just so,
making that small dark pool of ink
that says: here, this is finished,
this idea has come to rest,
you can stop holding your breath now.

And maybe that's what makes any ending beautiful—
not the finality of it, but the way it gives us
permission to pause, to let something settle,
before we lean forward again into the mystery
of the next sentence, which is already forming
on our lips, already reaching toward
its own necessary and beautiful conclusion.
The Sasquatch Believer
Created on November 16th, 2025
I want to tell you about the plaster cast
sitting on my desk next to the coffee mug,
the one that holds the imprint of something's foot—

seventeen inches long, five toes spread wide
like the fingers of a hand reaching up
from the bottom of a lake.

My wife calls it my paperweight of delusion,
but I call it evidence, the way a man
might call a single strand of hair a love letter.

You have to understand, I've been looking
for thirty years now, every weekend
driving into the mountains with my camera

and a bag of apples because I read somewhere
that Sasquatch has a fondness for fruit,
which makes sense when you think about it—

who among us doesn't want something sweet
after a long day of avoiding civilization,
of being the world's most successful introvert?

I've collected hair samples, analyzed scat,
spent thousands of hours reviewing footage
of what might be a creature or might be Gary

from the hiking club in a gorilla suit.
But I believe. I believe the way a priest
believes in God, without ever shaking His hand.

And last Tuesday, I finally saw him—
eight feet tall, covered in matted fur,
standing beside my tent at dawn.

We locked eyes for what felt like an hour
but was probably three seconds, and then
he spoke to me, his voice like gravel and honey:

"I've been trying to reach you about
your car's extended warranty."

And reader, I ran.

Two Silhouettes in the Dark
Created on November 16th, 2025
I was thinking about silhouettes the other day,
how they are really just the outlines of bodies
who have stepped between a light source and a wall,

and how two of them standing in the dark
are actually four things at once—
two actual people with their complicated insides,

their grocery lists and lower back pain,
and two flat black cutouts that belong to shadow puppetry
and the opening credits of Alfred Hitchcock films.

It's the kind of thing that makes you wonder
if we are all just interruptions of light,
brief obstacles in the path of photons

that have traveled ninety-three million miles
only to be stopped by your torso
or the back of your head.

And then there's the question of what the two silhouettes
are doing in the dark in the first place—
are they looking at each other or away?

Are they about to embrace or just happened
to be standing near each other when the lights went out,
the way strangers do in an elevator when the power fails,

suddenly aware of breathing,
the small sounds of existence in close quarters?

I like to think they are philosophical silhouettes,
pondering their own flatness,
how they have been reduced to edges,

all their inner complexity—kidneys, memories,
the ability to whistle—completely irrelevant
to their current two-dimensional predicament.

Maybe one silhouette is saying to the other,
"Isn't it strange that we are most visible
when we are least ourselves?"

And the other silhouette, being equally philosophical,
might reply, "Yes, but at least in the dark
no one can see us blushing about it."
The Cartomancer at Breakfast
Created on November 16th, 2025
I should tell you that I'm not very good at this,
shuffling these oversized cards at the kitchen table
while my tea grows cold in its blue cup.

The Fool keeps turning up, which seems about right,
and I'm never sure if the Tower means
my life is about to collapse or if it's just referring
to the stack of unpaid bills listing near the toaster.

You'd think by now I'd have figured out
whether to read the future in these painted figures
or simply admire how the medieval artist
made the hangman look so peaceful,
dangling there upside down like a bat at rest,
reconsidering the world from a different angle.

My wife Carolee says you have to trust your intuition,
but my intuition is usually telling me
to have another cookie or avoid that phone call,
so I'm not confident it's the best guide
for navigating swords and pentacles.

Still, there's something companionable
about sitting here with these enigmatic strangers—
the Empress in her pomegranate garden,
Death on his pale horse who might just mean
it's time to clean out the garage,
the Two of Cups suggesting that love
is simply two people agreeing to drink from adjacent vessels.

Maybe the real trick is not to play them right at all
but to let them play you, the way a good book
rearranges your thoughts without asking permission,
the way the morning light decides which objects
in the room deserve to be illuminated.

So I'll leave the Star card face-up on the table,
that naked woman pouring water into water,
and finish my tea, which has achieved
the perfect temperature for revelation—
not too hot for truth, not too cold for hope.

Behind the Waterfall
Created on November 15th, 2025
As I looked at this photograph,
it reminded me of the small cave
I found last summer behind a waterfall in Yosemite.
How I had to wade through knee-deep water
and duck under a curtain of falling white
to discover this pocket of stillness
carved out by centuries of patient erosion.

It occurred to me, standing there
with my back against the cool stone,
that this might be what consciousness is like—
a dry space behind the constant cascade
of experience, a place where you can stand
and watch the torrent without being swept away,
which sounds more philosophical than I intended,
but there I was, soaked to the waist,
thinking about Plato and his cave,
wondering if he'd ever actually stood in one.

The funny thing about being behind a waterfall
is how the world looks when filtered through
all that moving water—
blurred, softer somehow, as if someone
had smudged the edges of reality
with their thumb.

I could see people on the other side,
just shapes really, walking along the path,
taking their selfies, checking their phones,
living their lives in the bright noise
of the ordinary world, while I stood hidden
in this chamber of mist and echo,
neither here nor there exactly,
a witness with wet shoes and a growing sense
that I'd stumbled into some kind of metaphor
I wasn't quite equipped to handle.

You spend your whole life on one side
of the cascade, don't you?
Moving through the days, getting things done,
checking items off lists, and then one day
you duck behind the curtain and realize
you've been looking at everything backwards—
or maybe forwards for the first time—
and the sound of all that falling water
is actually quite beautiful from this side,
this quiet side, where you can see
how temporary it all is, how provisional,
how the light breaks apart in the spray
and reforms into something you could almost
mistake for meaning.

I stayed there longer than I should have,
long enough that my legs went numb with cold,
long enough to understand why hermits
choose caves, why mystics seek out
these liminal spaces between the world
and whatever's behind it.

But eventually you have to step back through,
don't you? Back into the noise and motion,
back to the parking lot and the drive home,
carrying with you this small sadness—
not quite grief, more like nostalgia
for a place you only just discovered,
knowing you'll never see things
quite the same way again,
now that you've stood behind the veil
and looked out.

What the Cold Teaches Us About Warmth
Created on November 15th, 2025
I remember how my daughter stood
at the window watching snow the way I used to,
nose pressed to the glass, breath making clouds
that bloomed and faded like thoughts
she hadn't learned to name yet.

Fifty years ago now, maybe more—
the arithmetic of memory gets unreliable
when you've crossed seventy-six winters.

My son was in the kitchen arguing with his mother
about the proper ratio of marshmallows to cocoa,
a debate that had been running in our family
since my own father started it,
though none of us would admit we were really arguing about
whose memory of childhood tasted sweeter,
whose version of the past got to survive.

This is what winter gave us then—
those rooms full of people I'd have walked through fire for
but mostly just walked through ordinary cold for,
scraping ice off windshields at dawn
so I could drive to their houses,
shoveling paths so they wouldn't slip,
layering myself in wool and worry
before I ventured out to where they were,
back when my knees didn't predict the weather
and my hands could grip a shovel without shaking.

My brother called one night from two states away—
he's gone now, ten years this March—
to tell me he remembered the year
our furnace broke and we all slept
in the living room like refugees from our own bedrooms,
how Dad kept the fireplace going all night,
how we woke to find him still awake at 5 a.m.,
feeding logs into the flames as if he were
feeding us directly, which I suppose he was,
the way I fed my own children,
the way they feed theirs now.

Loss had a way of teaching you
what you should have been grateful for all along—
the sound of my wife's slippers on the stairs,
gone silent now these seven years,
the particular way my father cleared his throat
before telling a story we'd all heard before,
the weight of a sleeping child in my arms,
heavy as a blessing, warm as proof
that I'd done at least this one thing right,
though my daughter's forty-eight now
and my son just turned fifty last month,
and I wonder if they remember any of this
the way I'm remembering it.

But here's what I've learned about grief—
it doesn't erase love, it just moves it
into a different room of the house,
makes it more urgent somehow, the way the cold
made you appreciate every source of heat:
the radiator's tick and hiss,
the dog curled against your legs—
three dogs ago now, all of them gone—
my wife's laugh from the next room
where she was trying to teach our grandson
the card game my uncle taught me
sixty winters before, her voice
living on in my daughter's voice now,
in this moment I'm reconstructing
from the fragments that haven't yet
slipped through the holes in my memory,
in that house that winter had turned
into a vessel for everyone we'd loved,
the absent and the present
gathered there together like the last
warmth at the center of the year.

So yes, I was grateful then—
though I didn't know enough to say it,
didn't understand that those ordinary moments
were the whole point, were everything—
for the cold that drove us together,
for the people who stayed, for the ones
who left us their recipes and their gestures,
for my children who now stand
at their own windows watching snow,
maybe remembering this, maybe remembering me,
carrying forward this inheritance
of cocoa arguments and card games,
of bodies huddled close against the dark,
of love that outlasted everything, even winter,
even us, even the ones who thought
we'd live forever, back when winter
was just weather, before we understood
it was a gift, a gathering, a way
of teaching us to hold each other
while we could, while we still had hands
that worked, and hearts that hadn't yet
learned everything they needed to know
about letting go.
Author's Notes: This poem is a work of fiction (mostly) written from my imagined perspective as the elder narrator.
What We Agree To
Created on November 14th, 2025
I've been thinking about those two words lately,
how they sound like the smallest possible answer
to the largest possible question,
like saying "sure" when someone asks if you believe in gravity.

I do. As if we're confirming something already true,
the way you might nod when the waitress asks
if you want coffee, except the coffee is the rest of your life
and you haven't seen the menu.

My neighbor asked me once what it felt like to say them,
standing there in front of everyone you know
and some people you don't, aunts who still call you
by your childhood nickname, your grandfather
who tells the same three stories at every gathering.

I told him it felt like signing a contract
written in a language you almost understand,
or agreeing to take a trip with someone
who has the map but you have the compass,
and neither of you is entirely sure which direction is north.

Because "I do" doesn't mean "I understand"
or "I've figured this out" or "I promise never to leave
wet towels on the bathroom floor,"
though that would be a nice bonus.

It means something more like "I'm willing to find out,"
which is less romantic than the songs suggest
but probably more honest, the way a house key
is less exciting than a love letter
but more useful when it's raining.

I think about those words now when I'm loading the dishwasher
or choosing paint colors for the hallway
or trying to remember if we're out of milk—
all these tiny agreements inside the larger one,
little "I do's" scattered through the ordinary hours
like breadcrumbs that lead you home.

The phrase itself is so plain, so unadorned,
like a wooden chair or a glass of water,
functional and humble, doing its job
without calling attention to itself,
which is maybe the point—

that the biggest promises should sound the smallest,
that forever should fit inside two syllables,
that love should be as simple as a handshake
and as complicated as everything that follows,
which is breakfast and arguments and forgiveness
and the way you learn to sleep beside another person,
their breath becoming part of the room's weather,
the landscape you wake to every morning,
familiar and surprising, known and unknowable,

like those two words we spoke once
and keep speaking, quietly, without speaking,
in every door we open, every light we turn off,
every time we come home to the same address,
the same face across the table,
the same answer to the same impossible question:
yes, still, I do.
Skies Within Skies
Created on November 14th, 2025
I was sitting in a chair by the window
with a cup of coffee and a book
when I came across the line about the brain
being wider than the sky,
and I have to admit I looked up
at the actual sky just then,
which was doing its usual thing—
a few clouds drifting by like aimless shoppers,
a jet trail dissolving into nothing.

This reclusive poet says the brain can contain the sky,
hold it inside with room to spare,
and I suppose she's right,
because there I was with the whole Friday sky
tucked into my head,
clouds and all,
plus the memory of last Friday's sky,
and the sky from when I was seven,
standing in my grandmother's yard.

The thing is, this poem's without a title,
just a simple number 632
as if the idea was too large for a name,
or maybe she just forgot,
the way I forget where I put my keys
even though my brain is supposedly wider than the sky.
And yet—here's the thing—I couldn't argue with her.

Sitting here, holding her book,
I did contain her sky,
or some approximation of it. Clouds
she'd watched from Amherst in whatever year
were drifting through my skull like refugees,
making themselves at home in the same space
where I keep all my squalid little memories,
my grocery lists, my knowledge of bus routes,
the lyrics to songs I wish I could forget.

Perhaps that's what she meant: not that the mind
is grand, but that it's helplessly roomy—
a junk shop where the sublime and idiotic
share the same dusty shelf. The infinite sky
crammed in beside the argument you had
last Tuesday, and the thing you said at work
that made you cringe, and your mother's maiden name,
and the exact expression on that woman's face
when you walked past her on the stairs and couldn't
remember who she was, though clearly, clearly,
she remembered you. All of it in there,
taking up the same amount of space,
which is to say: no space at all, and yet
enough to make you wake at 3 a.m. and stare.

And now here I sit with her brain
inside my brain,
her sky inside my sky,
and I wonder if this is what she meant—
that we keep adding rooms,
one consciousness inside another,
like Russian dolls made of light and memory,
each one wider than we think,
each one holding more
than it has any business holding,
until you can't tell anymore
where the sky ends
and the thinking begins.

What to Tell Her About Muffin
Created on November 13th, 2025
She's standing in the doorway asking
where's Muffin, her jacket still on,
backpack dragging on the floor, and I'm thinking
how do I explain this without lying,
without making death sound like a trip
he's taking, some farm upstate where dogs
run free and no one ever comes back?

I've been rehearsing all week—gentle
versions, clinical versions, the truth
dressed up in soft language like a body
in a casket wearing someone else's clothes.
But she's six and waiting and I can feel
the weight of what I have to say
pressing against my ribs like grief,

like the way Muffin used to lean
his whole body into my legs
when he wanted something, anything,
just to be close. She loved that dog
the way kids love—completely, without
the knowledge that what you love
can stop breathing while you sleep.

So I tell her: Muffin's heart got old.
His body stopped working. He's gone.
And I watch her face do that thing
faces do when they're learning
the world can take things back,
that love doesn't guarantee
keeping, that nothing does.

She asks if it hurt and I lie—
I say no, though I don't know,
though I held him while the vet
found the vein, felt him go slack
and heavy in my arms like a coat
falling off a hanger. What else
can you tell a child? The truth

is a luxury we can't afford yet.
She's crying now, asking why,
and I want to say: because bodies
fail, because time is a bastard,
because every good thing you love
will leave you or you'll leave it
and that's just how it goes—

but instead I pull her close,
let her sob into my shirt,
her small hands gripping my arms
like I'm the only solid thing left
in a world that just learned
to shift and crack beneath her.
Later, she asks if we can get another dog

and I almost laugh at how quickly
she moves through it, this first lesson
in loss, while I'm still standing here
holding his empty bowl, unable
to throw it away, unable to fill it,
caught between what was
and what will never be again.

Outside, the street is loud with living—
cars, voices, someone's music
bleeding through an open window.
Inside, we're learning the same
old human trick: how to stand upright
when the ground keeps opening,
how to breathe through the breaking.
The Centipede in My Tea Cup
Created on November 13th, 2025
You know how sometimes you reach for a cup
without looking, your hand already anticipating
the smooth curve of porcelain,
and then you glance down—

There he is, all legs and segments,
a miniature train of body parts
curled at the bottom of what was supposed to be
my vessel for morning tea.

I counted once, or tried to,
those pairs of legs rippling along his sides
like tiny oars on a Roman galley,
but lost track somewhere after twenty.

The ancient Greeks would have made him a monster,
given him a name that translates to "hundred-footed,"
though I suspect they too
would have been lousy at the actual math.

He seems unbothered by his predicament,
content to patrol the white interior,
a security guard making rounds
in the world's smallest, emptiest arena.

I think of all the cups I've drunk from—
the cracked ones, the inherited ones,
the one I bought in that shop in Maine
twenty years ago on a trip I barely remember—

and how none of them ever held anything
quite like this, this breathing comma of chitin,
this ambulatory ellipsis navigating
the place where my lips were meant to go.

So I carry him outside on the porch,
watch him pour himself over the rim
and disappear into the gap
between two floorboards,

then return to rinse the cup three times,
maybe four, before filling it with Earl Grey,
sitting down to wonder why
we remember the interruptions more than the routine,

why the centipede gets a poem
while a thousand ordinary mornings
slip away unnoticed,
like water through a drain.
When Summer Brings Autumn Around
Created on November 12th, 2025
I want to tell you about the afternoon
when summer finally introduced me to autumn,
not at some grand party with falling leaves
and pumpkin spice everything,
but on a Tuesday, in my backyard,
while I was reading the same paragraph
for the third time.

Summer came over first, of course,
still wearing her bright sundress,
her hair smelling of chlorine and cut grass,
and she said, You'll like my friend—
she's quieter than me, drinks tea instead of lemonade,
prefers cardigans to tank tops.

And there was autumn, standing by the fence,
her hands in the pockets of a jacket
I didn't know I'd been missing,
looking at the tomatoes on the vine
the way you look at old photographs,
with a mixture of affection and goodbye.

Summer did most of the talking, naturally,
telling stories about our long days together,
the 6 a.m. light, the 9 p.m. dinners on the deck,
while autumn just smiled and nodded,
occasionally pointing out how the evenings
were coming earlier now, softer somehow,
wrapping themselves around the neighborhood
like a shawl someone places on your shoulders
when you didn't realize you were cold.

I found myself leaning toward autumn's voice,
the way she spoke about the changing light,
about harvests and wool blankets,
about the pleasure of going inward
after months of staying out.

Summer didn't seem hurt, exactly.
She knew this was how it worked.
She'd be back, she said, waving as she left,
leaving her sunglasses on my patio table
as a promise, a deposit on next June.

But autumn stayed, settling into the chair beside me,
picking up a book of her own,
and we sat there together in the gentle afternoon,
the sky that particular shade of blue
that only exists in these in-between days,
when one friend is waving goodbye
and another is just saying hello.
