Winter's First Lesson
Created on November 12th, 2025
I want you to picture this:
a single pane of kitchen glass
starting to cloud in the lower right corner,
as if the cold is learning to write its name
in a language of dew.

The coffee maker sighs its final drip,
and I notice the cat curling tighter on the rug,
a furry comma between morning and what comes next.

Outside, the pine tree shivers—
not from wind, but from the thought of ice,
the way a student might flinch before a pop quiz
on seasonal etiquette.

You’d think after eons of practice,
winter would remember its entrance,
but here it is, fumbling with the doorknob,
leaving damp fingerprints on the handle.

I pull a sweater over my head,
the wool smelling faintly of cedar,
and realize I’m wearing preparation,
not yet necessity—
a costume for a play that hasn’t quite begun.

The radio plays a song I’ve never heard,
the lyrics about trains and stars
and other things that keep moving
while the world holds its breath.

And I smile because the absurdity is perfect:
a planet spinning through space,
and winter still learning to tie its shoes,
one frozen lace at a time.

Later, I’ll write a note to myself
on the fogged-up mirror:
Notice how the ordinary conspires
to make the strange feel like home.

But for now, I just watch the window’s edge
whiten like the margins of an unwritten page,
knowing the story will come,
but not yet—
not quite yet.
Is Chaos Chaotic?
Created on November 11th, 2025
The question keeps me up at night,
though Chaos himself sleeps soundly
in the armchair, snoring with perfect regularity,
his breath keeping time like a metronome.

I've been watching him for years now,
taking notes in the margins of my life,
and I'm starting to suspect he's not
what his name suggests.

Yes, he knocks things over—
the vase, my plans for Tuesday, the carefully constructed
arguments I make about the nature of order—
but always at precisely the moment I need them knocked over.

He arrives uninvited to dinner parties
but somehow improves them,
spilling red wine in patterns that look like constellations,
asking the questions everyone was thinking but too polite to voice.

I used to think he was random,
a force of pure disorder loose in my living room,
but now I see the way he arranges disasters
with the care of a florist composing a bouquet.

Last week he hid my car keys
which made me walk to the store
where I ran into an old friend
I'd been meaning to call for months.

Yesterday he erased the document I was working on,
and I rewrote it from memory—
better this time, clearer,
as if losing it had been necessary all along.

I'm beginning to understand
that Chaos might be the most orderly thing in my life,
that his disruptions follow patterns I'm only now learning to read,
like a language I've been speaking without knowing the grammar.

Maybe chaos is just order
that hasn't revealed its structure yet,
the way a seed looks like nothing
before it understands it's meant to be a tree.

Or maybe I'm overthinking this,
and Chaos is just sitting in that chair,
grinning at my attempts to make sense of him,
pleased that I'm finally asking the right question—

not what is chaos,
but who,
and whether the difference between disorder and design
is really just a matter of how far back you stand.

Here Lies Me
Created on November 11th, 2025
I've been imagining my headstone for weeks now,
and in every version, there's a typo—
"Here Lays Me" or maybe "Hear Lies Me,"
the kind of mistake that would make my English teacher
rise from her own grave just to shake her head.

It's not that I think death will be careless,
more that I was careless in life,
the sort of person who wrote "definately"
well into his thirties, who could never remember
if it was "affect" or "effect" that changed the outcome.

I picture tourists in the cemetery,
pointing at my stone with a kind of glee,
taking photos to text their friends—
"Look at this poor guy, couldn't even spell his way
out of existence properly."

Maybe they'll think it was intentional,
some kind of postmodern commentary,
me making a statement about the fluidity of language
from my permanent position in the ground,
when really it was just Harold at the monument shop
having a bad day with his chisel.

"Here Lies Me, Husband and Farther"—
that's the one I dread most, being called a farther,
as if I spent my whole life moving away from things,
which, to be fair, isn't entirely inaccurate
given my history with commitment and vegetables.

Or worse: "Beloved Son and Spelling Bee Runnerup,"
immortalizing that moment in fourth grade
when I forgot the second 'c' in "broccoli"
and had to sit down in front of everyone,
already practicing for this final sitting down.

The truth is, I'd probably forgive the error—
I forgave myself for so many small mistakes,
for burnt toast and missed exits,
for conversations where I said exactly the wrong thing
and had to live with it for decades after.

So let them carve "Here Lies Me" into the granite,
grammatically questionable, technically incorrect,
a fitting monument to someone who spent his life
trying to get things approximately right,
landing somewhere in the vicinity of truth,
close enough to be understood,
which is all any of us can really hope for
when we're trying to explain ourselves to strangers
standing in the sun, squinting at our particulars,
wondering what we meant, and whether we meant well.
That is Bull said the Bull
Created on November 10th, 2025
I like to think the first bull to hear the phrase
"That is Bull" must have been standing
in a field somewhere, chewing some grass,
when a farmer pointed at a broken wagon wheel
and said those exact words.

And the bull looked up, confused,
wondering what he had to do with any of this—
the wheel, the wagon, the man's frustration—
as if his entire species had just been conscripted
into the service of human disappointment.

Now every time someone dismisses a story
or waves away an exaggeration,
there's a bull in the metaphor, standing there
like an innocent bystander dragged into court,
guilty by association with... what exactly?

Stubbornness? The tendency to charge?
Or maybe it's the other thing,
the barnyard thing we're too polite to mention
in daily conversation, though we're happy enough
to invoke it when we think someone's not telling the truth.

Either way, I feel for the bulls,
how they've become shorthand for nonsense,
their muscular bodies and dark eyes
somehow containing all our human doubt,
all the times we've heard something that didn't add up.

And I wonder if somewhere in a pasture right now
there's a bull thinking, "That is Human,"
watching us scurry around with our little schemes,
our elaborate excuses, our wagon wheels that keep breaking,
trying to figure out what we have to do with anything at all.
Reading Dr. Seuss at Seventy
Created on November 10th, 2025
I found myself this morning reading "The Sneetches"
to no one in particular, just the coffee maker
and the morning light slanting through the kitchen window,
and I got to thinking about the machinery of it all—

that contraption Sylvester McMonkey McBean rolls into town,
the one that adds stars to bellies for three dollars,
then removes them for ten.

It's the kind of machine that couldn't exist
in our world of physics and biology,
but exists perfectly well in the world
where we actually live, the one where we line up
to have our stars added or subtracted,
where we pay good money to be told
we're finally on the right side of things.

You probably remember reading this as a child,
the way I do, and thinking it was about
some distant problem, some silly creatures
on some far-off beaches, not about you
sitting there in your pajamas with your juice box,
already learning the rules.

But here's what gets me now, reading it
while the coffee brews and the refrigerator hums—
the Star-Belly Sneetches and the Plain-Belly Sneetches
eventually can't tell each other apart.
They've been through the machine so many times,
adding and removing, removing and adding,
that no one knows who started with stars
and who started without.

And that's when McBean packs up and leaves,
rolling his contraption off to the next beach,
because there's no profit in creatures
who've finally figured out they're all the same.

The coffee maker beeps.
The sun has moved higher in the window.
And I'm standing here thinking about
all the machines I've walked through,
voluntary and otherwise,
all the stars I've had added and taken away,
and wondering if, like the Sneetches,
I've been through enough times now
to forget which version of myself
I started out as,

which might be exactly the point
of reading Dr. Seuss at seventy,
in your kitchen, alone, realizing
that the books we thought were teaching children
were actually teaching the adults we'd become,
if we were paying attention.
The Girl with the Bright Yellow Backpack
Created on November 8th, 2025
I used to see her every morning at the bus stop,
the girl with Down syndrome and the bright yellow backpack
decorated with cartoon characters I couldn't quite name,
standing next to what I assumed was her mother or grandmother,
someone patient, someone who seemed to know
the exact number of steps from the front door to the curb.

She would wave at the crossing guard,
at the man walking his terrier,
at me on the occasions when I happened to glance up
from my phone or my coffee or whatever urgent emptiness
I was attending to that particular week.
Her hand moved through the air like a small flag
or a bird that hadn't quite learned the mechanics of flight.

I suppose I waved back sometimes,
offered that casual nod we reserve for people
we see regularly but don't actually know,
the way you might acknowledge a painting
that hangs in a hallway you pass through
on your way to somewhere else.

Then one Tuesday or Wednesday—I can't remember which—
the bus stop was empty.
And the next day. And the next.
I found myself looking for that yellow backpack
the way you might look for a missing button
or a word that sits just beyond the edge of your tongue.

I never learned her name.
I never asked why she stopped appearing,
whether they moved away or changed their route
or something else I don't want to catalogue here.
But sometimes now, when I'm standing in line
or waiting for a light to change,
I think about all the small, persistent greetings
I failed to return with anything resembling attention,
how her hand kept moving through the air
like someone conducting an orchestra
only she could hear,
and how I never once thought to ask
what music she was listening to.

A Field Guide to Her Disappearances
Created on November 8th, 2025
I've been trying to identify her for weeks now,
the way you might try to name a bird
you keep glimpsing at the edge of the yard—
there for a moment in the hydrangea,
then gone before you can get the binoculars focused.

Wallflower, I think at first, noting
how she positions herself near doorways at gatherings,
how she seems to bloom only in the margins,
requiring the architecture of a room
to hold her upright, to give her
some solid surface against which to exist.

But then I remember my grandmother's garden,
those cheerful little wallflowers with their bright faces,
tough as nails, thriving in the cracks of stone,
and I realize that's not quite right—
there's something more fragile here,
something that flinches.

Shrinking violet, then. Yes.
The name itself a kind of disappearing act.
I picture the Victorian lady who named it,
probably admiring its modesty,
the way it hides its purple face in heart-shaped leaves
as if embarrassed by its own beauty,
as if existing were a kind of imposition
on the world's good manners.

But here's what gets me:
violets aren't really shrinking at all.
They're taking over my lawn as we speak,
sending out runners underground,
staging a quiet revolution
while everyone's distracted by the roses.

So maybe she's both, or neither,
or maybe I'm the one who needs
a better field guide, one that accounts
for the complicated taxonomy of human shyness,
the way a person can be simultaneously
fragile and persistent,
hidden and utterly present,
the way she can disappear into a corner
and somehow still be the most alive thing in the room—

a small purple bloom you almost overlook
until you crouch down, really look,
and realize the whole damn lawn
has been turning violet while you weren't paying attention.

Quixotic Q
Created on November 7th, 2025
I want you to imagine the letter Q
nestled in a bowl of alphabet soup—
your spoon lifting it where it bobbles,
suspicious of its tail, that curl that’s superfluous.

I have always been partial to Q’s curiosity,
the way it insists on company—U at its side always,
like an anxious friend who can’t bear silence alone,
or a host fetching an extra chair for the party.

You find it in quirks—quiet quirks—
in queenly quilts, quaint questions that start with “why,”
in quiver of wings at dusk, in quarts of lemonade spilled by kids
running too fast in the backyard, squealing.

And yet, Q can vex us—clunky in crosswords,
quandary in spelling bees, quirky in accents—
it demands respect, though it’s small, eccentric;
we forgive it because it gives us something curious to love.

So here’s to the Q—imperfect, peculiar,
a letter that, in its oddness, reminds us
we need oddness, just as we need “you.”

Long Time Passing
Created on November 5th, 2025
I was thinking about the crickets last night,
how they used to fill the August evenings
with their persistent orchestra,
rubbing their legs together in the grass
like tiny violinists tuning up for a performance
that never quite began, yet never ended either.

Where have all the crickets gone? I ask
the way the Kingston Trio used to ask
about flowers and soldiers and young girls,
each verse circling back to long time passing,
which is what I'm doing now, I suppose,
standing on the porch with my coffee,
listening to the silence where the crickets should be.

My daughter says it's pesticides.
My neighbor says it's climate change.
The internet says both, or neither, or possibly
they're just hiding, waiting for some signal
we can't hear, some cosmic all-clear
that will bring them back in their multitudes.

I remember when you couldn't walk through grass
without launching a dozen of them into flight,
those awkward leaps, their inability to land gracefully—
the way they were always somewhere else
by the time you looked down at where they'd been.
Now the lawn sits there, silent as a library,
which is another thing that's changing, come to think of it.

Maybe the crickets left with the lightning bugs,
packed their bags and caught the same train
to wherever disappeared things go—
that realm where payphones and handwritten letters
compare notes with manual typewriters
and actual film that you had to have developed
to see if your pictures came out.

Long time passing, indeed.
Though I suppose the crickets don't measure time
the way we do, in decades and generations,
mourning what we've lost while checking our phones
for news of other losses.
They just rubbed their legs and sang,
which seems, in retrospect, like a reasonable way
to spend an evening.

So here I am, listening for them,
the way I listen for other sounds that used to be:
the particular silence before the phone rings,
the click of a pen when someone's thinking,
the rustle of newspaper pages in the morning.
All of it passing, long time passing,
and me on the porch with my coffee,
wondering if the crickets will ever come back,
or if we'll just learn to live without them,
the way we've learned to live without so much else,
until someone younger asks, "What's a cricket?"
and I'll have to explain about the concerts in the grass,
the summer symphonies we used to take for granted,
back when long time passing was just a song.
Author's Notes: contest Where Have All the Crickets Gone? - Danna Hobart - Image my creation using photoshop and other illustration tools.
The Flaming Bird: How to Host Thanksgiving
Created on November 4th, 2025
I want to tell you about the turkey that caught fire,
not just singed or smoking but genuinely ablaze,
a flaming bird my uncle carried through the kitchen
like an Olympic torch, trailing smoke and profanity
while my aunt threw dish towels at him
as if he were a piñata filled with disaster.

This was after the dog ate half the stuffing,
before we discovered it, of course, so we all sat there
complimenting my grandmother on her bold new recipe,
nodding thoughtfully at what turned out to be
Kibbles 'n Bits mixed with sage and someone's good intentions.

You should have seen us pretending to enjoy it,
forkful after forkful of what amounted to
a crime against poultry and basic human decency,
my cousin actually saying, "Is that thyme I'm tasting?"
when what he was tasting was definitely Purina.

The mashed potatoes were actually fine
until my nephew asked why they were sweet,
and we realized my mother had used vanilla extract
instead of milk—an honest mistake if you don't read labels
or believe that all white liquids are interchangeable.

I sat there at the head of the table,
the one who'd promised to host a proper Thanksgiving,
watching my father eat candied potatoes that were just potatoes,
my sister picking at her salad because she'd become vegan
that morning, apparently, and I'd made nothing without butter,
and I thought about all those magazine photographs,
those tables laden with golden plenty and families
who look like they've never said a cruel word to each other.

But then my uncle started laughing about the flaming turkey,
and my nephew spit out his vanilla potatoes,
and someone opened the second bottle of wine,
and we were all laughing, even my grandmother,
who was laughing so hard she had to take out her teeth,
which made us laugh harder, which made the dog bark,
which made my nephew knock over the cranberry sauce—
the one thing that had turned out perfectly.

We ordered pizza around seven.
We ate it on paper plates in the living room,
and my father said it was the best Thanksgiving he could remember,
and I think he might have meant it,
or maybe he was just drunk on vanilla fumes and relief,
but I sat there on the couch, holding my slice,
looking around at these people I'd somehow failed
to feed properly, these faces I'd known my whole life,
and I realized that the disasters were just another way
of saying we were together, we were trying,
we were still showing up year after year
with our burnt offerings and our terrible timing,
and isn't that what gratitude looks like when you strip away
the centerpiece and the proper serving dishes—
just us, laughing at our own magnificent incompetence,
grateful no one called the fire department,
grateful the smoke detector batteries were dead,
grateful for pizza delivery and for each other,
especially for each other, God help us all.
Author's Notes: I created this poem by accident. I started writing about an actual Thanksgiving experience (fact), but as I got further into it, all sorts of additional thoughts (fiction) came to mind. When I was finished embellishing, this was the result. - Image is my creation using photoshop and other Illustration tools.
The Morning I Became Myopic
Created on November 2nd, 2025
I woke up squinting at the bedroom ceiling
which had become a pleasant blur of white,
the light fixture a soft explosion of brightness
like a small sun deciding to take up residence overhead.

Downstairs, I poured orange juice into what I hoped
was a glass and not the sugar bowl,
navigated by memory and faith toward the coffee maker,
that dark, humming altar I've worshipped at for years.

The newspaper had transformed into abstract art—
black marks dancing across white space
in patterns that suggested meaning
the way clouds suggest ships or the faces of old lovers.

I thought of Mr. Magoo, that cheerful catastrophe,
mistaking a bear for his nephew Waldo,
walking through construction sites as if they were parks,
his confidence inversely proportional to his vision.

And here I was, his philosophical cousin,
feeling my way through the familiar rooms,
each doorway a small act of faith,
each step a tiny leap into the probably safe.

There's something liberating, I'll admit,
about the world losing its sharp edges,
the way a face across the room becomes
just the idea of a face, a suggestion of features.

Maybe this is how we should approach everything—
the future, the past, the contents of our own hearts—
with Magoo's unshakeable optimism,
squinting at the blur and calling it beautiful,

walking forward with arms outstretched,
assuming the best about what we cannot quite see,
trusting that the bear is actually Waldo,
that the cliff is actually a curb, that the world
will catch us before we fall too far.

Thanksgiving Day
Created on October 27th, 2025
I invite you in—take off your shoes, sit
at this kitchen table where I peel potatoes
and try to count my gratitude like these lumps of skin—
unwanted, earthy, each with its salt and slip.

I want to thank you for the dawn that yawns open the curtains,
for the coffee’s bitter first sip, for the spoon balanced
between trembling and relief.
Thank you for the neighbor’s car alarm at three
reminding me I’m alive, even when sleep has left the room.

Thank you for the sticky sheet of honey on the counter,
for bread that breaks in my hands, warm and flawed—
those crumbs are confessions, familiar in their mess.
I’m grateful for that cat’s arched back by the radiator, the way steam
bends its hair into flames.

Thank you, too, for mistakes—burned pies, misread maps,
for saying “I love you” when I mean “I’m sorry,” or vice versa,
for compassion’s awkward stumble. Behold the tapestry woven
from apologies and laughter, from small failures held together
by stubborn threads of belonging.

I offer you thanks as if laying a table: imperfect, sincere—
such gratitude cannot be grandiose. It is the feather in the teacup,
the crack in the porch step where grass insists on living.

Because thank you isn’t about having everything right—
it’s about recognizing that I’ve been given breath, sky,
wide open hands and sometimes trembling ones—
and still I choose to give back this: my thanks.
The Vow that Silenced the World
Created on October 24th, 2025
When first the words were spoken, no one heard
the trembling in his voice, the way his breath
caught on the syllables like cloth on thorns.
He stood before the gathered multitude
and swore an oath so absolute, so fierce,
that even wind stopped moving through the trees.
The vow was simple: never to forget
what had been done, what blood had soaked the ground,
what children’s voices ceased their singing when
the soldiers came at dawn with fire and steel.

He promised this: that silence would become
his weapon and his witness, that his tongue
would hold the weight of all those vanished names
until the earth itself acknowledged them.
And something in his bearing, in the way
his hands hung motionless beside his body,
convinced the crowd that he would keep his word.
They felt it like a change in temperature,
a sudden coldness spreading through the square
as if the sun had turned its face away.

The days that followed proved him true. He walked
among the living like a monument,
his lips pressed tight against the urge to speak
of weather, harvest, love, or daily bread.
When others tried to draw him into talk,
he’d shake his head and gesture toward the sky
where birds still flew but made no sound at all—
for they too had been caught within the spell
of his commitment, bound by sympathy
to honor what his silence represented.

The silence spread like winter across fields.
First dozens, then hundreds took the vow,
refusing speech until the world confessed
its complicity in what had come to pass.
The markets emptied of their usual noise,
the taverns lost their laughter and their songs,
and even children learned to play in quiet,
their games conducted through elaborate signs
and gestures that replaced the words they’d known.
The silence grew until it had a weight.

It pressed against the doors of government,
it filled the halls where judges passed their verdicts,
it seeped into the chambers where the rich
counted their gold and planned their next campaigns.
The powerful began to feel it then—
this absence that accused them more than words,
this void that held the shape of all their crimes.
They tried to break it, sending heralds out
to proclaim new laws, to threaten punishment,
but threats mean nothing when no one responds.

The world had never known such stillness. Trade
continued, but in gestures and in writing,
and something in the silence made men honest—
as if the absence of their voices forced
them to confront what they had always hidden
behind the noise of commerce and debate.
The vow had done what argument could not:
it made the guilty visible, exposed
the mechanisms of their cruelty,
and left them naked in the quiet air.

Some say the silence lasted seven years,
that finally the powerful confessed
and begged forgiveness from the voiceless crowds.
Others claim it never truly ended,
that even now, in certain places, people
refuse to speak of what they know too well,
that silence has become a kind of prayer,
a way of holding space for those who died
without the chance to tell their stories, those
whose names were swallowed by the earth and time.

But this much can be verified: the man
who took the first vow kept it till his death.
They found him in his garden, on his knees,
his mouth still closed around the words he’d saved
for thirty years, waiting for the moment
when speaking them would matter, when the world
had finally been silenced long enough
to hear what he had promised to preserve.
And in his hand, a list of names so long
it took three days of reading to complete.
The Chipmunk's Complaint
Created on October 23rd, 2025
Now WHY should I go out tonight
When everything in here's just right?
My green chair hugs me like a mitt,
This plaid blanket's a perfect fit!

I've got my pile of sunflower seeds
(That's ALL a cozy chipmunk needs),
The kettle's singing its red song,
The stove's been crackling all day long.

There's smoke and tea-smell in the air,
A mushroom sprouting by my chair,
Some autumn leaves I dragged inside,
A spider friend who likes to hide.

But YOU say, "Chip, go climb a tree!
Go scamper! Leap! Run wild and free!"
Well, I say nuts to all of that—
I'm staying here. And that is THAT.

Today My Mind Declared It Nostalgia Day
Created on October 22nd, 2025
This morning, I remembered how my daughter told me
the moon follows our car because it wants to play,
and I didn’t correct her.

I could have mentioned orbital mechanics,
the illusion of parallax, the way distant objects
seem to track our movement—
but why would I bring gravity to a child
who knows the moon gets lonely?

She also thought our dog spoke English
but kept quiet, saving his words
for when he really needed them,
and that clouds were practice drawings
God hadn’t erased yet.

I was thirty-five then, thinking about tax brackets,
the structural integrity of the deck,
whether that noise in the engine was serious,
whether I’d updated my password.
My daughter wondered if the moon
might like to come in for cookies.

I remember the day she asked why I looked sad
and I said I wasn’t, just tired,
which was a lie, and she knew it.
She said,
“Your face is doing the sad thing.”

And she was right.

Children are terrible at metaphor
but perfect at truth.

They haven’t learned yet that we’re supposed
to call loneliness “busy” or despair “fine,”
or to pretend the moon is only a rock
that doesn’t care about us.

Tonight when I go to bed and look back
across seventy-six years,
I’ll remember tucking her in—the small nightlight
casting a moon on the wall—how she asked,
“Dad, can the moon see through our roof?”
and I said, “Yes, absolutely,
it’s watching over you right now,”
and for once I wasn’t the one
turning something simple into a lie.

The Visiting Professor
Created on October 21st, 2025
I like to think of hate as a substitute teacher
who shows up one morning unannounced,
wearing a rumpled suit and carrying a briefcase
full of mimeographed handouts from 1952.

The regular teacher—let's call her compassion—
is out sick, or attending a conference,
or simply exhausted and sleeping late,
so hate takes attendance in her absence,

calling out names with a slight mispronunciation
that makes everyone feel a little less themselves.
The lesson plan is simple: here is the world
divided into us and them, the deserving and the rest.

Most students sit through the period
doodling in their margins, watching the clock,
knowing this isn't the real curriculum,
that tomorrow the regular teacher will return

with her usual assignments about listening
and the difficult grammar of forgiveness.
They file out when the bell rings, blinking
in the hallway's fluorescent light, already forgetting.

But there are always a few—you've seen them—
who copy every word into their notebooks,
who raise their hands for clarification,
who stay after class to ask if hate

will be back tomorrow, and the next day,
and could they possibly get independent study credit
because they've never had a teacher
who made so much sense, who finally explained

why they feel the way they do.
And hate, adjusting his glasses, smiling,
says of course, of course, pulls up a desk,
and begins the lesson again from the beginning.
Author's Notes: contest ~ The Power Of Hate ~ Empath ~ - Poetress Luna WC 235 - Image provided by host.
The Impatient Patient
Created on October 20th, 2025
I have been sitting in this waiting room so long
I have memorized the fish in the aquarium—
there are seven, though one hides behind the plastic castle
most of the time, which I respect. Opening and closing
their mouths as if they had something to say about being patient.

I have read about celebrities I've never heard of,
their beach bodies and their breakups,
and I have studied the anatomy poster on the wall
until I am nearly qualified to perform my own surgery.

The receptionist has looked up at me twice,
both times to smile apologetically,
a smile I have come to understand means
"You are still here, yes, we see you, you continue to exist."
Or was that a look of puzzlement: "I see you're still here"?

I wonder if this is a test,
if somewhere in a back room the doctor is watching me
on a closed-circuit television, taking notes:
Subject shows signs of fidgeting at the thirty-minute mark.
At 45 minutes, subject has checked phone six times.
By the 60-minute point, subject appears to be considering
whether the plant in the corner is real or silk.

I could be doing so many things right now—
I could be at home not doing them, which is different
from sitting here not doing them,
the way being alone is different from being lonely,
though both involve a certain quality of waiting.

Perhaps impatience is just a form of faith,
the belief that your life is happening somewhere else,
in some other room where you are supposed to be,
and all you need is for someone to call your name,
to open the door and wave you through
into the rest of your life, which has been delayed
but is surely, surely about to begin.
The Physics of Haunting
Created on October 20th, 2025
It occurred to me this afternoon,
as I was driving down the road,
watching the air shimmering above the asphalt,
that ghosts and heat have something in common—
they both insist on rising.

You can see it if you look closely,
the way warmth distorts the landscape behind it,
bending the mountains, making the ground waver
like something seen through water,
and I thought: Yes, this is how the dead must travel,
hitching rides on thermals,
drifting up from the basement furnace,
from the pilot light on the stove,
from the laptop warming your thighs.

No wonder they congregate in old houses
with their radiators clanking all night,
their ancient boilers groaning in the cellar.
It's not the history they're after,
not the creaky floorboards or the tragic past;
it's simply that these places are warmer,
better for levitation, more conducive
to the general upward drift of the unembodied.

And maybe this explains why we feel them
most often in winter, when we're burning,
everything we can—wood, gas, even
our own calories just to stay alive—
creating all these invisible elevators
for the departed to ride toward the ceiling,
toward the attic, toward whatever comes next.

I like to think of them up there,
my grandmother, my college roommate,
that dog I had as a kid,
all of them floating near the crown molding,
warm and weightless, rising on the very same
principle that lifts a hawk or a balloon,
waiting for us to turn up the thermostat
so they can climb a little higher,
drift a little farther from the cold ground
where we left them.

Dr. Chen's Practice
Created on October 20th, 2025
I want to tell you about Dr. Chen
who spends his mornings smoothing out wrinkles
on dogs whose ancestors were bred to have them.

It's a strange calling, I'll admit,
like being a professional eraser of history,
a reverse sculptor working against the original design.

The Shar-Pei puppies arrive with their excess skin
folded like expensive linen napkins
or those accordion files we keep in closets,

and Dr. Chen, with his steady hands and tiny scissors,
removes what centuries of Chinese breeders
so carefully cultivated—the folds that made them

look like worried philosophers, like old men
contemplating the nature of existence
while waiting for their dinner.

You have to wonder what the dogs think,
waking up from anesthesia with their new faces,
smooth as river stones, tight as drum skins.

Do they feel lighter? More aerodynamic?
Or do they miss the weight of all those wrinkles,
the way we might miss an old coat

we've finally thrown away, the pockets still holding
the shape of our hands, the collar remembering
the particular angle of our necks?

Dr. Chen tells me it's for their health—
the folds trap moisture, breed infection,
obscure their vision like heavy curtains.

And I believe him. I do.
But sometimes I think about those dogs
walking out of his office into the afternoon light,

their faces unfamiliar even to themselves,
wondering if beauty is always this practical,
this kind, this absolutely necessary.

The robin sings
Each morning
Right outside your window—
But you're scrolling,
Scrolling,
Scrolling through your phone.

The clouds make shapes:
A dragon!
A sailing ship!
A giant's face!
But you're looking down,
Down,
Down at the sidewalk cracks.

Your dog wags his tail
Wag-wag-wag-wag-wag
Every time you enter—
Pure joy on four paws!
But you're thinking about
Dinner,
Laundry,
Bills,
Tomorrow's meeting.

The stars come out
Twinkle-ific!
Spark-tacular!

Winking hello—

Gone inside,
Curtains closed,
Lights on.

Love taps you on the shoulder,
Whispers in your ear,
Leaves notes in your lunchbox,

To hear

To see

You're loved-loved-LOVED
Every single day.

Wondering

Wonder back.
Strange Familiar Room
Created on October 19th, 2025
I think it was a Friday when I first noticed
I had wandered off the map of myself
and into some unmarked territory
where the street signs were written in my handwriting
but I couldn't remember putting them there.

It's like when you say a word over and over—
"fork" or "elbow" or your own name—
until it becomes just a sound,
a collection of syllables that have forgotten
what they were supposed to mean.

Except this time the word is you,
and you've been repeating yourself so many times
you've worn away the definition.

I imagine this is what happens to mirrors
when they spend too much time looking at themselves,
which of course they can't help doing,
being mirrors. They must get tired
of their own reflections, the same silver face
staring back with that blank expression
that could mean anything or nothing at all.

Some people get lost in the woods.
Some people get lost in cities where every street
looks like the one they just turned from.
But I got lost in the hallways of my own thinking,
took a left at a memory, a right at a regret,
and suddenly I was in a room I'd never seen before
even though I'd built it myself, years ago,
and apparently had been living in it ever since.

The funny thing about getting lost in yourself
is that you're always exactly where you are.
You can't call out for help because you're the only one home.
You can't ask for directions because you're supposed to be
the one who knows the way.

So you sit down on the floor of this strange familiar room
and you wait, I suppose, for yourself to find you,
which is a bit like waiting for your shadow
to come around the corner and apologize
for following you all these years
without introducing itself properly.

The Stone Steps
Created on October 18th, 2025
I was admiring the front steps of a house
on my street this morning, the way
the marble has been worn down in the middle
by a century of comings and goings,
the stone hollowed out like a soup spoon.

You can see where everyone walked,
right up the center, avoiding the edges
the way we all do, following the path
of least resistance, or maybe just
the path everyone else has taken.

It made me think of my neighbor, Mrs. Chen,
who sits on her porch most afternoons
watching the street like it's a movie
she's seen before but doesn't mind seeing again.
Her face has that same quality—

weather-worn by years of tears,
smoothed down in places by grief
the way water shapes rock,
the way footsteps hollow out stone,
slowly, imperceptibly, until one day

you notice the depth of it.
But here's the thing: those steps are beautiful.
More beautiful, I'd argue, than new marble,
than any surface that hasn't been touched
by the patient friction of living.

Mrs. Chen smiled at me this morning,
and I could see in that smile
all the stairs she's climbed,
all the thresholds she's crossed,
the weight of every arrival and departure

pressed into the architecture of her face.
And I thought, this is what we're doing,
all of us, wearing each other down
and being worn down in turn,
making hollows where the light can pool.

Does Two Plus Two Equal Four?
Created on October 18th, 2025
My teacher says that two plus two
Will always equal four,
But I say that's ridiculous—
It could be three, or more!

For instance, if you've got two cats
And then you get two more,
You might end up with five or six
(If someone's keeping score).

Because those cats might have some kittens
Hiding in a drawer,
So two plus two cats doesn't equal
Four cats anymore!

And if you've got two scoops of ice cream,
Then you get two more,
By the time you eat them all
There's zero on your floor!

My teacher frowned and shook her head
And pointed to the door,
But I still say that two plus two
Might not be always four.

The Famous Cliff
Created on October 18th, 2025
So you've driven all this way, what, three hours?
Four? The GPS kept losing signal
in the mountains and you had to guess
which turnoff, which dirt road rutted
with last winter's damage. You're here now.

The famous cliff. The one everyone talks about
like it's some kind of pilgrimage site,
some destination for the truly done.

And it's beautiful, you have to admit.

The ocean down there working its shift,
all that indifferent crashing and foam,
salt stinging your lips like regret,
the rocks like broken teeth, the gulls
wheeling and screaming their gull screams—
let's be honest—like rusty hinges,
but nothing like transcendence.

You expected a sign. A guardrail.
Something official saying "Don't," or "Do Not,"
or "Here Lies the Body of Common Sense."
But there's just dirt and scrub grass and the edge,
that clean line where the earth decides
it's had enough of being solid.

You could do it. That's the thing.
You could actually do it. No one's here
to stop you, no one's going to rush over
with a blanket and a hotline number.
It's just you and the physics of it,
gravity's old math.

But here's what's weird: you're hungry.
Your stomach is actually growling,
which seems like a betrayal, like your body
hasn't gotten the memo about despair.
And you're thinking about that taco truck
you passed twenty miles back,
its hand‑painted sign flickering under bulbs,
steam rising from the grill like a promise,
and whether they're still open,
and whether the al pastor is any good.

You're thinking: I drove all this way.
You're thinking: I should at least see it through.
But you're also thinking:
Tacos.
I need to pee.
My phone's almost dead.

What if I just stood here a while,
what if I just looked at it, this edge,
this drop into nothing,
and then turned around?

Would that make me a coward or a survivor?
Would that make me someone who chose
the taco truck over the abyss,
the mundane over the mythic,
the stupid animal's need to eat
over the grand gesture?

The wind picks up. It's cold.
It whips your hair, chills your bones—whispers:
Go back.
You're wearing the wrong jacket.
You didn't plan for this—the weather of your ending:
your nose running, your hands shaking,
your teeth chattering,
not from fear but from being human,
cold, alive.

You take a step back. Then another.
The cliff doesn't care. The ocean
keeps erasing itself.

And you—you're already thinking
about the drive back, about whether
you can find that taco truck in the dark,
about how you'll tell this story later,
or maybe you won't tell it at all.

Maybe you'll just keep it, this moment
when you stood at the rim of the world
and chose, for no good reason, to stay.

The GPS beeps low battery as you turn,
the gulls creak overhead.

I stayed. The ocean kept erasing itself.
That was my choice.

The Currency of Affection
Created on October 18th, 2025
I have been noticing how often we say it—
love you—the way we used to say so long
or see you later, a little verbal wave
as we back out of conversations,
our hands already reaching for our keys.

It's become a kind of punctuation mark,
a way to end the sentence of an interaction,
and I wonder if we've turned affection
into small change, something we keep
in our pockets to hand out freely,
the way people used to carry mints or tissues.

Not that I'm complaining, exactly.
There's something democratic about it,
this scattering of tenderness like birdseed,
this assumption that everyone deserves
at least the sound of being cherished,
even if it's just the sound.

But sometimes I think about the word love
and how it used to be a heavier thing,
something you'd carry carefully in both hands
like your grandmother's china or a sleeping child,
not something you'd toss casually over your shoulder
while walking away.

My friend tells me the ancient Greeks
had four different words for varieties of affection—
eros and philia and storge and agape—
which seems excessive until you realize
we're trying to use one word to mean everything
from I would die for you to thanks for holding the door.

Maybe that's the problem, or maybe it isn't a problem at all.
Maybe the word is like a dollar bill
that's been folded and unfolded so many times
it's soft as cloth, passed from hand to hand
until no one remembers whose pocket it started in,
but it still buys something, doesn't it?

Which brings me back to the question
of whether we mean it, and I think
maybe meaning isn't the right word.
Maybe it's more like humming—
you do it without thinking, but it still
makes a kind of music, still fills
the silence with something warmer than silence.

So when I say it tonight to my wife
I'll mean it the way I mean my own breathing,
the way the heart means its beating—
not as a declaration but as a fact,
not as poetry but as the plain truth
of being alive in the same house,
the same moment, the same brief
and unrepeatable Tuesday evening
when the light is fading and the dog
is sleeping and we're all still here,
still finding our way through this strange life
with nothing but these small, worn words
to guide us home.
What She Becomes
Created on October 17th, 2025
I've been thinking about the zombie bride
who got married last Halloween night,
how she stood at the altar, dignified,
in her dress with its lace sewn tight.

Not undead wife, which sounds like a complaint
you'd file with customer service somewhere.
Not zombie spouse, though the term is quaint—
the wedding announcement didn't go there.

The certificate doesn't have a box to check
for previously deceased, I'd bet.
No line that asks about the wreck
of dying, or how long since death you met.

I like to think she's simply married now,
that love doesn't care about your pulse,
doesn't ask questions about the how
or measure the warmth that results.

She's what we all become in the end—
someone trying to hold ceremony near,
someone who believes a ring can mend
and transform us into something dear.

The groom adjusts his tie with nervous care.
She holds her plastic bouquet tight.
Two people standing there, a pair
who still believe in Halloween's light.

The Blind Date
Created on October 17th, 2025
I have been thinking about the blind date,
not the kind where two strangers meet
in a coffee shop with sweaty palms and rehearsed anecdotes,
but the other kind—the fruit that cannot see.

Picture it there in the bowl on your kitchen counter,
that wrinkled brown oval among the bright oranges
and the self-satisfied apples,
unable to admire its own sweetness,
oblivious to the sunlight streaming through your window.

It has traveled so far—from some palm tree
in California or Tunisia, dried under a sun
it could never witness, boxed and shipped
in darkness it couldn't distinguish from light.

And now it sits there, sightless,
which is probably for the best
because if it could see, it might notice
how often you reach past it for the banana,
how you've forgotten it's even there
among the seeing fruits, the ones that seem
to catch your eye as you rush past.

But here's what I admire about the blind date:
it doesn't need to see you to be sweet.
It doesn't require your recognition
to contain all that concentrated sugar,
that dense, dark flesh that tastes
like honey's older, wiser cousin.

The blind date asks for nothing—
no eye contact, no second glance—
just patient faith that eventually
you'll remember it's there, waiting,
and when you finally reach for it,
you'll discover it knew all along
exactly what it had to offer.

The Falling
Created on October 17th, 2025
Have you seen them—
the starfish in the tide pools,
their five arms reaching
like small suns pressed against the rocks?

I used to think they were only creatures of the sea,
born there, living their whole lives
in the cold grip of the Pacific.
But last night I stood on the beach
watching the sky, and one star—
I swear this is true—
loosened itself from Cassiopeia
and fell, not burning, not streaking
across the darkness like the meteors do,
but drifting, the way a leaf falls
through October air, or the way
a tired bird finally descends.

It fell into the water without a sound.

This morning I went back to the tide pools
and there were more of them,
the starfish, dozens clinging to the stones,
their bodies the exact color of rust,
of old light, of something that has traveled
a long way to arrive here.

I knelt down. I put my hand
into the cold water and touched one.
It didn't move. It held on
to its rock with the patience
of something that has learned
a new way to shine.

Tell me, what do you think happens
to light when it grows weary?
Where do you think the burning goes
when a star decides it has illuminated enough
of the emptiness?

I think it comes here.
I think it falls into the ocean
and learns to crawl, to cling,
to eat the small mussels,
to live without fire.

I think the sky is full of tired light
and the sea is full of fallen stars
and we, walking along the shore,
are walking through a graveyard
and a nursery at once—

all those extinguished suns
finding their way to a second life,
smaller, slower, closer to the earth,
but still here, still holding on,
still reaching their arms out
into the world.

The Otter Day
Created on October 17th, 2025
All morning the otter has been swimming
in the cold creek, diving and rising,
shaking the water from its whiskers,
being exactly what it is—
nothing more, nothing less.

I have been standing here on the bank
watching, my hands in my pockets,
my breath making small clouds in the autumn air,
and I keep thinking: this is enough.
This is what I came for.

The otter doesn't know I'm here.
It doesn't perform or hesitate.
It simply moves through the current,
its body a dark arrow,
its purpose clear as the water itself.

How long have I been trying
to be something other than what I am?
How many years spent apologizing
for the shape of my life,
the quiet way I move through the world?

But the otter—
the otter has no such concerns.
It catches a fish, eats it on a rock,
slides back into the stream,
and the morning continues, blessed and simple.

You do not have to be remarkable.
You do not have to save anyone or anything.
You only have to let your body
do what it was made to do—
to swim, to breathe, to be present in the cold water.

The sun is climbing now above the trees.
The otter dives again, surfaces again,
its fur sleek and shining.
Somewhere a bird is calling.
Somewhere the world is asking nothing of us.

Tell me, isn't this enough—
to stand here watching, to feel
the morning air on your face,
to know that you, too, are swimming
in the one wild current of your life?

The Correspondence
Created on October 16th, 2025
Every day someone is born
and every day someone dies,
and somewhere between those two events
a star collapses into itself, or flares,
or simply goes dark.

I don't know if there is a correspondence—
if your grandmother's last breath
coincided with the dimming of some distant light,
or if the baby crying in the hospital
arrived as a new sun ignited.

But I like to think of it this way:
that we are all burning,
each of us a small fire
fed by the same elements that feed the stars—
hydrogen, oxygen, the will to shine.

And when we go out, we go out
the way stars do—not into nothing,
but into something else, our light
traveling outward, still moving
through the dark.

This morning I watched a sparrow
land on the feeder, then fly away,
and I thought: this is what we do,
we arrive, we eat, we leave,
and the world goes on without us.

But also with us—because the light
we made is still traveling, still
reaching toward whatever comes next,
still saying: I was here,
I burned, I belonged to the family of things.

What is Thoughtful?
Created on October 16th, 2025
I saw a heron this morning standing
in the shallows, so still
you might have mistaken it for a stick,
or a shadow, or nothing at all.

But it was thinking—I'm sure of it—
the way it held its body,
the way its eye followed the small movements
beneath the surface of the water.

Is this not thoughtfulness: to wait,
to watch, to let the world
reveal itself in its own time?
Not to rush, not to demand?

The grasses along the shore were bending
in the wind, each blade
leaning into the morning light,
and I thought: they are considering something too.

Perhaps thoughtfulness is not what happens
in the mind alone, but what the body does
when it pays attention—
the way you stop on the path

to let a beetle cross, the way you notice
the particular blue of today's sky,
different from yesterday's,
the way you hold a question

without needing to answer it right away.
Tell me, when did you last stand still
long enough to hear your own breathing,
to feel the pulse in your wrist,

to remember you are alive,
here, now, in this moment
that will not come again?
What else could matter more than this?

The Meeting
Created on October 13th, 2025
I remember the coffee was terrible that morning,
the kind that tastes like it's been sitting
on the burner since dawn, which it probably had.
Someone made a joke about it—I forget who—
and we all laughed, the way you do
when you're waiting for something important to begin.

There were eight of us around the table.
I sat near the window where I could see
a gardener trimming the hedges outside,
his shears making that soft clicking sound
that seemed louder than it should have been.

The President spoke about acceptable losses,
a phrase I'd heard before but never really
considered the way you might consider
a piece of furniture you walk past every day
until someone asks you to describe it.

Acceptable. Loss. Two words
that don't belong together, like saying
"the friendly fire" or "the necessary evil."
But there they were, side by side on the page,
and someone was reading them aloud
in a voice that suggested they made perfect sense.

I thought about my daughter that morning—
she would have been four—asking me at breakfast
why the sky was blue, and me trying to explain
something about wavelengths and atmosphere,
knowing even as I spoke that the real answer
was simply "because it is."

There was a vote. I don't remember
if I raised my hand or just nodded,
but I remember the gardener outside
had moved to a different hedge by then,
still making that soft clicking sound,
still doing the work he came to do.

Years later, I would read about the shadows
burned into walls, the permanent silhouettes
of people going about their morning routines,
and I would think about that meeting,
the terrible coffee, the clicking shears,
and how we all went home for dinner that night
as if we'd done nothing more remarkable
than approve the minutes from the previous meeting.

I still don't know if there was another way.
But I know that somewhere in Hiroshima,
while we were deciding, a mother was probably
making breakfast, a gardener was probably
trimming hedges, and someone was probably
telling a joke that would make everyone laugh.

I know this doesn't make it better.
I'm just telling you what I remember.

Listen
Created on October 12th, 2025
Have you seen them, the ancient ones,
circling each other across the torn sky,
their wings and hooves writing stories
in the language of thunder and light?

For a thousand years they have been
dancing this way—the griffin's talons
carving new valleys, the unicorn's horn
splitting mountains like ripe fruit.

Watch how they meet above the meadow,
how their fury shakes the earth
until flowers forget their names
and rivers run backwards from the sea.

The old woman in the village says
they fight over a jewel of pure starlight,
but I have been watching, listening
to the spaces between their roars.

See how the griffin's claws never quite
find their mark, how the unicorn's charge
always falls just short of the heart—
this is not war, but conversation.

They are teaching the world how to change,
how mountains must sometimes break open
to let new springs flow, how the sky
needs tearing to let the light through.

What do you call two immortal beings
who have forgotten how to die,
who make their own purpose
from the raw clay of endless time?

They are the world's memory of wildness,
the reason children still believe
in magic, the proof that some things
are too beautiful to be tamed.

Tonight they rest on opposite peaks,
calling to each other in voices
that sound like wind through ancient trees,
like the first word ever spoken.

Tell me—when you have lived forever,
when you have seen every sunrise,
what else is there to do but love
so fiercely it reshapes the world?

Didn't I Love You Before?
Created on October 11th, 2025
Didn't I love you before—
when you were stumbling through your own life
like a young deer through brambles,
uncertain where to place each hoof?

I think I did.
I think I loved you when you didn't know
the names of things yet,
when your hands were clumsy
and your words came out all wrong.

Look—the sparrow outside my window
is learning to crack open
sunflower seeds. She drops more
than she eats. She tries again.
Does the morning love her any less?

You were never perfect.
Neither was I.
We were like two streams
moving through the same valley,
sometimes clear, sometimes
muddied by spring rains,
sometimes barely flowing at all.

But didn't we keep moving?
Didn't we keep trying to find our way
back to each other, to ourselves,
to that place where the water runs clean?

Tell me—what is love
if not this: the willow bending
in the storm but not breaking,
the grass flattened by snow
rising again in April,
the body returning, always returning,
to what it knows by heart?

I loved you before you were whole.
I love you now, still becoming.
The world doesn't ask the oak
to be finished growing.
Why should we ask it of each other?

Jack's Progress
Created on October 10th, 2025
The other day I was standing in the supermarket
among the pumpkins piled like orange cannonballs,
wondering who Jack was anyway,
the one with the lantern we've been hearing about
since we were small enough to believe
that vegetables could protect us from ghosts.

Turns out he was Irish, or the idea was,
though back then they used turnips—
those hard, bitter roots you'd need an hour
and a strong arm to hollow out,
just to wedge a coal inside and call it light.

I suppose we should admire the commitment,
carving a face into something that doesn't want one,
all to ward off spirits who probably couldn't care less
about your agricultural efforts,
but I keep thinking about the first person
who held up a turnip to the darkness and said,
This will do, this will be enough.

Then we got involved, naturally,
and found the pumpkin waiting here like a gift,
soft-fleshed and cooperative,
practically begging to be scooped clean
and given a grin the width of a dinner plate.

Now you see them everywhere in October—
on porches, in windows, lined up at school entrances
with their identical triangular eyes,
their LED candles that never burn out or down,
safer, someone decided, than actual flame,
as if we'd forgotten that the whole point
was a little danger, a flicker that might fail.

I wonder what old Jack would think,
his name attached to this bright parade
of vegetables smiling in the suburbs,
their battery packs tucked in like secrets.

Probably he'd shrug and light another turnip,
one the size of his fist, the flame
no bigger than a thumbnail, and walk off
into the kind of dark we've learned to keep at bay
with twenty watts and a timer,
still believing, as he did, that a carved face
means something when you can't see
what's watching from the other side.

Where Socks Go to Die
Created on October 9th, 2025
Every few months, I open the drawer,
expecting a reunion, a neat parade of socks,
but there’s always one missing,
slipped through some cosmic laundry chute.
I imagine it wandering, a cotton ghost,
haunting the dryer's lint trap or the space
behind the washing machine,
where dreams of pairing go to die.

I’ve searched the usual places—
under the bed, where dust bunnies
plot their quiet rebellions,
or tangled in the sheets,
like a lover who left without a note.
But socks don’t just vanish;
they choose exile, don’t they?
Fed up with being stretched over heels,
tired of the spin cycle’s endless waltz.

Perhaps they're transitioning into sock monkeys,
or maybe they’ve found a secret sock Valhalla,
a place where single socks gather,
no longer mismatched, no longer judged,
sipping coffee in some parallel universe
where washing machines hum lullabies
and no one folds them into submission.
I picture them laughing,
their threads unraveling in joy.

And yet, I keep the odd ones,
those widowed argyles and plain whites,
stacked in the corner of the drawer,
a monument to hope or laziness.
Each time I do laundry, I half-expect
one to limp back, sheepish,
muttering about a wild night in the hamper.
But they never do. They’re gone for good,
leaving me to wonder if I, too,
might one day slip away
to wherever socks go to die.
Author's Notes: Image created by me using photoshop and generative AI
The Fly on the Wall
Created on October 9th, 2025
I noticed a fly on the wall this morning,
a speck of black jitter buzzing faintly,
parked above the bookshelf, near a lamp
that’s been flickering like a tired eyelid.
It’s just there, legs thin as whispers,
watching me shuffle through papers and dust.

What’s it doing, this tiny critic?
Taking notes on my coffee-stained mug,
my habit of talking to the radio,
as if it’s got opinions on the news?
I smirk at the thought—me, a spectacle,
my life a stage for this insect audience.

But what if it’s not just a fly?
What if it’s a philosopher in disguise,
pondering the weight of my small routines—
the way I leave crumbs on the counter,
or how the clock’s tick outpaces my plans?
Maybe it sees what I miss: the slow fade
of light through the window, the room’s quiet hum.

I could flick it away, end its vigil,
but there’s something in its stubborn perch,
like a poet who lingers too long at a desk,
searching for meaning in a spill of ink.
It’s almost noble, this fly’s patient gaze,
making me wonder if I’m the one
who’s been buzzing in circles all along.

When the Moon Whispers to the Tide
Created on October 8th, 2025
The sun dissolves, its honeyed embers spent,
As moonlight stitches silver through the dusk.
You stand where shadows weave their fraying tent,
Where day and night entwine in fleeting trust. 

The moon ascends, a lantern carved from frost,
While waves unfurl their scrolls of salted lore.
Does she beckon with a voice half-lost,
Or do they climb to kiss her glowing core? 

The ocean hums a hymn of shattered glass,
Each crest a fleeting prayer to starlit skies.
What secrets thread through currents’ ceaseless dance,
That you might catch if you could read their sighs? 

Perhaps she spins her tales of cosmic drift,
Of orbiting through voids where dreams decay.
Perhaps the tide murmurs its restless gift—
A love that carves its name in spray. 

The foam, a lace of whispered promises,
Curls back, then surges with a lover’s plea.
Each wave a parchment etched with lunar kisses,
Rewritten nightly by the restless sea. 

You trace the moon’s pale ribbon, spun and frail,
A trembling bridge where light and water weave.
Their dialogue unfolds—a ghostly veil,
Where truth hums soft in riddles shells believe. 

What binds the tide’s wild pulse to lunar flame?
What vows are sworn where crest and glow align?
They whisper while we dream, their ancient game,
In tongues of tide that spark the shore’s design.

The Mirror at Seven A.M.
Created on October 8th, 2025
I used to greet the bathroom mirror
with something like indifference,
the way you'd nod at a neighbor
whose name you can never quite recall.

But lately it has become an oracle,
this silvered rectangle above the sink,
offering prophecies I never requested—
news of a crease near the mouth,
a strand of hair staging its silver rebellion,
the slight sag where my jawline
used to make a cleaner exit.

The mirror, it turns out, is a terrible liar.
It refuses to remember me
the way I remember myself,
that version from—what was it—
eight years ago? Ten?

Sometimes I catch my reflection mid-shave
and think I see my father
peering back through the foam,
as if he's been hiding in there all along,
waiting for the right moment
to wave hello through my own face.

This morning I tried squinting,
that old trick of blurring the details,
but the mirror adjusted for the light,
sharpened its focus like a camera
determined to capture the truth.

So I've decided to lower my expectations.
The mirror is just doing its job,
reporting the daily news without commentary,
a faithful journalist covering the slow,
unremarkable story of a face
learning to look like someone else—
someone I'll probably grow to recognize
if I stand here long enough,
patient as the glass itself,
which has been practicing this same reflection
every morning, without complaint,
for years.

The Bruised Bird
Created on October 8th, 2025
A bird with a bent wing flops into my kitchen,
feathers smudged like a chalkboard erased wrong.
I thought it was a clock at first,
ticking backward on the counter,
its beak a needle stitching air to nowhere.
Hope’s a reckless kite, caught in a tree’s teeth.

The window yawns, but the bird just stares,
as if gravity’s a joke told badly.
I offer it a crumb, and it sings—
not a song, but a car alarm in a dream.
The bruise blooms purple, a tiny galaxy
spinning on its chest, whispering fly anyway.

Then I realize I’ve lost the sky’s address,
left it scrawled on a napkin in a diner.
The bird hops onto a spoon,
declaring itself captain of a sinking ship.
Fate’s gate swings, but we don’t pass through—
we dance, clumsy, on a floor of cracked plates.

A lightbulb hums, pretending to be the moon.
The bird’s eye glints, a coin flipped for luck.
Loss lingers like ash in a windless room,
yet this bruised thing flaps, wild, toward a crack
in the wall where chaos kisses chance.
I laugh, tender as a bruise, and let it go.

The Unread Postcard
Created on October 8th, 2025
You find it in a drawer,
a postcard from some beach town,
its edges curled like a smile
you forgot to return.

What if it’s been waiting there,
its palm trees and sunset
mocking your oversight,
a message from someone
who thought you’d care enough to read?

You flip it over,
the handwriting faded,
like a whisper from a radio
left on in another room,
and there’s a soft ache in the absence,
tempered by a grin at your own neglect.

The twist comes softly—
you slip it back into the drawer,
not because you don’t care,
but because some gaps
are funnier left alone,
like a joke you laugh at
without knowing the punchline.

Should I Be
Created on October 8th, 2025
You mutter “thank you” as the coffee slides across the counter,
its warmth seeping through the cup
like a secret you didn’t ask for.
“No worries,” she says,
her words light as a feather
brushing the morning air,
and you’re left standing there,
wondering if you should be—
worried, that is.

The room hums with the clink of spoons,
the hiss of the espresso machine,
and you think, what if she’s right?
What if the world’s just a page
in a book you forgot to finish,
its plot unraveling in the steam
rising from your coffee?
A mouse of doubt scurries
through the labyrinth of your thoughts,
nibbling at the edges of calm.

You sip, and the bitterness stings,
a playful jab from the universe,
as if to say, “Relax, but not too much.”
She’s already wiping down the counter,
her apron a flag of nonchalance,
while you sit by the window,
watching the light bend through the glass,
wondering if worry is just a lanyard
you wove to tether yourself
to a morning that doesn’t care.

And here’s the catch—
what if “no worries” is a riddle,
not a promise?
What if you’re the one
writing this story in your head,
chuckling at the thought
that a simple cup of coffee
might be the only thing
keeping you from asking
if you should be worried
about not being worried at all?

Eye See You, I Spy
Created on October 7th, 2025
"Eye see you!" roared the Old Man’s voice,
His squint was sharp, he had no choice.
"I spy!" the mouse squeaked, bold and spry,
His tiny eyes lit up the sky.

The Old Man glared through dusty glass,
Saw crumbs and coins in blades of grass.
"I spot your tricks, your cheesy schemes!"
He bragged, his face all grins and gleams.

The mouse, he danced on tabletop,
"I spy your socks, your mop, your slop!
My peepers catch each nick and dime!"
He puffed his chest, his pride sublime.

They boasted loud, their eyes like hawks,
Saw rash and gash, old clocks and socks.
"I’m king of sight!" each claimed with glee,
Till both got lost in what they’d see.

The Doe at Dusk
Created on October 7th, 2025
You’re walking the dog, leash slack in your hand,
when you see her, a doe, stepping out from the pines,
her fawn trailing like a shy footnote.
The light is doing that thing it does in late summer,
smearing gold across the field,
as if the sun forgot its lines and just gave up.

She doesn’t bolt, this doe, though her ears twitch,
catching the hum of your dog’s low growl,
the kind he saves for squirrels and shadows.
Her eyes, dark as the bottom of a well,
hold you there, pinned to the grass,
while the fawn nibbles a clover patch, oblivious.

It’s not like you expected this, you know,
this quiet scene unfolding like a page
from some book you meant to read but didn’t.
The air smells of cut hay and something older,
maybe the earth’s own breath,
stirring up thoughts you forgot you had.

You wonder if she’s forgotten her own fear,
standing there, legs thin as reeds,
while the fawn stumbles, all knees and curiosity.
The dog tugs, eager for the next scent,
but you stay, letting the moment stretch,
a thread of wonder pulled taut against the dusk.

And isn’t it strange, you think,
how the world keeps offering these gifts—
a doe, a fawn, a fading sky—
when all you did was step out the door
with a plastic bag and a leash,
expecting nothing but the usual walk home?

The Apology Chair
Created on October 6th, 2025
There was a chair in the hallway
outside my childhood bedroom—
wooden, uneven, and perfectly designed
for saying you're sorry.
You sat on it after slamming the door
or shouting shut up at your sister,
or for that day I decided
it would be funny
to put my goldfish in the bathtub.

The rule was simple:
say you're sorry,
and mean it—though how exactly
one measures sincerity at age nine
remains a mystery
to both children and the adults
they are slowly turning into.

I remember once
trying to apologize
for knocking over a potted plant
with my plastic lightsaber,
but got distracted midway
by a loose thread in my sock,
and the tiny, sudden idea
that maybe the plant
wanted to fall.

What I'm saying is,
we’re taught so early
to perform regret
like it’s a school play—
you remember your lines,
bow at the end,
and hope no one notices
you forgot the part
where you feel it.

Still, I’ve kept a chair like that
in the back of my mind—
not for punishment
but for pause,
a place to sit
when the days get too loud,
or when I start to believe
that being sorry
is the same as being forgiven.

It isn’t, of course.
But sometimes,
when no one's around,
I sit there anyway
and tell the goldfish
I’m still sorry.

The Backyard Takeover
Created on October 6th, 2025
This morning I noticed the fence leaning,
a tired spine under the weight of years,
its wooden slats splintered, soft as my knees
after too many trips up the attic stairs.
The body, I mean, not the house—though both
seem to sag in the same quiet defeat.

But look at the yard: crabgrass and clover
have staged a coup, their green fists punching
through cracks in the patio’s old concrete.
Ants march in neat rows, claiming the crumbs
of my breakfast left on the wicker chair.
Nature doesn’t care about my stiff joints.

I tried to pull a dandelion once,
thinking I could tame the chaos with a tug.
It snapped, left roots grinning underground,
as if to say, We were here first.
My heart, that old pump, skips a beat or two,
while the weeds write their novel in the dirt.

I sit by the window, counting my aches,
each one a plot point in a story I’d rather
not read. Yet the vines keep climbing the shed,
their leaves a bright defiance against rust.
Maybe that’s the trick—not to fight the green,
but to let it grow, wild, over my bones.

Count Elfula’s Spooky Spin
Created on October 6th, 2025
Count Elfula pops out at dusk,
His cloak’s all swirly, soft as husk.
He skips where chalk-white lanterns glow,
And winks at shadows in a row.

You mustn’t say he can’t go play,
Don’t tell him ghosts should fade away!
He tugs at clouds of crimson mist,
And hugs the moon with every twist.

What’s that? He’s munching candy corn?
His cape got tangled in a thorn!
He rolls down grass where sidewalks end,
And giggles with the midnight wind.

Oh, Elfula, keep spinning free,
Your silly dance makes stars all glee!
On Halloween, your grin’s the best,
It lights the night with goofy zest.

Across the Room
Created on October 6th, 2025
Her eyes catch his across the room,
A spark that lights the trembling air.
She lingers in the shadows gloom,
Too shy to cross, too small to dare.

His laugh, a breeze through open doors,
Stirs dreams she locks in silent doubt.
Her heart, a flicker barred by shores,
Fears glances meet and spark will rout.

The crowd hums low, a restless tide,
Yet he’s a shore she’ll never reach.
Her thoughts, like embers, glow and hide,
Each step a word she cannot speak.

What if his glance should meet her own?
The risk of hurt looms sharp and near.
She stays, a statue carved of stone,
And lets the moment disappear.
The Silent Boundary
Created on October 6th, 2025
Something draws me to the forest's edge,
Where tangled undergrowth meets mown grass,
A line that nature never meant to hold
But humans carve with posts and twisted wire.
The pine trees lean as if to whisper secrets,
Their bark peeled back like pages from a book
Forgotten in the rain, now curling soft.
I pause here often, feeling the divide
That separates the farm from deeper shade,
Where light filters through leaves in dappled patterns,
Awakening a quiet ache within.

Beyond this fence, the woods extend their arms,
Inviting yet forbidding, full of promise
That pulls at buried longings in my chest.
The air grows thick with scents of damp earth,
Moss clinging to the trunks like ancient grief,
And ferns unfurl in slow, deliberate grace.
No path leads straight into that dim realm;
Instead, faint trails diverge among the roots,
Worn by deer or perhaps by my own steps
From times when duty yielded to desire,
A momentary lapse into the wild.

Yet boundaries persist, as if the land
Itself conspires to keep us in our place,
With gaps that tempt but rarely let us through.
The apples on the orchard side hang heavy,
Their skins flushed red with unspent ripeness,
While ice from winter storms still scars the bark
Of pines bent but never fully broken.
I mend these fences yearly, stone by stone,
Though something in the frost heaves them apart,
A force that mocks our efforts at control,
Revealing how thin barriers truly are.

In moments like this, standing at the rim,
The farm behind me hums with daily tasks,
But passion stirs beneath the calm facade,
A restrained fire that burns without a flame.
The night will come to cloak these woods in sleep,
Yet underneath, the roots entwine and reach,
Defying lines we draw upon the ground.
What if I stepped across, just once, to feel
The undergrowth embrace my weary frame,
To sigh with winds that travel unchecked paths?
The difference might be small, or vast as years,
But here I linger, bound by unseen chains.

The snow will fall again, blanketing all,
Erasing edges till the world seems one,
But spring returns to sharpen every gap,
Reminding us of choices left untaken.
This tension holds me, intense yet concealed,
A passion buried deep like hidden streams
That carve their way through soil without a sound.
The woods watch on, indifferent to my gaze,
Their mystery a mirror to my own,
Where farm and forest meet in silent pact,
And I remain, observing from the verge.
The Climb
Created on October 5th, 2025
An old man climbs the building's concrete steps,
His shaking hand grips rails of rusted steel,
Each stride a tax on joints that creak and bend,
Yet he pursues a form that time won't break.

Through stairwell glass he sees a city pond,
Where one pale swan drifts quiet in the dusk,
Its wings too weak to lift it from the pond—
The swan he watched here fifty years ago.

What game is this, that skin must sag and tire,
Yet mind, a spinning flare, would chase the stars?
He looks down at the swan, then past the streets,
And finds what time has failed to take away:
His daughter's face, the theorems he once proved,
Still burn as bright as neon in the dark.

Will he, grown old, reach out for such fixed forms,
Or grin at time's cruel trick, its stubborn trap?
He pauses on the landing, breathing hard,
But climbs another flight toward the roof.

The body fails. The knees give out. The heart
Drums warnings that he's learning to ignore.
Yet in this weathered frame, he weaves a spell—
Each memory a thread that will not fray.

A bird's faint call through city smog breaks free,
One sparrow singing over traffic's roar.
He thinks: this stubborn song against the noise—
It mirrors what persists inside his mind.

The building's height cuts through the urban dusk,
Each step a choice to climb instead of rest,
Each thought a rung to lift above the dark,
Each word a light—his own—that will not dim.

He reaches for the rooftop door at last,
Where sky meets steel, where city glows below,
And stands above the ground in endless night,
Still reaching, still alive, refusing stone.
